Page of 1
JEDEC PROPOSEDSCOPE
PROPOSED
1. Scope
This standard defines the DDR6 specifications, including features, functionalities, AC and DC characteristics, pack-
ages, and ball/signal assignments. The purpose of this standard is to define the minimum set of requirements for 
JEDEC-compliant DDR6 devices.
This document was created based on the LPDDR6 (JESD209-6) and DDR5 (JESD79-5) standards, and some aspects 
of the following standards: DDR (JESD79), DDR2 (JESD79-2), DDR3 (JESD79-3), DDR4 (JESD79-4), and 
LPDDR5 (JESD209-5).
Each aspect of the standard was considered and approved by committee ballot(s). The accumulation of these ballots 
was then incorporated to prepare the DDR6 standard.


=== PDF PAGE 26 ===
 Page 2 of 27
FUNCTIONAL DESCRIPTION
JEDEC PROPOSEDOVERVIEW
PROPOSED
2. Overview
2.1. Functional Description
DDR6 is a high-speed synchronous DRAM device internally configured with pseudo-split die (PSDs), each containing 
3 or 6 double-data-rate (DDR) I/Os (DQs) and a shared differential data strobe (DQS) for both PSDs. The DQs from 
each PSD contribute to forming pseudo-channels. The bank architecture is two (2) banks in 16 bank groups per PSD. 
Further description of PSD organization and pseudo-channel configurations is provided in subsequent sections.
The PSD can be accessed pseudo-independently in the 2p3 and 2p6 configurations or combined in the 1p6 and 1p12 
configurations. The PSD configurations support operational modes such as 1p6, 1p12, 2p3, and 2p6, where "1p" and 
"2p" denote the number of pseudo-channels and the second number denotes the number of DQs per PSD. Configura-
tion details are defined in the Addressing Tables section.
The devices use a shared clock (CK) between both PSDs for command/address inputs that operates at the same rate as 
the shared DQS that is used for data. There are six single-data-rate (SDR) command/address (CA) pins that the 
memory controller uses to transmit command, address, bank, configuration, and training information to the device. CA 
signals are latched on the rising CK edges, with the commands starting on an even rising CK edge when indicated by 
a HIGH signal on the SDR CS pin that is 2N in duration and centered on the even rising CK edge. Commands are 
2nCK, 4nCK and 6nCK in duration. See the Command Truth Table for details.
The DDR I/O signaling is designed to operate with on-die termination on both the memory controller and the device.
Data access size is configuration dependent, with 128 data bit prefetch supported. A single BL24 read or write access 
for a 3 DQ PSD consists of 72-bits (64-bits of data and 8-bits of non-data). A single BL24 read or write access for a 6 
DQ PSD consists of 144-bits (128-bits of data and 16-bits of non-data). Non-data refers to bits that may be stored in 
the array and accessed using address carve-out, or bits that are not stored in the array, such as unstored CRC.
Read and write accesses to the devices are burst oriented. Accesses begin with the registration of an ACTIVATE 
command, followed by a READ or WRITE command.
The address, bank and bank group bits registered coincident with the ACTIVATE command are used to select the row 
and the bank to be accessed. The address, bank and bank group bits registered coincident with the READ or WRITE 
command are used to select the bank and the column location for the burst access.
Prior to normal operation, the device must be initialized. The Power-Up and Initialization section provides detailed 
information covering device initialization.


=== PDF PAGE 27 ===
Page of 27
FUNCTIONAL DESCRIPTION
JEDEC PROPOSEDOVERVIEW
PROPOSED
2.1.1. Pad Definition and Description
Table 1 — Pad Definition and Description  (Sheet 1 of 2)
Symbol
Type
Description
Note
RESET_n
Input
 RESET: Reset is active when RESET_n is LOW, and inactive when 
RESET_n is HIGH. RESET_n must be HIGH during normal operation. 
RESET_n is an asynchronous signal.
 1
CK_t, CK_c
Input
 Clock: CK_t and CK_c are differential clock inputs. The first crossing point 
is the rising (falling) edge of CK_t (CK_c) and second crossing point is 
falling (rising) edge of CK_t (CK_c). Command/Address and CS inputs are 
sampled on the crossing point that is the rising (falling) edge of CK_t 
(CK_c). One CK_t and CK_c pair are shared for both pseudo-split die (PSD0, 
PSD1).
1
CS_0, CS_1
Input
 Chip Select: CS is part of the command code, the pulse width is 2CK and is 
sampled on the even rising (falling) edge of CK_t (CK_c) unless the device is 
in power-down mode where it becomes an asynchronous signal. All 
commands are masked when CS is registered LOW.

CA[5:0]_0, 
CA[5:0]_1

are multi-cycle, the pins may not be interchanged between devices on the 
same bus.

 DQS_t, DQS_c
 Input 
/Output
 Data Strobe: DQS_t and DQS_c are the differential output clock signals 
used to strobe data during a READ and differential input clock signals used to 
latch WRITE data. The differential crossing point is edge-aligned with 
READ data and centered for WRITE data. One DQS_t and DQS_c pair are 
shared for both pseudo-split die (PSD0, PSD1).

Note
RESET_N_[A,B]_xy
Input
 RESET: Reset is active when RESET_n is LOW, and 
inactive when RESET_n is HIGH. RESET_n must be 
HIGH during normal operation. RESET_n is an 
asynchronous signal.
1,4,5,7
CK_t_[A,B]_x, 
CK_c_[A,B]_x
Input
 Clock: CK_t and CK_c are differential clock inputs. 
The first crossing point is the rising (falling) edge of 
CK_t (CK_c) and second crossing point is falling 
(rising) edge of CK_t (CK_c). Command/Address and 
CS inputs are sampled on the crossing point that is the 
rising (falling) edge of CK_t (CK_c). One CK_t and 
CK_c pair are shared for both pseudo-split die (PSD0, 
PSD1).
1,4,7
CS[3:0]_A0_xy, 
CS[3:0]_A1_xy, 
CS[3:0]_B0_xy, 
CS[3:0]_B1_xy
Input
 Chip Select: CS is part of the command dcode, is two 
UI the pulse width is 2CK and is sampled on the even 
rising (falling) edge of CK_t (CK_c) unless the device 
is in power-down mode where it becomes an 
asynchronous signal. All commands are masked when 
CS is registered LOW. Chip Select number signifies 
the Rank with a min of 1 and a max of 4. 
Unused/unavailable ranks are NC.

CA [5:0] _A0_xy, CA[5:0] 
_A1_xy, CA[5:0]_B0_xy, 
CA[5:0]_B1_xy
Input
 Command/Address Inputs: CA signals provide the 
command and address inputs according to the 
Command Truth Table. Note: Since some commands 
are multi-cycle, the pins may not be interchanged 
between devices on the same bus. CA5 (6th CA) is an 
optional pin which provides Chip ID (CID) address 
inputs. If unused, this is either CS3 or NC depending 
on package configuration.
1,3,4,6,7
DQ[5:0]_A0_xy, 
DQ[5:0]_A1_xy, 
DQ[5:0]_B0_xy, 
DQ[5:0]_B1_xy
Input / Output
 Data Input/Output: Bi-directional data bus.
1,3,4,7
DQS0_[t,c]_[A,B]_x, 
DQS1_[t,c]_[A,B]_x
Input / Output
 Data Strobe: DQS_t and DQS_c are the differential 
output clock signals used to strobe data during a READ 
and differential input clock signals used to latch 
WRITE data. The differential crossing point is 
edge-aligned with READ data and centered for WRITE 
data. One DQS_t and DQS_c pair are shared for both 
pseudo-split die (PSD0, PSD1). DQS1 is NC for 2p6 
and 1p12 package configurations.
1,4,7
ALERT_A0_xy, 
ALERT_A1_xy, 
ALERT_B0_xy, 
ALERT_B1_xy
Output
 ALERT: If an error is detected and reported to the 
fault register, the ALERT encodes the type of error on 
the pin. See the Alert section for details. Each PSD has 
an Alert. Alert A1 and B1 will be NC for 1p12 
configurations.
1,4,7
ZQ_[A,B]_x
Reference
 ZQ: ZQ is used to calibrate the output drive strength 
and the termination resistance as calibration reference. 
There is one ZQ pad per die.
2,4,7
VDD, VDDQ, VPP
Supply
 Power supplies: Isolated on the die for improved 
noise immunity. Includes the main power, DQ power 
and row activation power.
VSS
Ground
 Ground Reference: Power supply ground reference.
RFU
Input / Output
 Reserved for future use.
NC
 No Connect: No internal electrical connection is 
present.
NA
Not Active: Internal package connection, not 
used/needed. Only applicable to block diagram die 
connection.

2.1.2.2. Package Connectivity Swizzle Rules
In order to keep package connectivity optimized for signal integrity, there may be cases where die to package connec-
tivity may need to change. The following are acceptable signal swaps:
1)
DQ[5:3] within the same PSD.
2)
DQ[2:0] within the same PSD.
NOTE 1: This pin is referenced to VSS.
NOTE 2: In a multi-die package stack, one die will arbitrate for all dice.
NOTE 3: Pseudo-Split Die 0 (PSD0) and Pseudo-Split Die 1 (PSD1) have independent pads.
NOTE 4: Suffix A [1:0] and B [1:0] signify DRAM package IO Signal Group, X24 has two IO groups, X12 only 
uses Group A. Each group is split into two sub-groups [1:0].
NOTE 5: System designers must connect both RESET_N_[A,B] to a common net on PCB hardware for both X12 
and X24 packages.
NOTE 6: A1 and B1 nets are NC for 1p12 configuration. See package block diagram for details.
NOTE 7: xy suffix illustrates package BGA to die mapping. x=die/dice and y = PSD within the die/dice. Suffix is 
not shown in combination BGA map. See block diagram(s) for further details.

3)
Full PSD[1:0] which includes all associated signals (i.e. DQ, CA, CS, Alert).

2.1.3. Base Die Buffering (BDB) DRAM Package
2.1.3.1. Introduction
This section defines the features and functional operations of Base Die Buffering (BDB) DRAM package. DDR6 can 
support BDB DRAM which is desinged for high-density multi-die stacked package, facilitating reduced load on CK, 
CS, CA, and DQ/DQS signals for improved singal integrity as well as lower power relative to unbuffered DRAM 
package.
In multi-rank package, unbuffered DRAM has multiple indipendent DRAM dies wire bonded together, increasing 
electrical loading on the system channels. The BDB DRAM technology mitigates this with the “Base DRAM Die” 
(hereinafter “Base Die”) being a single electrical load, exclusively interfacing the memory controller for the stacked 
DRAM package no matter if the stack height is 2, 4, or 8. One or more vertically stacked “Core DRAM Dies” (here-
inafter “Core Dies”) are directly connected to the Base Die through die-to-die interconnects within the package. This 
topology isolates external IO between the host and the base die and internal IO between the base die and core dies, 
improving memory subsystem SI and saving power from Core Dies by turning off redundant IO circuit blocks mainly 
built for external communications.
MRxx[y]=1 indicates that the DRAM is BDB.
Package implementation details are not in scope of this standard.
2.1.3.2. Fuctional Description
Addressing and Organizations
The BDB DRAM supports an extra CA pin, CA5, for CID address inputs which are used for determining the targeted 
logical rank that is desired to perform the given commands. Commands that have CID address bits are ACT, RD, WR, 
PRE, REF and RFM. For other commands that do not have CID address bits, the base die is the targeted logical rank 
unless otherwise specified.
Targeted logical rank is selected by CID[2:0] and DDR6 can support up to 8 logical ranks. The base die is associated 
with logical rank 0. The number of available logical ranks in package is provided by MRxx[y:z].

The functional behavior of logical rank(s) shall not deviate from that of physical rank(s) of unbuffered DRAM pack-
ages, except when noted in this standard.
Operation
The Base Die serves as the logical host interface for the entire stack. CA inputs are buffered and decoded centrally on 
the base die, which then redriven to the target core die(s) corresponding to the decoded CID.
Timing Parameters
Because of buffering operation in the base die and in package die-to-die interconnects between the base die and core 
dies, additional asynchronous delays are needed. TBDns is added to tAA. Different Logical Rank (DLR) timing 
parameters need to be defined in future ballots.

2.1.4. Core Architecture
The core architecture will be a Pseudo Split Die (PSD) implementation with separate left and right DRAM arrays (each 
array is half of the total DRAM density) with a common IO block containing a shared CK and DQS.
For 2p3 and 2p6 configuration, each half array will have an independent Chip Select (CS) and Command/Address 
(CA) bus. Both half arrays will share a CK and DQS clocking port. The two PSDs cannot transfer data in opposite 
directions simultaneously as they share strobes; however, one PSD may begin to transfer data before the other. It is the 
Host controller’s responsibility to provide proper turnaround times based on the last PSD transferring in one direction 
to the first PSD transferring in the other. One PSD may transfer data without the other transferring any data at all.
For the 1p6 configuration, each die will have a single CS, CA and CK/DQS clocking port per device.
For the 1p12 configuration, each die will have a single CS, CA and CK/DQS clocking port per device.
BL24 is the burst length for all configurations.
Example die architectures for each configuration is shown in the sections below.
NOTE: Within the following die diagrams, the placement of the DRAM array BGs/BA and pads are for illustration only.
The meaning of the abbreviations are as follows:
PSD (Pseudo-Split Die)
Portion of a DRAM die with independent chip select(CS), command/address (CA) bus and DQ bus, but a CK 
port (CK_t/c, DQS_t/c) which is common to both of PSDs
BG
Bank Group

2.2. Speed Grade
2.2.1. Speed Grade
DDR6 SDRAM devices support multiple speed grades defined by their maximum data rate in MT/s (Mega Transfers 
per second). Speed grades represent the maximum data rate capability of the device. Higher speed grade devices can 
be operated at lower data rates with appropriate timing adjustments.
Table 9 — DDR6 Speed Grade

NOTE 2: tCK(max) shall be less than or equal to 416.7ps
NOTE 3: Higher speed grade devices may be operated at any lower supported data rate with timing parameters 
adjusted according to Mode Register settings for the target frequency

DATA PACKET FORMAT
JEDEC PROPOSEDOVERVIEW

2.3. Data Packet Format
The data packet format is based on burst length 24. The entire data packet is divided into 8-bit sub-packets. In the 
following figures, numbered bit locations are data bits. Shaded bit locations depend on device configuration and Mode 
Register settings. In the figures, the green and blue bit locations may be Metadata bits or CRC bits, which is selected 
by Mode Registers upon initialization. (Reference to Metadata section and CRC section.)
Figure 14 — 2p6 and 1p6 Data packet Format
Figure 15 — 2p3 Data packet Format
2.3.1. Metadata Format
DDR6 supports a Metadata feature for Write and Read operations. In this mode, the data and Metadata bits are 
written/read in parallel to/from the array. The Metadata feature supports the DRAM storing 0, 8 or 16 bits of Metadata 
as MD_OFF, MD_8b and MD_16b, respectively, where one of these modes is selected by MRxx:OP[y] at initializa-
tion.
For 2p6 and 1p6 DRAM configurations, Metadata sub-packets are 8 bits. When MD_OFF is selected, both sub-packets 
are non-Metadata (Non MD). Non MD bits are not stored in the DRAM array, but are available for other functions like 
CRC. MD_8b mode enables blue bit locations for Metadata and green bit locations for Non MD, as shown in the tables 
below. MD_16b mode enables both blue and green bit locations for Metadata, also as shown in the tables below.

JEDEC PROPOSEDCLOCKING
PROPOSED
3. Clocking
DDR6 SDRAM's command and address interface operates from a differential Clock pair (CK_t/CK_c). Commands 
and Addresses are registered single data rate (SDR) at every rising edge of CK_t and falling edge of CK_c. CS is 
sampled at every other rising edge of CK_t and the falling edge of CK_c.
DDR6 uses a double data rate (DDR) data interface. The data interface uses two different types of clock pairs for Read 
(CK_t/CK_c) and Write (DQS_t/DQS_c). CK_t and CK_c are used to drive DQ Read, and they are source synchro-
nous to the DQS during Read operation. The DQS is bi-directional. The DQ Write data is registered by DQS at every 
rising edge of DQS_t and rising edge of DQS_c for write operation.
DDR6 SDRAM uses an un-matched DQS-DQ path for Write to enable high speed performance and save power in the 
SDRAM. Therefore, a per-pin training by a host is required to adjust a timing of each DQ signal relative to DQS. This 
will allow a data eye to arrive at a SDRAM DQ receiver latch being centered on the DQS transition.
CA is matched with CK, and CAs skew training shall be used to compensate the increased CA and CK path delay.
Figure 22 — Clocking and Interface Relationship: Read Operation

4. Initialization and Training
4.1. Power-up and Initialize sequence
For power-up and reset initialization, in order to prevent DRAM from functioning improperly, default values for the 
following MR settings need to be defined.
4.1.1. VREFCA, VREFCS and CK/CS/CA ODT control
DDR6 SDRAM supports CKOTP, CSOTP and CAOTP for default operation. Since there are various types of pack-
ages and modules, DDR6 internally have OTP registers for CA, CS and CK for initial state. Default RTT value of 
CA/CS signals, as seen from the package balls, is set to 240Ω by OTP and MR definitions, regardless of the number

=== PDF PAGE 55 ===
Page of 136
POWER-UP AND INITIALIZE SEQUENCE
JEDEC PROPOSEDINITIALIZATION AND TRAINING
PROPOSED
of dies in the package, by enabling CA/CS ODT in a single die. When all dies enable CK ODT in the initial state, the 
effective RTT becomes the default RTT divided by the number of dies.
Since appropriate VREF level and ODT values are affecting each other, VREF and ODT values need to be changed 
simultaneously with single command to avoid abnormal operation. DDR6 SDRAM supports Apply function for 
VREF, and ODT MR for CK, CS, and CA. When MRUPDATE is issued to change VREF or ODT MR, it's stored in 
shadow-registers. When Apply MRUPDATE is issued, values stored in corresponding shadow-registers are trans-
ferred to corresponding normal-registers, and impact on function and MRR operation. Every shadow register's RESET 
default value is same as normal-registers RESET default value.
Changing CK/CS/CAODT or Apply operations toward these registers are only allowed in MRUPD mode. Other than 
MRUPD mode, using MRW command, changing VREFCS/CA is allowed, but it directly impact on normal-registers 
without changing shadow-registers.
In 2PSD configuration, VREFCA, VREFCS, CA ODT, CS ODT normal/shadow registers are PSD Mode Registers, 
while CK ODT normal/shadow registers are Shared Mode Registers. Apply operation is Functional Mode Registers 
which is in Shared Registers. So, in 2p3 or 2p6 configurations, VREFCA, VREFCS, CA ODT and CS ODT registers 
shall be controlled by each of PSD, while CK ODT register can be controlled by either of PSD. Apply operation is 
shared by MRUPDATE command of either of PSD.
Shadow-registers are activated bypassing normal-registers in train state. When train-state in CSTM is entered, CS 
VREF and CS ODT MR's shadow-registers are activated without applying operation. When train-state in CATM is 
entered, CS VREF, CA VREF, CS ODT, CA ODT MR's shadow-registers are activated without applying operation. 
Bypassing operation is disabled when exit from train-state. Any changes on VREFCS and VREFCA in CSTM and 
CATM directly stored in shadow-registers and will be applied to normal-registers when Apply MRUPDATE is issued.
DDR6 SDRAM also supports CA-parity recovery mode, defined on MR[y] OP[a]. When this mode and CA parity 
check modes enabled, VREFCA shadow-register value is automatically applied to VREFCA normal-register when CA 
parity error occurs.
In physical multi-rank configuration, MRUPDATE command for Apply-VREF/ODT command needs to be issued 
simultaneously to all ranks. To accomplish successful transition of VREF and ODT, Apply operation is recommended 
to be issued in MRUPD mode, because broadcasting CS is only allowed in MRUPD mode.
4.1.2. Voltage Ramp and Device Initialization
The following sequence shall be used to power up the DDR6 SDRAM. Unless specified otherwise, these steps are 
mandatory.
1)
While applying power (after Ta), RESET_n is recommended to be LOW (equal or less than 0.2 x VDD) and all 
other inputs shall be between VSS and VDD. The SDRAM outputs remain at High-Z while RESET_n is held

LOW. Power supply voltage ramp requirements are provided in Table 2. VPP must ramp at the same time or

2)
Following the completion of the voltage ramp (Tb), RESET_n shall be maintained LOW for a minimum of 
tINIT1, after which RESET_n can be de-asserted to HIGH(Tc). DQ, DQS_t, DQS_c, voltage levels shall be 
between VSS and VDDQ to avoid latch-up.
3)
At least tINIT2 before RESET_n de-assertion, CS level is required to be VSS to prevent malfunction.
4)
After RESET_n is de-asserted (Tc), CK/CS/CA/DQ and DQS ODT state should be valid following the default 
states.

7)
Between Tg to Th, the following initial configuration modes shall be completed prior to other training modes: 
a)

tDLYCAL (Tg)

is recommended to configure VREF and ODT registers to appropriate level and issue Apply MRUPDATE.
9)

values with input clock frequency, before host start the Read and Write related training sequences.
10)
Only in MRUPD mode can the host change the clock frequency without Full-PD with Frequency change. 
Frequency change sequence shall be done accordance with FSP and switching specification. 
a)
Host sets FSP-WR to 1.
b)
Host sets Frequency MR that matches the 2nd clock frequency.
c)
If needed, host sets CK/CA/CS ODT and CA/CS VREF values for 2nd clock frequency on shadow regis-
ters, and issues CK/CA/CS Apply MRUPDATE to set 2nd FSP registers.
d)
Host issues MRUPDATE to change FSP-OP to 1 and turn off the clock after tCKFC_E. From the rising 
edge of CK_t (at cycle Tb3) until the rising edge of CK_t at the end of tCKFC_X timing (Tg0), only DES 
command is allowed and CS should keep low.
e)
If FSP_OP transition includes changes on VREFCA or VREFCS value, following command (Tg0) after 
the clock frequency change need to be delayed satisfying VREF transition specification.
f)
The period after turning off and before turning on the clock, CK_t and CK_c shall be driven differen-
tially.
g)
The clock with new frequency shall start tCKFC_X before the next MRUPDATE.
h)
The host shall synchronize with DRAM with their internal clock phase by either CPS of CSTM or CK 
Sync Off functional mode followed by CK sync.

4.1.4. DDR6 FSP and switching
The Frequency Set Points (FSP) allow the DDR6 SDRAM CK/CS/CA Bus to be switched between 2 different oper-
ating frequencies, with changes in voltage swings and termination values, without ever being in an untrained state 
which could result in a loss of communication to the SDRAM. This is accomplished by duplicating all CA Bus mode 
register parameters, as well as other mode register parameters commonly changed with operating frequency.
These doubled registers form 2 sets that use the same mode register addresses, with read/write access controlled by 
MR bit FSP-WR (Frequency Set-Point Write/Read) and the DRAM operating point controlled by another MR bit 
FSP-OP (Frequency Set-Point Operation). Changing the FSP-WR bit allows MR parameters to be changed for a 
selected Frequency Set-Point without affecting the DDR6- SDRAM’'s current operation.
Once all necessary parameters have been written to the selected Set-Point, changing the FSP-OP bit will switch oper-
ation to use all of the new parameters simultaneously with Full PDE with frequency change, eliminating the possibility 
of a loss of communication that could be caused by a partial configuration change.
2 mode registers for each of the parameters above can be modified by setting the appropriate FSP-WR value, and how 
device operation can be switched between operating points by setting the appropriate FSP-OP value. The FSP-WR and 
FSP-OP functions operate completely independently.
Unlike normal-registers with FSP, shadow-registers are shared within FSPs. When MRUPDATE is issued to change 
VREF or ODT, it is buffered on shadow-register. When Apply MRUPDATE is issued, FSP-WR value specifies to 
which FSP the shadow value is applied.

4.2. Training
4.2.1. Multi-Cycle CS Mode Enable (MCME) Command
The Multi-Cycle CS Mode Enable (MCME) command is a single-function command to enable MRUPDATE Mode 
with multi-cycle CS (MCCS).
Prior to successfully completing the CS, VREFCS, CA and VREFCA trainings, or any time the status of the CS, CA 
and CK alignment is unknown, the CAs are driven with additional setup and hold beyond the CS assertion. For the 
device to latch the MCME in cases where the alignment between CS, CA and CK may be unknown, the CA inputs 
must reach the proper command state and provide tMC_Setup of setup prior to CS transitioning from LOW to HIGH, 
CS must remain HIGH for tMC_CSPW, and the CA inputs must remain in the proper command state and provide 
tMC_Hold of hold after CS transitions from HIGH to LOW.
While the DRAM may only decode CA[2:0] of the MCME command, all CA inputs are high (H) to ensure proper 
parity.
Following the issuing of the MCME command, tMCME_Delay needs to be met before the first cycle of an MRUP-
DATE begins. No commands are allowed during tMCME_Delay.
The MCME command timing diagram and timing parameters are shown in the figure and table below.

4.2.2. Per DRAM Addressability (PDA)
Per DRAM Addressability (PDA) enables the programming of a selected device on a rank. The PDA applies to MRW 
commands and MRUPDATE Mode, except when entering PDA Enumeration Mode or disabling Mode Register Func-
tional Modes (MR05), and when writing to the PDA mode registers. The Direct Addressable PDA Mode Register 
(MR02) contains the Enumeration ID which is common to both PSDs, while the Page Space PDA Mode Register 
MR82 contains the Select ID, which can be unique per PSD.
PDA is a CA interface-only method where a unique Enumeration ID can be programmed in MR02[3:0] and a per PSD 
unique Select ID is programmed in MR82[3:0]. Programming the unique Enumeration ID requires the use of the DQ 
signals and the PDA Enumeration Mode. The Enumeration ID is a 4-bit field, and the Select ID is also a 4-bit field. 
When the Select ID, written by an MRW command or MRUPDATE sequence, matches the Enumeration ID, or when 
the Select ID is set to the “All DRAM” code of 1111b, an MRW command or the MUPDATE sequence will be 
executed.
During the RESET procedure, the Enumeration ID and Select ID values are reset to the default 1111b.
4.2.2.1. PDA Enumeration ID Programming
1)
PDA Enumeration Mode is a Functional Mode within MR05 that can only be entered by the MRUPDATE 
sequence with MR05=06h after entering MRUPDATE Mode.  PDA Enumeration Mode requires DQS to be low 
when entering and exiting the mode.
2)
Upon entering the PDA Enumeration Mode, the DRAM will internally reset the DQS rising pulse count to 0 to 
prepare for counting 4 rising pulse edges (DQSR1 - DQSR4)
3)
As shown in the timing diagram below, the second DQS rising edge (DQSR2) and the fourth DQS rising edge 
(DQSR4) are used to sample the data on DQ0 and DQ1

As an example, the following sequence could be used to program unique PDA Select ID fields per device: 
1)
Send an MRW command with MR82[3:0] and the 'Select ID' opcodes with encoding 0000b
2)
Send MRW commands with settings specific to device 0000b. This can be any number of MRWs.
3)
Send an MRW command with MR82[3:0] and the 'Select ID' opcodes with encoding 0001b
4)
Send MRW commands with settings specific to device 0001b. This can be any number of MRWs.
5)
Repeat for any number of devices
6)
Send an MRW command with MR82[3:0] and the 'Select ID' opcodes with encoding 1111b to enable execution 
of all MRW commands and MRUDPATE sequences on all devices

4.2.3. DDR6 ZQ Calibration
Only Background ZQ Calibration mode is supported in DDR6. In Background Calibration mode, calibration of the 
output driver and CA/DQ ODT impedance across process, temperature, and voltage occurs in the background of device 
operation and is designed to eliminate any need for coordination among channels within a single package.
ZQ re-calibration may be required as the DDR6 SDRAM voltage and/or temperature changes due to changes in the 
system environment. ZQ calibration can only be performed when the VDDQ voltage is at or above its nominal value 
of 0.6V within the range allowed by the specification, otherwise the memory controller calibration shall halt the ZQ 
calibration.
Changing CK, CS andCA ODT values and/or DQ, DQS ODT values will not alter the existing recalibration scheme, 
therefore there is no need for immediate recalibration.
4.2.3.1. Calibration During Powerup and Initialization
ZQ calibration is automatically performed by all DDR6 dies in a package during the initialization/powerup sequence 
before Td, as shown in Figure xx (Power-up, Initialization and Power-off Procedure). A ZQCal Latch commandoper-
ation shall be initiated issued to all DDR6 dies that share a common clock, on or after Tg regardless of the state of the 
ZQ Update Flag (ZQUF). See Table 1 for calibration latency and timing.
Asserting ZQ Reset will set the calibration values to their default setting. When ZQ Stop is enabled, the ZQ resource 
is available for use by other devices.
DDR6 SDRAM package will have one or more ZQ initiator die(s) and ZQ target die(s). ZQ Interval MR setting is only 
applicable to ZQ Initiator die (MR7 OP[1]). These settings will be ignored by ZQ Target dies.
After the power up initialization and reset sequences have been completed, ZQUF MR7 OP[0] =0B.
DDR6 packages with more than one ZQ pin may include more than one ZQ Initiator die.
4.2.3.2. Background Calibration
In this mode, pull-down/ODT and pull-up calibration will be performed in the background and kept up to date by the 
DRAM. Re-calibration will be performed by the DDR6 SDRAM within the time interval, tZQINT, specified in MR7 
OP[5:4].
Pull-down/ODT calibration is controlled by each DDR6 die using an external ZQ resistor connected between VDDQ 
and a package ball or pin (ZQ resources). These ZQ resources may be shared among a number of DDR6 dies up to a 
maximum of NZQ. Calibration will be automatically performed as part of power-up/initialization and after RESET 
assertion. Subsequent re-calibration will be kept up to date by the DRAM. Self-arbitration by the DRAM insures that 
up to NZQ dies within a package can share a common external ZQ resistor and avoid conflicts. Noise immunity will 
not be compromised when sharing the external ZQ calibration resistor.
When automatic pull-down/ODT calibration is complete, pull-up/Voh calibration will start automatically. At the 
completion of pull-down/ODT and pull-up/Voh calibration, MR7 OP[0] bit (ZQUF) will be set if the new calibration 
codes do not match the currently latched codes. An MRR of this bit will notify the memory controller that new cali-
bration results are available and that a ZQCal Latch operation (following ZQCal Latch timing constraints) should be 
issued to ensure accurate calibration of Pull-down, ODT and Voh is consistently maintained. The setting of ZQUF is 
unique to each die regardless of configuration or sharing of the ZQ pin(s). Alternatively, the memory controller may 
choose not to monitor ZQUF and periodically initiate a ZQCal Latch operation by setting the appropriate functional 
mode in the MR6 register.


=== PDF PAGE 66 ===
 Page 42 of 136
TRAINING
JEDEC PROPOSEDINITIALIZATION AND TRAINING
PROPOSED
Setting the MR7 OP[3] = ZQ Stop will halt all background calibration activity. Re-setting MR7 OP[3] to zero will 
immediately start a calibration sequence, where all DDR6 dies sharing the ZQ resource will recalibrate in a serial 
fashion.
Figure 28 — Background ZQ Cal timing diagram
Latching ZQ Calibration Results in Background Calibration Mode
Latching ZQ calibration results is accomplished with the MRUPDATE sequence for ZQ Latch during initialization 
and MRW command during runtime as shown in Figure 1. This command loads new calibration results into the 
pull-down/ODT devices and pull-up drivers.
A ZQCal Latch command operation may be issuedinitiated anytime outside of power-down when there are no DQ 
operations pending or in progress. The results from the most-recently completed calibration will always be latched 
with each ZQCal Latch commandmode register writes through MRUPDATE or MRW. If CA/CS ODT is enabled, the 
CS shall maintain a deselect state during tZQLAT to allow CA/CSODT calibration settings to be updated. Operations 
that initiate DQ operations are always dis-allowed during tZQLAT.
The DQ and CA/CSODT calibration values will not be updated until ZQCal Latch commandoperation is performed 
and tZQLAT has been met, with the following exceptions: ZQ calibration will automatically occur for each die on 
power up/initialization and after Reset_n pin assertion. A ZQCal Latch command operation may be issuedinitiated to 
all DDR6 dies with the shared clock, on or after Tg.
When a ZQCal Latch commandoperation is executed the ZQUF bit will be reset to 0 before the expiration of tZQLAT.
Maintaining Accurate Calibration - Background Calibration Mode
To maintain Pull-down/ODT calibration and Voh calibration:
1)
Periodically, based on tZQINT, issue an MRR to MR7 to check the ZQUF OP[0] bit status for each DDR6 die.
2)
If MR06 OP[0]= 00000001B, issue a ZQCal Latch commandfunctional mode register write.
Repeat 1-2 as needed. Alternatively, the memory controller may choose not to monitor ZQUF and periodically issue 
ZQCal Latch commandoperation. In this case the most-recently-completed calibration results will always be latched 
and will be no older than tZQINT.

It may be permissible for the memory controller to ignore ZQUF and not issue ZQCal Latch commandoperation if both 
of the following are true:
1)
CA/CSODT and CK ODT are disabled
2)
The DDR6 SDRAM is in an idle state, or in Self Refresh or Power Down mode.
Re-calibration will still occur in the background to ensure accurate driver/ODT settings are available should they be 
needed. In this case, the memory controller should ensure a ZQCal Latch commandoperation is performed prior to 
resuming data traffic if ZQUF is set.
When the memory controller de-asserts ZQ Stop it shall reset MR7 OP[3] to zero for all dies sharing the ZQ resource 
within 100ns as described in 1.2.1.1. The ZQUF will be updated and results from the recalibration can be latched after 
the appropriate tZQCAL time (tZQCAL4, tZQCAL8 or tZQCALTBD) has been satisfied.
4.2.3.3. ZQ Stop Functionality
ZQ Resistor Sharing in Background Calibration Mode
In Background Calibration mode, a ZQ Stop function is provided to enable other device(s) to share the ZQ resistor. 
This function is enabled by MR7 OP[3] =ZQ Stop. When another device needs to use the ZQ resistor, MR7 OP[3] shall 
be set to 1 for all DDR6 dies that share ZQ resources. This will halt background calibration operations within delay 
time tZQSTOP (see Table 1) for each DDR6 die. Once tZQSTOP has expired for all DDR6 dies that share the ZQ 
resource, other dies may use the resource. When the ZQ resource is no longer needed by the other die(s) the ZQ Stop 
MR bit should be reset to 0 to allow background calibrations to continue normally. When the ZQ Stop MR bit is reset 
to 0, periodic background calibrations will be re-started.
Since ZQ Stop inhibits the DDR6 die from recalibrating, note that changing system conditions while the ZQ Stop MR 
bit is set may cause the DDR6 ZQ calibration accuracy to deviate from specification. To ensure continued accurate 
calibration as discussed in section 1.1.2.2, the ZQ Stop MR bit shall not be set for longer than tZQINT in background 
calibration mode.
Stopping Background Calibration when VDDQ is Powered Off
In Background Calibration mode, before entering Power-down mode, the calibration shall may be halted by setting ZQ 
Stop on initiator die. ZQ Stop may be issued on Target dies asynchronously or simultaneously. When Power-down 
mode is exited, ZQ Stop should be reset to 0 to re-enable background calibration. Resetting of ZQ Stop on the ZQ 
Initiator die will start background calibration(s) immediately.
In Background Calibration mode, after expiration of the appropriate tZQCAL time (tZQCAL2, tZQCAL4, tZQCAL8 
or tZQCALTBD) from resetting of ZQ Stop, the memory controller may check the ZQUF flags or may issue a ZQCal 
Latch commandoperation to each die sharing the ZQ resource. To guarantee recalibration of all dies sharing the ZQ 
resource within tZQCAL, the MR7 OP[3] ZQ Stop bit shall be reset for all Target die(s) sharing the ZQ resource either 
before, or no later than 100ns after, the MR7 OP[3] ZQ Stop bit is reset on the ZQ Initiator die.
.

ZQ Reset
Setting the ZQ Reset MR bit resets the output impedance calibration to a default accuracy of ±30% across process, 
voltage, and temperature. The ZQ Reset command operation is also executed by writing MR7 OP[2] = 1b. The ZQ 
Reset command operation will also reset the ZQ Stop MR7 OP[3] and ZQUF MR7 OP[0] bits to 0. ZQ Reset will not 
change the state of the ZQ Interval MR bits MR7 OP[5:4]. If CA/CS ODTis enabled, the CA bus shall maintain a dese-
lect state during tZQRESET to allow CA/CSODT calibration settings to be updated. Operations that initiate DQ oper-
ations are always disallowed during tZQRESET. The ZQ Reset MR bit shall be reset to 0b by the DRAM after 
tZQRESET. To reset the ODT and output impedance in a multi-die package, the ZQ Reset function shall be issued to 
all dies regardless of ZQ Initiator or ZQ Target designation. The host shall ensure ZQ RESET and ZQ STOP mode 
register bits are not set up simultaneously.
Multi-die Package Considerations
Up to NZQ DDR6 dies within a single package may connect to the same ZQ resistor. ZQ stop function is executed by 
writing MR7 OP[2] =1b and resetting MR7 OP[2] =0b is managed by Initiator die and may be issued to other die 
sharing a ZQ resource asynchronously or simultaneously. ZQCal Latch commands, when required, are necessary for 
each die. When multiple dies share a ZQ resource and a ZQ Initiator die is managing calibration, the MR7 OP[3] ZQ 
Stop bit shall be set to 0B for all ZQ Target die sharing the ZQ resource. No other arbitration considerations are 
required.

Each DDR6 die within a package includes a single ZQ pin and associated ZQ calibration circuitry. Calibration value 
from this circuit will be generated and used according to the following protocol:
•

ZQ External Resistor, Tolerance, and Capacitive Loading
To use the ZQ calibration function, a 120 ohm, ±1% tolerance external resistor shall be connected between the ZQ pin 
and VDDQ. The total capacitive loading on the ZQ pin shall be limited to CZQ_N.

4.2.4. DQ Swizzling Discovery Mode (DSDM)
During DDR6 DRAM initial training, Data (DQ) bits are used to control the DRAM and receive feedback from it. At 
this stage, the host has no knowledge of the mapping between host DQ signals and DRAM DQ pins. The following 
sequence enables the host to discover the DQ mapping before training CA or DQ. The host shall perform mapping 
discovery for DQ [2:0]. Mapping discovery for the remaining DQ bits at this stage, for different configurations (e.g., 
2p6 or 1p6), is optional.
4.2.4.1. DSDM Entry
DQ Swizzle Discovery Mode (DSDM) is entered by issuing an MRUPD sequence that writes OP[x] = 1b to mode 
register MRxx. Upon entering DSDM, the DRAM shall configure all DQ signals as outputs.
4.2.4.2. DSDM Operation
While in DSDM, the host and DRAM shall follow the procedure below to discover the DQ bit mapping: 
1)
The host shall issue an MRUPD sequence to program the DQ Mapping Mode register (MRxy) with OP[x] = 1b, 
as defined in Table 1, to select and enable a single DQ bit.
2)
In response, the DRAM shall drive the selected DQ bit to a static High level while driving all other DQ bits to 
a static Low level. The host shall sample the DQ signals and record the corresponding DQ mapping.
3)
Steps 1 and 2 shall be repeated until the host has discovered the mapping for all DQ bits.
4)
If the host writes an undefined DQ location (OP[6] and OP[7]) in MRxy, the DRAM shall stop driving all DQ.
4.2.4.3. DSDM Exit
To exit DQ Swizzle Discovery Mode (DSDM), the host shall issue an MRUPD sequence to program MRxx with OP[x] 
= 0B. After completing the required DQ mapping discovery and exiting DSDM, the host shall proceed to Chip Select 
Training Mode using the discovered DQ control bit mapping.

4.2.5. Frequency Set Points (FSP)
4.2.5.1. Input Clock Frequency Change
DDR6 comes with Frequency Set Points (FSP) allowing the DDR6 SDRAM to be switched between 2 different oper-
ating frequencies, which can have different termination and Vref values. MRs from these 2 FSPs share the same 
register addresses. Frequency Set Point Write/Read (FSP-WR) controls which group of the 2 registers are being 
accessed. Frequency Set Point Operation (FSP-OP) indicates which of the 2 FSP is applied. Prior to an input clock 
frequency change event, the DRAM is already operating at FSP[x] based on FSP-OP[x], FSP[y] MRs can be updated 
by setting the FSP-WR[y] ahead of frequency change operation. Setting the FSP-OP[y] bit indicates to the DRAM that 
new FSP is going to be applied. Power Down Entry or Self Refresh Power Down Entry command is issued to the 
DRAM. DRAM applies FSP[y] MRs after entering into Power Down or Self Refresh Power Down state. Input clock 
frequency can be updated while the DRAM is in either Power Down or Self Refresh Power Down state. Once the new 
input clock frequency is stable, Power Down exit or Self Refresh Power Down Exit command is issued to the DRAM.
Figure 30 — Frequency Set Points (FSP) State Diagram
DDR6 SDRAM supports input clock frequency change under the following steps: 
1)
MRs of the FSP[y] used for the next frequency already contain the correct values used for the that frequency
2)
FSP-OP[y] used for the next frequency is set indicating that new FSP[y] is going to be applied
3)
Power Down Entry or Self Refresh Power Down Entry command is issued to the DRAM
4)
DRAM applies FSP[y] MRs after entering to Power Down or Self Refresh Power Down
5)
Input clock frequency can be updated

4.2.5.2. Frequency MR
Host shall inform the DRAM of operating clock frequency via Frequency MR. At initialize sequence, Frequency MR 
shall be set by MRUPDATE command to the value matching to the Clock frequency on MR09[3:0]. After the first 
Frequency MR is set, host shall sustain clock input without changing the frequency at least tDLYCAL.
Only in MRUPD mode can the Frequency MR of the 2nd FSP be set using FSP-WR on MR00[2]. The 2nd FSP 
Frequency MR can be set without requiring clock frequency matching or a sustained clock signal for tDLYCAL.
Once the registers of Frequency MR are set, and the MRUPD mode is exited, the input clock frequency needs to match 
the value selected by FSP-OP.Related timing parameters are tCCD_L, tCCD_L_WR, tCCD_L_WR2, tCCD_dlr, 
tCCD_WR_dlr, nODTLon_WR and nODTLon_WR_NT.

4.2.6. Chip Select Training Mode (CSTM)
4.2.6.1. Introduction
Chip Select Training Mode (CSTM) is a method to facilitate the loopback of a sampled sequence of the Chip Select 
(CS) signal. Once this mode is enabled, the device actively samples and drives feedback, where the CS signal is only 
sampled on the rising edge of even Clock (CK) cycles.
The host follows the CSTM procedure to align all phases of connected devices to achieve the maximum CS eye.
CSTM has two (2) states which can be toggled by DQ[1:0]: Command (CMD) State and Train State. During Command 
State, only the MRUPDATE sequence is actively processed. The Host drives CA[5:0] low outside CMD State during 
CSTM. During Train State, every set of 34 CK rising edge samples (68 CK in total) will be included in a logical 
computation to determine the CSTM sample result that is sent back to the Host on the DQ2.
CSTM includes making changes to VREFCS. VREFCS update and stabilization time depends on the VREF Current 
Generator (VRCG) for shorter delays. If VRCG is enabled via MRxx[b], tVREFCS_short or tVREFCS_long delay 
times are used, depending on the number of VREFCS steps being change. However, if VRCG is disabled, all VREFCS 
changes require the tVREFCS_weak delay.
4.2.6.2. CSTM Entry and Exit
Prior to entering CSTM, MRUPDATE Mode must be enabled by the MCME command. VREFDQ, DQ/DQS ODT 
and DQ drive strength settings must be adjusted to values that allow slow speed operation.
CSTM is entered and exited through MR05 (Functional Mode Register). Entry or Exit on either PSD (2p3 or 2p6 
configuration) will apply the operation to both PSDs. Entry and Exit operations must wait for previous MRUPDATE 
operations to complete.
4.2.6.3. Entering CSTM
To enter CSTM, the Host issues the MRUPDATE sequence to MR05[7:0]=024h with DQS differentially low prior to 
the 1st CS pulse of the MRUPDATE sequence. DQS must remain differentially low during CSTM entry. Once 
tMRUPD_Delay has been satisfied, CSTM has been entered in Command State.
Figure 32 — CSTM Entry Sequence

4.2.6.4. Exiting CSTM
To exit CSTM, the Host issues the MRUPDATE sequence to MR05[7:0]=00h during Command State with DQS 
differentially low. During CSTM Exit, DQS must remain differentially low until tMRUPD_Delay is satisfied. After 
tMRUPD_Delay, CSTM mode is exited.
Figure 33 — CSTM Exit Followed by CATM Entry
Issuing an MRUPDATE sequence exit during the CMD state will exit both CSTM and MRUPDATE modes within 
tMRUPD_Delay.
4.2.6.5. CSTM DQ Function and Mapping
CSTM uses the DQ bus, sampled by DQS, for function control. DQ[1:0] are input pins latched by DQS for CSTM 
control logic. The host is required to drive identical waveforms on the DQ[1:0] pins of both PSDs.
During CSTM, DQS_t/DQS_c are input only to sample DQ[1:0] as the function code. DQS is required to be differen-
tially low except while sampling DQ[1:0]. CSTM uses a four (4)eight (8) bit Function Code (FuncCode[37:0]), gener-
ated by foureight DQS rising pulses to sample DQ[1:0]. Entering CSTM internally resets the DQS rising pulse count 
to 0 to prepare for counting foureight rising pulses. Each Function Code is the combined sample value of the 2nd, 4th, 
6th, and 8th DQS differential rising edges (DQSR2, DQSR4, DQSR6, DQSR8, respectively) and the 4th DQS differ-
ential rising edge (DQSR4) as shown in the CSTM Function Code Mapping table.
DQ2 is an output pin for the CSTM sample results, per PSD.
All other DQ pins, if present, are unused during CSTM and are disabled.

defined as DQ[1:0] leading edge to DQSR2 and the hold timing tDHTrain is defined as DQSR2 to DQ[1:0] trailing

4.2.6.6. Command and Train States
Command (CMD) State is the default state after CSTM entry. CMD state is MRUPDATE mode enabled with CSTM 
functions controlled by DQ[1:0] and DQS. Only the MRUPDATE sequence and DES command are valid in the CMD 
State. During CSTM, the MRUPDATE sequence is only used to trigger functions and to change shadow register bits 
related to CSTM settings such as VREF CS, CS ODT, CK ODT, DQ/DQS ODT, and DQ driver strength, as needed. 
Shadow register "Apply" is needed to update any of the normal mode register values for use during CSTM CMD State. 
The VREFCS generator references the VREF CS Mode Registers settings while in CMD State.
Train State is used to adjust the VREF CS shadow registers setting, perform training sequence sampling, and output 
training results on DQ2. Train State uses CK ODT and CS ODT shadow register values, as well the VREF CS shadow 
register value (CA ODT and VREF CA shadow register values are also applied but have not impact since the CA bus 
is not used). In Train State, the command decoder is disabled and CA is required to be low all the time. VREFCS 
change functions will only apply to the VREF CS shadow register, which updates the VREFCS generator since it is 
referencing the shadow register while in Train State. Upon completion of CSTM, FuncCode[37:0]=8'b00000000

Code[37:0] is RFU will be ignored.

FuncCode[37:0]=48b'00001000: "Force Train State (FTS)" function. The FTS function forces Train State and 
switches from using normal VREF and ODT MRs to using the VREF and ODT shadow registers. The FTS function 
can be issued in both the CMD State and Train State. During Train State, VREFCS is controlled by the VREF CS 
shadow register.
FuncCode[3:0]=4b'1001: “VREFCS Step Down (VSD)" function. The VSD function forces the VREF_CS shadow 
register value to decrease by one step. If the register is at the minimum value, the VREFCS will remain at the minimum 
value if issued additional VSD functions. The VSD function can be issued in either CMD or Train state.
FuncCode[3:0]=4b'1010: “VREFCS Step Up (VSU)" function. The VSU function forces the VREF_CS shadow 
register value to increase by one step. If the register is at the maximum value, the VREFCS will remain at the maximum 
value if issued additional VSU functions. The VSU function can be issued in either CMD or Train state.
FuncCode[7:0]=8b'1VVVVVVV: "VREF Set Value (VSV)" function. The VSV function sets the VREF CS shadow 
register bits VREFCS[6:0] to FuncCode[6:0].
Note: VREFCS does not use the VREF CS shadow register during the CMD state, so changes will not be applied until

FuncCode[37:0]=48b'00001100: “Switch DQ2 Output (SDO)" function. The SDO function will switch DQ2 to output 
training results from the internal CK phase 0 to the internal CK phase 180. After tDQ2Output is met, DQ2 will output 
the CK phase 180 CS training result. Issuing the SDO function when DQ2 is already outputting CK phase 180 will not 
have any new effect, however tDQ2Output still needs to be met.
FuncCode[37:0]=48b'00001111: "Sample (SMP)" function. The SMP function performs the following operations to 
prepare for CS sampling: reset CS sample counter and logic, reset CK phase 0 and 180 internal training results to fail, 
and force DQ2 high. After the reset to CSTM delay (tRST2CSTM) is met, the CS pattern can be sampled. The Host 
uses a “1100" toggle pattern on CS, where each '1' or '0' represents a 2nCK long pulse. The CS is sampled on rising 
CK edges (internal CK phases 0 and 180). The Host sends 34 CS toggles (17 High and 17 Low pulses, each pulse is 
2nCK wide, 68nCK in total). The first CS high sample starts the internal CK counting on the next CS high sample. 
Once counting begins, the sample and check CS will continue for the even CK edges over the next 64nCK. The CS 
high pulse width is checked on the internal CK phase 0 (Q0) and the CS low pulse width is checked on internal CK 
phase 180 (Q2). Passing samples are recorded as '0' and failing samples recorded as '1'. After counting 64nCK cycles, 
the 16 Q0 results (CS sample on CK phase 0 is expected to be high, when passing is recorded as "0") and the 16 Q2 
results (CS sample on CK phase 180 is expected to be low, when passing is recorded as "0") are checked separately 
with a logical OR. The Q0 training result are output on DQ2, where a “0" indicates a pass and a "1" indicates failure. 
If a pass is detected, the output remains at "0" until switched to CMD State or next SMP/SDO function. The Host 
captures the Q0 pass/fail results after tDQ2Output, uses SDO function to switch DQ2 to output Q2 training results, and 
then captures the Q2 pass/fail results after tDQ2Output. The CS output result are reset by issuing the SMP function 
before sending out the next CS toggle pattern. Excessive CS pulses will not cause a new CS sampling unless a new 
SMP function has been issued. DQS is required to be differentially low during SMP function until all CS samples are

Issuing MRUPDATE sequences or executing a new CSTM function must wait for a previous operation to complete. 
The only exception is an MRUPDATE sequence for CSTM Exit, which may be used to exit CSTM during CMD State 
with DQS differentially low at least tFunc_Delay plus the required VREFCS update and settling delay after the last 
DQS differential rising edge.
The following figure is an example of the sequence to switch to CMD State and exit CSTM when DQS pulse count 
has been lost. The Host issues at least 816 DQS pulses with DQ[1:0]=00b, where each DQS pulse meets tFunc_Delay 
between rising edges. CSTM exit command must meet tFunc_Delay the required VREFCS update and settling delay 
(tVREFCS_long in this example) after the last DQS differential rising edge. CAs remain as "L", as the starting state 
may be the Train State.

4.2.6.8. Example CSTM Sequence
An example of the CSTM sequence is described below:
1)
If not in MRUPDATE mode, then use MCME to enter MRUPDATE mode.
2)
Complete the DQ Swizzling Discovery Mode (DSDM) operation to identify the host to DRAM DQ mapping.
3)
Set DQS differentially low. Enter CSTM and wait tMRUPD_Delay.
4)
Update the CS_ODT and VREF_CS Mode Register values, if required, and Apply.If required, update the CS 
ODT and VREF CS shadow register values and Apply to normal MR for use in CMD State. The VREF CS 
normal MR value must be valid in CMD State to allow the MRUPDATE sequence to execute properly.

=== PDF PAGE 85 ===
Page of 136

PROPOSED
5)
Update the CS_ODT and VREF_CS shadow register values, if required. The VREFCS value must be valid to 
allow the MRUPDATE sequence to execute properly, and Train State will use the VREF CS shadow register 
value.If required, update the CS ODT and VREF CS shadow register values for use in Train State.
6)
Switch from CMD State to Train State. Wait for tFunc_Delay and VREFCS update and settling delay. The 
shadow registers are being used.
7)
Update the VREFCS setting via the VSU or VSD function. Wait VREFCS update and settling delay after the 
last VSU/VSD.
8)
Issue the SMP function to prepare for sampling. After tFunc_Delay, DRAM is ready for CS sampling can begin 
and DQ2 output will be “1".
9)
Perform CS training (VREFCS, CS timing):
a)
The Host sends the CS toggle pattern 110011001100..., 68nCK in total.
b)
The CS toggle pattern is sampled with the first CS high sampled by an even clock edge. Counting starts 
on the second CS high sample and counts 32 samples (64nCK) before sending the output results on DQ2. 
Within the 64nCK sample window, all Q0 CS samples must pass for the DQ2 output to be “0".
c)
The Host reads DQ2 after tDQ2Output and records it, issues the SDO function to output Q2 training 
results, and again reads DQ2 after tDQ2Output and records it. All Q2 CS samples must pass for the DQ2 
output to be “0".
d)
The Host calculates the Q0 and Q2 training results, where both values being “0" is considered a pass. 
(Both PSDs and both the Q0 and Q2 phases need to be “0" for the training is considered PASS.)
10)
Repeat step 7 to 9 as necessary to complete the first round of CS training. Host should analyze training results 
to identify DRAMs into four CK phase groups based on the center of CS eye. Different groups' CS eye center 
should be about 1 tCK apart. If all DRAM happens to be in same group then, skip step 11.
11)
Use the FCS command to switch to the CMD State, wait tFUNC_Delay and the VREFCS update and settling 
delay.
12)
Choose one of the CK phase group devices as the target group. Issue CPS function to the other CK phase groups 
to adjust their CS eye mask center to align with target CK phase group. Each CPS function will shift CS eye by 
1 nCK (90 degrees). Multi CPS functions may be needed on CK phase groups that are multiple tCK away from 
target group.
13)
Repeat step 6 to 9 for another round of CS training with all devices even CK phase aligned. The Host can 
analyze the training results and apply the CS delay to the center of common CS eye window to achieve the max 
CS eye margin.
14)
Use the FCS command to switch to the CMD State, wait tFUNC_Delay and the VREFCS update and settling 
delay.
15)
Exit CSTM. After tMRUPD_Delay, CS is “trained".
4.2.6.9. CS-CK Sweeping and CK Phase Alignment
The following explains how to align the internal CK phase of different DRAM.
Fundamentally, the purpose of CSTM is to align the CS eye of each DRAM, which can be described with a 2D shmoo 
of the CS to CK delay on the X-axis and the VREFCS on the Y-axis. The CS to CK delay (CS rising edge to CK rising 
edge) is a common setting that impacts all shared signals, while the VREFCS can be used to center each CS eye prop-
erly. To maximize the CS margin, all CS eye should be centered and the CS to CK delay is adjusted to compensate for 
mismatch between the external and internal clocks.
During CSTM, the Host calculates the CS to CK delay referencing a fixed Host clock phase. This chosen phase 
remains fixed during whole training procedure. The example below chooses the Host clock phase Q2 as the reference 
clock phase for the CS to CK delay calculation.
The following figure shows four DRAMs (A, B, C, D) to explain the CK phase detection during the CS-CK sweep, 
with an assumption of a 0.25tCK CS to CK mismatch. The host start CS-CK sweeping with the delay at 0. Due to 


=== PDF PAGE 86 ===
 Page 62 of 136
TRAINING
JEDEC PROPOSEDINITIALIZATION AND TRAINING
PROPOSED
external to internal mismatch, the CS to T0 CK delay is 0.25tCK. DRAM A's Q1 aligns to T0. To have CS centered at 
Q0, the CS-CK delay will be 1.75tCK which is the center of CS eye mask. DRAM B's Q2 aligns to T0, so the center 
of CS eye mask will be 0.75tCK. DRAM C's Q3 aligns to T0, so the center of CS eye mask will be -0.25tCK or 
3.75tCK. And DRAM D's Q0 aligns to T0, so the center of CS eye mask will be -2.75tCK. In this example, host selects 
DRAM B as the target for all other DRAMs to align to. The Host issues the CPS function once to DRAM A, twice to 
DRAM D and three times to DRAM C. After CPS functions, all CS eyes are center aligned to DRAM B, which has 
internal Q0 aligned to T2 CK. The CS eye center 0.75tCK is the extra delay needed to center CS at T2 CK, which is 
Q0 of all DRAM. The Host labels the internal phase accordingly.
Figure 41 — Example of a CS-CK Sweep with Four DRAMs having Different 
Internal CK Phases

4.2.7. VREFCS
VREFCS is defined as a ratio of the VDDQ voltage. VREFCS is independently controlled per PSD with host MRW 
to MR86[6:0] in normal operation, or by the VREF_CS shadow register with VREFCS Apply Functional Mode 
(MR06[2]=1) in MRUPDATE mode.

4.2.8. Command Address Training Mode (CATM)
4.2.8.1. Introduction
The command address bus must be trained before nominal frequency operation. Command Address Training Mode 
(CATM) is a method to facilitate the loopback of a logical combination of the sampled CA [5:0] signals. In this mode, 
the CK_t/CK_c differential clock (CK) is running and the Chip Select (CS) qualifies when the CK samples the CA 
signals. CATM is performed per-CA bit and per even/odd CK phase to adjust the per-bit skew and duty cycle distor-
tion. The Host sends a long CA burst, or multiple CA bursts, with CS high, and the CA pattern is captured on each CS 
qualified differential CK rising edge.
CATM has two (2) states which can be toggled by DQ[1:0]: Command (CMD) State and Train State. During 
Command State, only the MRUPDATE sequence is actively processed. During Train State, the burst or bursts of CA 
data qualified by CS high will be sampled on every differential CK rising edge and compared with a PRBS pattern in 
a logical computation to determine the CATM output results for feedback to the Host.
The controller and DRAM use matching PRBS generators to compare per even/odd CK phase and per-CA bit in 
parallel, and feedback is returned on DQ2. The internal VREFCA default level is suitable for MRUPDATE operation 
during initialization, but the VREFCA must be trained to achieve suitable receiver voltage margin for normal opera-
tion. The Host follows the CATM procedure to center the internal VREFCA in the CA data eye and to adjust timing 
of the CA signals to maximize CA timing margin. While in CATM, no functional commands are executed. The func-
tional command interface is restored only after exiting this mode.
Prior to entering the CATM, the DQ swizzling discovery mode must be completed, and CSTM must have been 
performed to align the CK phase to achieve the maximum CS eye.
CATM includes making changes to VREFCA. VREFCA update and stabilization time of tVREFCA_short or tVREF-
CA_long are used, depending on the number of VREFCA steps being change.
During CATM, per-pin VREFCA offset is expected to be disabled. If previously enabled, the Host needs to disable 
any per-pin VREFCA offset before performing CATM.
4.2.8.2. CATM Entry and Exit
Prior to entering CATM, MRUPDATE Mode must be enabled by the MCME command. VREFDQ, DQ/DQS ODT 
and DQ drive strength settings must be adjusted to values that allow slow speed operation.
CATM is entered and exited through MR05 (Functional Mode Register). Issuing the entry or exit on either PSD (2p3 
or 2p6 configurations) will apply the operation to both PSDs. Entry and Exit operations must wait for previous MRUP-
DATE operations to complete.


=== PDF PAGE 92 ===
 Page 68 of 136
TRAINING
JEDEC PROPOSEDINITIALIZATION AND TRAINING
PROPOSED
4.2.8.3. Entering CATM
To enter CATM, the Host issues the MRUPDATE sequence to MR05[7:0]=035h with DQS differentially low prior to 
or during the 1st CS pulse of the MRUPDATE sequence. DQS must remain differentially low during CATM entry. 
Once tMRUPD_Delay has been satisfied, CATM has been entered in the Command State.
Figure 42 — CATM Entry Sequence Following CSTM Exit
4.2.8.4. Exiting CATM
To exit CATM, the Host issues the MRUPDATE sequence to MR05[7:0] = 00h in the Command State with DQS 
differentially low. During CATM Exit, DQS must remain differentially low until tMRUPD_Delay is satisfied. After 
tMRUPD_Delay, CATM mode is exited.
Figure 43 — CATM Exit Followed by MRUPDATE Mode Entry
Issuing an MRUPDATE sequence exit during the CMD state will exit both CATM and MRUPDATE modes within 
tMRUPD_ Delay.
4.2.8.5. CATM DQ Function and Mapping
CATM uses the DQ bus, sampled by DQS, for function control. DQ[1:0] are input pins latched by DQS for CATM 
control logic. The Host is required to drive identical waveforms on the DQ[1:0] pins of both PSDs.

During CATM, DQS_t/DQS_c (DQS) are input only to sample DQ[1:0] as the function code. DQS is required to be 
differentially low except while sampling DQ[1:0]. CATM uses a four (4)eight (8) bit Function Code (Func-
Code[37:0]), generated by four (4)eight (8) DQS rising pulses to sample DQ[1:0]. Entering CATM internally resets 
the DQS rising pulse count to 0 to prepare for counting the four rising pulses. Each Function Code is the combined 
sample value of the 2nd, 4th, 6th, and 8th differential DQS rising edges (DQSR2, DQSR4, DQSR6, DQSR8, respec-
tively) and the 4th differential DQS rising edge (DQSR4) as shown the CATM Function Code Mapping table.
DQ2 is an output pin for the CATM sample results, per PSD.
All other DQ pins, if present, are unused during CATM and are disabled.
As shown in the figure below, the leading and trailing DQS differentially low timing (tDQSPreTrain and tDQSPost-
Train, respectively) must be met before and after the DQS pulses. tFunc_Delay is the minimum time between Function 
Codes. Each DQS pulse must meet tDQSPWTrain on both high and low pulse.

DQ[1:0] must meet setup and hold timing (tDSTrain and tDHTrain, respectively). The setup timing tDSTrain is 
defined as DQ[1:0] leading edge to DQSR2 and the hold timing tDHTrain is defined as DQSR2 to DQ[1:0] trailing 
edge. The same setup and hold timing requirements apply to DQSR4, DQSR6, and DQSR8.
Figure 44 — Example Timing for Functional Codes
NOTE: CK and time breaks are not included.
4.2.8.6. Command and Train States
Command (CMD) State is the default state after CATM entry. CMD state is MRUPDATE mode enabled with CATM 
functions controlled by DQ[1:0] and DQS. Only the MRUPDATE sequence and DES command are valid in the CMD 
State. During CATM, the MRUPDATE sequence is only used to trigger functions and to change shadow register bits 
related to CATM settings such as VREFCA, CA ODT, CK ODT, DQ/DQS ODT, and DQ drive strength, as needed. 
Shadow register "Apply" is needed to update any of the normal mode register values for use during CATM CMD State. 
The VREFCA generator references the VREF CA Mode Registers settings while in CMD State.
Train State is used to adjust the VREFCA shadow registers setting, perform training sequence sampling, and output 
training results on DQ2. Train State uses CK_ODT, CS_ODT, CA_ODT shadow register values, as well the VREF 
CS and VREF CA shadow register values. In Train State, CA is required to be low except when sending the training 
sequence. VREFCA change functions will only apply to the VREF CA shadow register, which updates the VREFCA 
generator since it is referencing the shadow register while in Train State. Upon completion of CATM, Func-
Code[37:0]=48b'00000000b forces the return from Train State to CMD State, restoring use of the normal VREF CA 
Mode Register. The Host can apply the VREF CA shadow register value to VREF CA MR (MR1A84[6:0]) per training 
results during CMD State or after exiting CATM using the VREF CA and CA ODT Apply Functional Mode 
(MR05[xy]6[7:0]=08h).Shadow register values are not changed by entering or exiting CATM, by entering CMD State, 
or by entering Train State.
Issuing the shadow register Apply during CMD State or after exiting CATM, switching from CMD State to Train State, 
and switching from Train State to CMD State, requires waiting the appropriate VREFCA update and stabilization 
delay which depends on the number of VREFCA steps being changed.

4.2.8.7. CATM Functions
CATM uses a four (4)eight (8) bit Function Code (FuncCode[37:0]) to execute training functions. When a Func-
Code[37:0] is received, the corresponding functions executes within tFunc_Delay. Some functions can only be used 
in certain CATM states. FuncCode[37:0] issued outside of an allowed state will be ignored. Any unused Func-
Code[37:0] is RFU and will be ignored.

FuncCode[37:0]=48b'00000000: "Force CMD State (FCS)" function. The FCS function may be issued in both the 
CMD State and the Train State.
FuncCode[37:0]=48b'00001000: "Force Train State (FTS)" function. The FTS function forces Train State and 
switches from using normal VREF and ODT MRs to using the VREF and ODT shadow registers. The FTS function 
can be issued in both the CMD State and Train State. During Train State, VREFCA is controlled by the VREF CA 
shadow register.
FuncCode[3:0]=4b'1001: “VREFCA Step Down (VSD)” function. The VSD function forces the VREF_CA shadow 
register value to decrease by one step. If the register is at the minimum value, the VREFCA will remain at the minimum 
value if issued additional VSD functions. The VSD function can be issued in either CMD or Train state.

FuncCode[3:0]=4b'1010: “VREFCA Step Up (VSU)” function. The VSU function forces the VREF_CA shadow 
register value to increase by one step. If the register is at the maximum value, the VREFCA will remain at the

register bits VREFCA[6:0] to FuncCode[6:0].
Note: VREFCA does not use the VREF CA shadow register during the CMD state, so changes will not be applied until 
Train State is entered. Continuous VSD and VSU operation is possible if tFUNC_Delay between each operation is 
met. However, tThe Host must wait the required VREFCA update and settling delay after last VSU or VSD any VSV 
function.
Figure 47 — VSV Function Example and tVREFCA_short/long Requirement
NOTE: 1:CK and time break are not included on purpose. The diagram is intended to serve as a reference for consecutive 
VSU/VSDVSV behavior and tVREFCA_short/long timing requirement. Any additional timing requirements not depicted in the 
figure must also be satisfied.
NOTE: 2: VREF CA registers are shown initially updated to an appropriate previously set value as recommended in section XXX, 
Voltage Ramp and Device Initialization.
FuncCode[37:0]=48b'00001111: “Sample (SMP)” function. The SMP function performs the following operations to 
prepare for CA sampling: reset the LFSR, reset the CATM internal training results to fail, force DQ2 high, and enable 
CS high qualified sampling of the CA burst or bursts. After the reset to CATM delay (tRST2CATM) is met, the (first) 
CA burst can be sampled. The Host sends the correct PRBS pattern on the CA bus, qualified by CS high. The first 
cycle of the CA burst with CS high must be sent by the Host corresponding to a phase 0° CK rising edge, as previously 
identified through CSTM. The DRAM will sample the CA burst on this CK differential rising edge and each subse-
quent differential rising edge of CK until CS is sampled low. The first phase 0° CK rising edge with CS low terminates 
the burst; only CA samples latched prior to this CK rising edge will be included in the burst pattern comparison. The 
Host may use any CA burst length that is a non-zero multiple of 4 nCK. The CA sampling may be broken into multiple 
CS high qualified CA bursts, with each burst separated by "gaps" in which CS is low. In this case, each CA burst length 
must be a multiple of 4nCK and must be sent corresponding to a phase 0° CK rising edge. The LFSR is not advanced 
during any CS low "gap" period.

PROPOSED
For each sample of the CA bus, the sampled value is compared against the expected value from the internal PRBS 
generator. Passing samples are recorded as '0' and failing samples recorded as '1'. These results are accumulated across 
the full all bursts per CA bit and even/odd phase using a logical ‘OR’ operation. Note, "even" phase corresponds to 
both 0° and 180º phases of CK; "odd" phase corresponds to both 90° and 270° phases of CK. This yields 10 separate 
feedback bits if CA5 is not enabled: 5 CA lanes x 2 even/odd phases. If CA5 is enabled, this increases to 12 separate 
feedback bits: 6 CA lanes x 2 even/odd phases. See the table below for the feedback sequence.
DQS is required to be differentially low during the CA burst or bursts. After the (last) CA burst is terminated and 
tCATM2FBO is met, the DRAM is ready to output feedback results. After this point, whenever the DRAM receives 
FuncCode[37:0]=48b'00000101 it will execute the Feedback Output (FBO) function. On the first execution of the FBO 
function, the first feedback bit on DQ2 is CA0_E, with the results being available tADR after the last rising DQ edge 
of the FBO function. A feedback bit value of ‘0’ indicates the entire burst passed for that CA bit and phase. A feedback 
bit value of ‘1’ indicates a fail for that CA bit and phase at some point during the burst. On the next execution of the 
FBO function, DQ2 will output the next feedback bit, CA0_O, and so on for each iteration of the FBO function until 
the final feedback has been output. If additional FBO functions are executed after the final feedback bit has been 
output, the final feedback bit will continue to be output. The Host can reset the feedback results and the LFSR for the 
PRBS pattern by issuing the SMP function again, enabling a new CS qualified sampling of the CA bus.

DQS differential rising edge.
The following figure is an example of the sequence to switch to CMD State and exit CATM when DQS pulse count 
has been lost. The Host issues at least 816 DQS pulses with DQ[1:0]=00b, where each DQS pulse meets tFunc_Delay 
between rising edges. CATM exit command must meet tFunc_Delay plus the required VREFCA update and settling 
delay after the last DQS differential rising edge. CAs remain as "L", as the starting state may be the Train State.

Linear Feedback Shift Register (LFSR). This sequence generator will be used on the Host to generate the bit stream 
for the CA burst. The DRAM will use the same sequence generator to generate the expected data and then compare

the received data with the expected data to determine if the CA bits were transferred successfully from the Host to the

The polynomial to be used for the 24-bit Galois LFSR is x^24 + x^22 + x^21 + x^19 + x^17 + x^14 + x^10 + x^4 + 
1. The figures below show the implementation of the PRBS generator using a 24-bit shift register and XOR gates, as 
well as the mapping of LFSR bits to the CA bus. Error checking on the CA burst is performed in 4nCK units (corre-
sponding to 20 bits if CA5 is disabled or 24 bits if CA5 is enabled). The LFSR updates to a new 24-bit value every 
4nCK. Note that the same bits are mapped to CA[4:0] whether CA5 is enabled or not.
Figure 51 — Galois LFSR 24-bit Circuit
Figure 52 — LFSR 24-bit Mapping to CA Bus

default seed of 24'h4894AC are shown in the table below, where L0 is the first data pattern driven from the host and

4.2.8.9. Example CATM Sequence
An example of the CATM sequence is described below:
1)
If not in MRUPDATE mode, then use MCME to enter MRUPDATE mode.
2)
If not previously completed, perform the DQ Swizzling Discovery Mode (DSDM) operation to identify the Host 
to DRAM DQ mapping.
3)
If not previously completed, perform CS Training Mode to align all CK phases to achieve the maximum CS eye.
4)
Set DQS differentially low. Enter CATM and wait tMRUPD_Delay.
5)
Update the CA_ODT and VREF_CA Mode Register values, if required, and Apply.If required, update the CA 
ODT and VREF CA shadow register values and Apply to normal MR for use in CMD State. The VREF CA 
normal MR value must be valid in CMD State to allow the MRUPDATE sequence to execute properly.
6)
Update the CA_ODT and VREF_CA shadow register values, if required. The VREFCA value must be valid to 
allow the MRUPDATE sequence to execute properly, and Train State will use the VREF_CA shadow register 
value.If required, update the CA ODT and VREF CA shadow register values for use in Train State.
7)
Switch from CMD State to Train State. Wait for tFunc_Delay and VREFCA update and settling delay. The 
shadow registers are being used.
8)
Update the VREFCA setting via the VSU or VSDVSV function. Wait VREFCA update and settling delay after 
the last VSU/VSDVSV.
9)
Issue the SMP function to prepare for sampling. After tRST2CATM, The DRAM is ready for CA sampling.
10)
Perform CA training (VREFCA, CA timing):
a)
The Host sends the CA burst or bursts of the PRBS pattern with CS high.
b)
The CA burst pattern(s) is(are) sampled on each CS high qualified CK differential rising edge, comparing 
each bit with the expected PRBS pattern and accumulating the feedback results per CA bit and even/odd 
CK phase using a logical ‘OR’ operation. A resulting value of ‘1’ indicates a fail while a ‘0’ indicates a

pass. Note: "even" phase corresponds to both 0° and 180º phases of CK; "odd" phase corresponds to both

c)
After terminating the (last) burst and waiting tCATM2FBO, issue the FBO function repeatedly to output

Calculate the full training results. When the results for all CA bits and CK phases are ‘0’ for both PSDs,

11)
Repeat step 8 to 10 as necessary to complete the first round of CA training.
12)

delay.
13)
Exit CATM. After tMRUPD_Delay, CA is “trained”.

4.2.9. Rx CK/DQS/CA/CS CTLE Control
4.2.9.1. Rx CK/DQS/CA/CS CTLE Control
Rx CK/DQS/CA/CS in DDR6 shall have a Continuous-Time Linear Equalizer (CTLE) to compensate channel loss for 
high frequency operation by providing increased gain at higher frequencies, offering a simple and effective solution 
for DDR6. Rx CK/DQS/CA/CS CTLE training shall be performed after CSTM and CATM sequence.
4.2.9.2. MR1C– Rx CTLE Control for CK, DQS

4.2.10. Per-pin VREFCA
DDR6 adopts Per-pin VREFCA training to compensate for CA input offset due to SOC transmitter mismatch, DRAM 
termination mismatch, and DRAM CA path mismatch. Per-pin VREFCA training allows host to program the reference 
VREF level to an optimum point for each single-ended CA receiver. Per-pin VREFCA training is performed after 
CATM exit.The offset levels among optimized VREFCA levels for each CA pin, which is obtained from VREFCA 
training in CATM, shall be applied as the per-pin VREFCA offset.
