4.2.11. Write Leveling Training Mode
4.2.11.1. Introduction
The DDR6 SDRAM supports a ‘write leveling’ feature to allow the controller to compensate CK to WRITE DQS 
timing skew. The DDR6 Write Leveling Training also allows for an unmatched path between CK and DQS within the 
DRAM, and thus supports an internal Write Leveling Training flow to account for the difference in internal delays.
The DDR6 DRAM also provides a programmable timing in its write logic, controlled by the Write Leveling Internal 
Cycle Alignment (WICA) mode register (MRy), which provides a means for improved performance of the device’s 
receiver. The proper setting of this register shall be determined by the memory controller, either as described in the 
sections below, or by some other means. This delay setting is specific to each DRAM, its Write Preamble setting, and 
the operating frequency being used. PDA select ID is allowed while in WL training mode to program settings specific 
to each DRAM. Once the proper setting has been determined for a given Write preamble setting, and operating 
frequency, that setting would be subsequently restored to the DRAM after reset, power-cycle, or return to a previously 
used operating frequency.
The memory controller can use the ‘write leveling’ feature and feedback from the DDR6 DRAM to adjust the 
controller’s differential DQS (DQS_t - DQS_c) output delay to align to the phase and cycle that corresponds to the 
Write Latency (WL) delay after the WRITE command. The memory controller involved in the leveling must have an 
adjustable delay setting on the DQS pins to align the rising edge of differential DQS with the timing at the receiver 
that is the pin-level Write Latency (External Write Leveling Training), and to align DQS with the internal DRAM 
Write Latency timing point (Write Leveling Internal Cycle Alignment). The internal DRAM Write Latency timing 
point may be skewed from the pin-level Write Latency timing point. The host will minimize this skew (tDQSoffset) 
through the Write Leveling Training flow.
Since the system and DIMM delays vary, the DRAM will support the ability for the host to align the DQS timings with 
a pin-level Write Latency differential CK (CK_t-CK_c) edge. This alignment is referred to as External Write Leveling. 
Once the DQS host timings are aligned at the DRAM Write Latency timing, the internal DRAM timings are optimized 
for lowest power and internal delay. This is accomplished when the host enables the Internal Write Timing setting in 
MRx. In order to compensate for the difference in delay, the host will execute an Internal Write Leveling Training 
sequence, which includes sweeping the Write Leveling Internal Cycle Alignment (WICA) settings in MRy (Write 
Leveling Internal Cycle Alignment Operation) and then finalizing the differential DQS phase and offset (Write 
Leveling Internal Phase Alignment and Final Host DQS Timing Operation).
During Write Leveling Training (both External and Internal), the DQS pattern shall include the full programmed write 
preamble and only the first toggle of the normal data burst sequence. The DRAM will sample the Internal Write 
Leveling Pulse relative to the first DQS toggle of the data burst sequence (last rising differential DQS edge sent by the 
host) and asynchronously feed back the result of this sample on the DQ bus, transitioning between tWLOmin and 
tWLOmax. If the DQ bus output is LOW at tWLOmax, the Internal Write Leveling Pulse signal was sampled while it 
was deasserted (LOW). Likewise, if the DQ bus output is HIGH at tWLOmax, the Internal Write Leveling Pulse signal 
was sampled while it was asserted (HIGH). The sampled feedback state will remain on the DQ bus until a subsequent 
Write Leveling sample changes the state, or until Write Leveling Training is exited.
The Internal Write Leveling Pulse is generated in response to a WRITE command, and held statically low otherwise. 
To perform the Write Leveling training, the controller repeatedly sends a WRITE command, delays DQS, and moni-
tors the DQ feedback after tWLOmax until a transition from 0 to 1 is detected, indicating alignment with the Internal 
Write Leveling Pulse.
During Internal Write Leveling Training flows, the host will apply an offset to the starting point or the final setting of 
the DQS signals. The offsets are referred to as WL_ADJ_start and WL_ADJ_end. This will minimize the tDQSoffset 
variation across different DRAM devices. The WL_ADJ_start and WL_ADJ_end values depend on the tWPRE 
setting.


=== PDF PAGE 114 ===
 Page 90 of 136
TRAINING
JEDEC PROPOSEDINITIALIZATION AND TRAINING
PROPOSED
When External and Internal Write Leveling Training flows are complete and the final WL_ADJ_end offset has been 
applied to the DQS timings, the DQS is phase aligned and cycle aligned for write operations. During the training 
sequence, the DRAM in Write Leveling training mode will apply ODT to the strobes in the same P R O P O S E D 
DDR6 Write Leveling Item # 1897.67 Page 3 of 8 way as for functional operation. All non-target ranks (which will 
not be in Write Leveling Training Mode) will apply ODT as defined for functional operation. Prior to executing the 
DDR6 Write Leveling Training Flow, the DRAM tWPRE value must be configured to the functional operation setting.
NOTE: DQS ODT is based on a DQS PARK Mode and is not enabled and disabled with DQ ODT timings.
4.2.11.2. Write Leveling Mode Registers
The MR fields for Write Leveling Training, Internal Write Timing, and Write Leveling Internal Cycle Alignment are 
part of MRx and MRy. To enter Write Leveling Training Mode, the controller shall program MRx:OP[a]=1. Likewise, 
to exit Write Leveling Training Mode, the controller will program MRx:OP[a]=0. For pseudo split die, the Write 
Leveling internal cycle alignment may be performed on either pseudo split die. Once leveling is completed on the 
selected pseudo split die, the host shall apply the resulting WICA training values into the WICA mode register shared 
by both pseudo split dies, ensuring consistent alignment within a single die. The Internal Write Timing, once enabled 
(MRx:OP[b]=1), shall remain enabled through the Internal Write Leveling Training flow and for functional operation. 
The host is responsible for incrementing the Write Leveling Internal Cycle Alignment setting until the Internal Write 
Leveling Pulse is pulled early enough to align with the differential DQS signal that was previously aligned with the 
pin-level Write Latency timing. The Write Leveling Internal Cycle Alignment setting only applies when the Internal 
Write Timing is enabled.
4.2.11.3. External Write Leveling Training Operation
When Write Leveling Mode is enabled (MRx:OP[a]=1) and the Internal Write Timing is disabled (MRx:OP[b]=0), the 
DRAM will have an Internal Write Leveling pulse that indicates how the DQS signals shall be aligned to match the 
pin-level Write Latency timings. The rising edge of the Internal Write Leveling Pulse will align to the rising edge of 
the differential CK signal that coincides with Write Latency (WL), as shown in the following diagrams.
The differential DQS signals driven by the controller during leveling mode must be terminated by the DRAM based 
on ranks populated. Similarly, the DQ bus driven by the DRAM must also be terminated at the controller.
All data bits shall carry the leveling feedback to the controller across the DRAM configurations 2p3, 2p6, 1p6 and 
1p12.
The following diagram shows the timing sequence to enter Write Leveling Training Mode, operation during Write 
Leveling Training Mode (with Internal Write Timings disabled), and the timing sequence to exit Write Leveling 
Training mode. An MRW command is sent to enable Write Leveling Training Mode. Prior to sending the MRW 
command to enable Write Leveling Training Mode, the host memory controller must drive the DQS signals differen-
tially low. After tWLPEN time, the controller can send a WRITE command, followed by strobe pulses. The DQS 
signals shall always drive differentially low, except during the WRITE preamble and burst strobe sequence. There is 
no restriction as to how early the strobe pulses are sent, so long as they are no earlier than WL/2 after the WRITE 
command. The DRAM will sample the Internal Write Leveling Pulse relative to the first DQS toggle of the data burst 
sequence and asynchronously feed back the result of this sample on the DQ bus, transitioning between tWLOmin and 
tWLOmax.
While in Write Leveling Training mode, the host controller may send ACT and PRE commands. The DRAM will 
ignore these commands. The address associated with the ACT and the WRITE commands may be any value. The 
following timing diagrams demonstrate the timing requirements associated with Write Leveling Training Mode Entry, 


=== PDF PAGE 115 ===
Page of 136
TRAINING
JEDEC PROPOSEDINITIALIZATION AND TRAINING
PROPOSED
the Internal Write Leveling Pulse alignment during External Write Leveling Training, and the DQ sample feedback 
timing.
Figure 54 — Write Leveling Training Mode Timing Diagram (External Training, 
0 Sample)
Figure 55 — Write Leveling Training Mode Timing Diagram (External Training, 
1 Sample)
The Memory controller initiates Write Leveling mode of all DRAMs in a rank by setting MRx:OP[a]=1. When 
entering write leveling mode, the DQ pins are in undefined driving mode. Since the controller levels one rank at a time, 
all non-target ranks will set Write Leveling Mode to disabled. The Controller may assert non-target ODT through the 
normal WRITE command protocol. DQS_RTT_PARK termination will apply to the DQS_t and DQS_c signals.
The Controller shall drive DQS differentially LOW prior to sending the MRW command to enable Write Leveling 
Training Mode. The WRITE command must occur after a delay of tWLPEN relative to when the MRW enabled Write 
Leveling Training Mode.
The DRAM will sample the Internal Write Leveling Pulse and asynchronously feed back the result of this sample on 
the DQ bus, transitioning between tWLOmin and tWLOmax. The controller can sample the state of any DQ bit after 
tWLOmax. Based on the sampled state, the controller decides to increment or decrement the DQS delay setting and 
launches the next WRITE command with associated DQS_t/DQS_c pulse (or pulse sequence) after some time, which 
is controller dependent. Once a 0 to 1 transition on the DQ bus is detected, the controller locks DQS delay setting and 
external write leveling is achieved for the device. Write Leveling Training may be executed by issuing consecutive 
Write commands after entering Write Leveling Mode has been enabled (no requirement to exit and reenter between 
each Write command). The minimum Write to Write command spacing during Write Leveling Mode is tWL_Write

4.2.11.4. Write Leveling Internal Cycle Alignment Operation
After the external Write Leveling Training step is completed, and after the host DQS signals have been aligned to the 
pin-level Write Latency timing, the host will apply a negative offset (WL_ADJ_start) to the DQS timing. The 
WL_ADJ_start offset moves the DQS timing prior to the Internal Write Leveling Pulse (causing LOW observed on 
the DQ pins), and it is dependent on the tWPRE setting (shown in table below). This will be a reference point for 
aligning the Internal Write Leveling Pulse via the WICA settings in MRy.
The Internal Cycle Alignment training begins when an MRW command is issued to enable Internal Write Timing in 
MRx (keeping OP[a]=1 and setting MRx:OP[b]=1). The host sweeps the Write Leveling Internal Cycle Alignment 
(WICA) delay settings in MRy, starting with the 0tCK offset default. Increasing the WICA mode register offset setting 
speeds up the timing of the WRITE command by reducing the WRITE Command to Internal Write Pulse delay until 
the DQS is aligned with the internal Write Leveling Pulse assertion. Alignment results in a logic HIGH on the DQ pins.

internal Write Leveling Pulse. Once this is complete, the host will then add a positive offset of WL_ADJ_end (depen-

Internal Write Timing will remain enabled and the Internal Cycle Alignment setting will retain the coarse setting that 
was trained. After every reset, the host must either restore these settings or execute the full Write Leveling Training 
flow.

4.2.11.6. DRAM Termination During Write Leveling
When the DRAM is in Write Leveling Mode, the DQS_c/DQS_t termination (DQS_RTT_PARK) and the Command 
and Control termination (RTT_CA, RTT_CK, RTT_CS) will be the same as for functional operation. The DQ signals 
will not be terminated in the DRAM, but instead will be driving values to the controller. The host controller will apply 
termination for the DQ signals.

4.2.12. Per-pin VREFDQ
DDR6 supports per-pin VREFDQ offset based on a common VREFDQ setting. Per-pin VREFDQ can adjust input 
common mode level which is distributed to each pin.

4.2.13. Host Receiver Training (HRT) Mode
4.2.13.1. HRT Introduction
DRAM shall support a Host Receiver Training function similar to Write Training Mode, to maintain robust link perfor-
mance, especially in systems that employ multiple operating modes to enhance I/O scalability and reduce training time. 
This DRAM feature enables the host to perform receiver data training including internal receiver drift compensation 
and maintain optimal signal integrity throughout system operation.
4.2.13.2. HRT pattern
HRTM can select the pattern type between user defined pattern vs PRBS with MR setting (MRDD). The PRBS mode 
provides an independent (different pattern) per DQ lane using a 24-bit Fibonacci LFSR. One 24-bit F-LFSR generator 
will support x6 (max) DQ lanes. The polynomial for the Fibonacci LFSR is x^24 + x^20 + x^11+ x^5 + 1. For the Host 
DQ Receiver drift improvements, the DRAM shall drive user defined pattern and/or LFSR pattern during bus idle time. 
Figure 1 and Figure 2 show the details of left shift Fibonacci LFSR hardware and 24-bit PRBS pattern.
Figure 61 — Fibonacci 24-bit LFSR circuit
Figure 62 — 24-bit PRBS pattern details
4.2.13.3. Entry and Exit for Host Receiver Training Mode (HRT)
To enter Host Receiver Training Mode (HRT), the host memory controller shall issue a Mode Register Write (MRW) 
command targeting MR05 OP[4:0] = 10010B, with the HRT Mode entry opcode. When the DRAM is in Receiver 
Training mode, commands are still actively processed.
To exit HRT Mode, the host memory controller shall issue an MRW command to MR5 OP[7:0] = 00000000B, with 
the HRT Mode Exit opcode.
4.2.13.4. Host Receiver Training Operation
Host to configure mode register with read pattern or LFSR details and this training is entered by issuing an MRW 
command. Host to issue a READ command, which causes the DDR6 DRAM to drive the MR content depending on

the mode register settings (LFSR vs Read pattern). The timing of data return and strobe sequence match a regular Read 
operation which supports back-to-back read the user read pattern / LFSR data. HRT mode is exited by issuing an MRW 
command. In this training mode, the only valid commands are READ and MRW.
Host Receiver Training Mode Registers:
4.2.13.5. PRBS Pattern Generator for WTM and HRT modes
PRBS generator will start with the seed (Example 24b LFSR Seed: F7410B) and the below LFSR pattern table shows 
the example of shift mode where each DQ lanes gets different pattern on the lane. The upper 5 extra staging flip-flop 
bits getting reset to 1, hence, users shall only independently program the LFSR seed [23:5] and need to calculate LFSR 
seed [4:0] to correctly initialize the LFSR. The LFSR seed S[4:0] is calculated using XOR and the details are given 
below. This is same as Write Training Mode LFSR pattern. Table 1 illustrates the mapping of the pattern to DQ [5:0].
LFSR Seed Calculation
S4 = XOR(S23,S19,S10,1)
S3 = XOR(S22,S18,S9,1)
S2 = XOR(S21,S17,S8,1)
S1 = XOR(S20,S16,S7,1)

S0 = XOR(S19,S15,S6,1)
Figure 63 — LFSR Pattern for Host Receiver Training Mode DQ mapping
DDR6 DRAM devices implement a Host Receiver Training (HRT) function that drives training patterns onto the DQ 
signal pins to the host and in case of Write Training Mode (WTM), it receives the LFSR pattern from the host on the 
DQ pins. The output pattern shall be sourced either from a user-defined pattern or from an internally generated Linear 
Feedback Shift Register (LFSR) sequence.
4.2.13.6. LFSR Registers
An independent 24-bit Fibonacci LFSR generator provides patterns for up to 6 DQ lanes. The source of the DQ output 
pattern shall be selected via MRDD:OP[0], where this field selects between LFSR output and the user-defined read 
pattern resource. The MRDD[3] field shall be used to reset the LFSR seed value and associated staging flip-flops. 
Upon reset, the 5-bit staging flip-flops shall be initialized to their default value of 11111.
If MRDD[0] is set to 1b0, MRD8to MRDAcontains the user-defined pattern. MRD7 is used to assign Pattern Inversion 
per DQ lane. If MRDD[0] is set to 1b1 and MRxx[2] is set to 1b0, then the training shall use LFSR pattern generator 
and the MRD8 to MRDA registers allow users to select LFSR Seed register UI23-0. These LFSR seed /user pattern, 
inversion registers are per PSD and common for both Write training mode and Host receiver training modes.
NOTE: For the 2p3 configuration, only DQ[2:0] from the LFSR output shall be used; all remaining DQ bits shall be ignored. For the 
1p6 configuration, only DQ[5:0] from PSD0 shall be used. For the 2p6 and 1p12 configurations, DQ[5:0] from PSD0 and DQ[11:6] 
from PSD1 shall be used.

4.2.14. DQ RX Offset Calibration Training
4.2.14.1. Offset Calibration Training Description
DDR6 SDRAM provides Offset Calibration training to reduce an intrinsic offset of DQ Rx. It is required for the 
DRAM to meet its receiver eye mask spec and required to operate the training every power-up and initialization 
training sequence to cope with the change of SDRAM operating condition. Offset calibration is enabled by 
MR05[4:0]=10100 and this can be issued in either normal mode or MRUPDATE Mode. When the SDRAM starts the 
training, DQ channel, where all the DRAM dies sharing the DQ, can be floated or can be biased toward VSS. DQ ODTs 
shall be automatically enabled to values determined by the SDRAM to be suitable for proper operation after 
MR05[4:0]=10100 is issued for starting an Offset calibration training.
Calibration will be performed by the DDR6 SDRAM automatically within the time interval, tOSCAL, and the calibra-
tion results will also be automatically updated within tOSCAL. In any case, DQ operations are disallowed during the 
DQ RX offset calibration. After calibration is done, offset calibration is disabled by MR05[4:0]=0h as this calibration 
is not self-clearing.
To reset the offset calibration to a default value, the Rx offset reset MR bit (OCC RESET) needs to be asserted 
(MR13[7]=1). In any case, operations that initiate DQ operations are always disallowed during tMRD after MR13[7] 
asserted. The OCC RESET MR bit shall be reset to 0b by the DRAM after tMRD.
4.2.14.2. Offset Calibration Training Sequence
Below is DQ RX Offset Calibration Training sequence.
1)
Issue a Rx Offset Cal. Start (MR05[4:0]=10100) for starting a DQ RX Offset Calibration Training
2)
Wait tOSCAL until the DDR6 SDRAM completes the Offset Calibration
3)
Issue a Rx Offset Cal. Exit (MR05[4:0]=0h) for exiting a DQ RX Offset Calibration Training
Figure 64 — DQ RX Offset Calibration Training Timing

4.2.15. Read Preamble Training
Read preamble training supports read leveling of the host receiver timings. This mode supports MRR transactions that 
access the Read Training Pattern and cannot be used during any other data transactions. Just like Read Training Pattern, 
Read Preamble Training needs to be entered with CRC disabled. Read preamble training changes the read strobe 
behavior such that the strobes are always driven by the DRAM, and only toggle during a MR programmed preamble, 
postamble and the actual burst of the read data. This mode enables the host to detect the timing of when the first data 
and associated strobe is returned after a read command.
4.2.15.1. Entry and Exit for Preamble Training Mode
The DRAM enters Read Preamble Training Mode by setting MR05:OP[4:0] = 10011B using MRW command. Read 
Preamble Training Mode is exited by setting MR05:OP[7:0] = 0. Read Preamble Training should not be disturbed by 
an ACT command until the completion of the training.
4.2.15.2. Preamble Training Mode Operation
Once the DRAM is placed in Read Preamble Training Mode, the only data transactions supported are MRR 
commands. All non-data commands, such as MRW, are still supported in this mode. Once READ Preamble Training 
is enabled, the device will drive DQS_t LOW and DQS_c HIGH within tSDOn and remain at these levels until an 
MRR command is issued.
During read preamble training, a programmed DQS preamble/postamble setting will be used. Once the MRR 
command is issued to MRyz, the device will drive DQS_t/DQS_c after CL-tRPRE RL-tRPRE-(Read_D-
QS_Pre-shift),where tRPRE is programmed in MR0C:OP[4:2] register, like a normal READ burst with the Read DQS 
pre-shift setting MR0C:OP[1:0] programmed in MR0C applied. In response to the MRR to the designated Read 
Training Pattern Address, the device must also drive the DQ pattern as per the Read Pattern configuration while in this 
mode. The MRR commands may be sequenced to enable back to back bursts on the DQ bus. Read preamble training 
mode is exited within tSDOff after setting mode register MR05:OP[7:0] = 0.
Figure 1 illustrates the timing of the strobe being driven differentially low after Read Preamble Training Mode is 
enabled. It includes the strobe timing sequence following a MRR command issued to access the Read Training Pattern. 
The sequence shows a programmed preamble/postamble consisting of 2tCK static-low followed by 2tCK toggling 
(MR0C: OP[4:2] = 001), and a programmed 0.5tCK postamble (MR0C: OP[7:6] = 00). Table 1 specifies the timing 
requirements for programmed preamble/postamble (2tck static low followed by 2tck toggle preamble and 0.5tck 
postamble) applicable to Read Preamble Training Mode.
Figure 65 — Timing Diagram for Read Preamble Training Mode Entry, Pattern

4.2.16. Read Duty Cycle Adjuster (DCA)
DDR6 SDRAM supports Mode Register-adjustable Read (RD) DCA to allow the memory controller to adjust the 
DRAM’s internally generated read clock tree and DQ duty cycle to compensate for systemic duty cycle errors of all 
DQS and DQs.
The RD DQS DCA is located in the read clock tree (or equivalent place), following the clock synchronization phase 
correction scheme.
The controller can adjust the duty cycle through all the DCA mode registers and can determine the optimal Mode 
Register setting for DCA in multiple different ways.
In case of 4-phase internal clocks, for example, since QCLK(90˚)/IBCLK(180˚)/QBCLK(270˚) are adjusted based on 
ICLK(0˚), the controller can first confirm that the first BL is synchronized with ICLK(0˚) through clock sync., and 
then perform the full DCA training operation which needs to have an even number of MRR(or Read) - MRR(or Read) 
timing to avoid confusion whether the first BL is synchronized with ICLK(0˚) or IBCLK(180˚).
4.2.16.1. Duty Cycle Adjuster Range
The global DCA is applied for all DQS and DQs within the entire PSD of the DRAM die. The step range between the 
step 0 to 7(-7) is as follows. The difference of actual value between step N and step N+1 is defined to be within a spec 
range, since the variation of duty cycle by changing DCA code is not linear.
Figure 66 — Read Duty Cycle Adjuster Range

4.2.16.2. Relationship between DCA Code Change and Single/Two-phase Internal 
Clock(s)/DQS Timing
In case of the read clock tree used single/two-phase clock(s) scheme, the duty-cycle ratio of the DQS can be adjusted 
directly according to the internal clock(s) controlled by the DCA code. Note that tDQSCK is not changed by DCA 
code change.
Using a 2-phase clock scheme, the rising edge of the 0° clock is the reference edge, while the 180° clock is adjusted 
based on 0° clock. The rising edge of 0° clock is for even burst bit data, and the rising edge of 180° clock is for odd 
burst bit data.
The global DCA adjustment uses the “RD DCA for single/two-phase clock(s)” mode register bits, MR40:OP[7:4]. A 
positive DCA adjustment results in a larger duty cycle ratio, while a negative DCA adjustment results in a smaller duty 
cycle ratio.
In addition to the global DCA adjustment, a RD per-pin DCA adjustment allows an additional step range of -3 to +3, 
per DQS/DQ. The per-pin DCA adjustment is additive to the global DCA adjustment, as shown in Table 2.
Like the global DCA adjustment, the actual step size for the per-pin DCA adjustment cannot be defined since the vari-
ation of duty cycle by changing DCA code is not linear, however the per-pin DCA adjustment will be approximately 
the same as the global DCA adjustment.
Table 65 — Read DCA Range Examples (Not All Possible Combinations)
Global DCA Adjustment
Per-Pin DCA Adjustment
Total DCA Adjustment at Pin
DCA Step -3
DCA Step -3
DCA Step -6
DCA Step -2
DCA Step +2
DCA Step 0
DCA Step +2
DCA Step -3
DCA Step -1
DCA Step +7
DCA Step +2
DCA Step 9

4.2.17. Write DQ Training Mode (WTM)
4.2.17.1. WTM Introduction
Initial DQ link training is critical with respect to the link performance with multiple modes to enhance IO scaling and 
training time in system. DDR6 DRAM requires pattern capability, better pattern type that increases robustness for 
signal integrity interactions. The Write Training Mode provides the ability to select two pattern types with and reports 
error count on a per-pin for system level optimization. The two pattern types are LFSR independent per-pin and user 
pattern.
4.2.17.2. WTM pattern
WTM can select the pattern type between clockuser patternvs LFSR seed with MR setting (MRDE). The LFSR mode 
provides an independent (different pattern) per lane using a single 24-bit Fibonacci LFSR per Pseudo Split Die (PSD). 
The polynomial for the Fibonacci LFSR is x24 + x20 + x11+ x5 + 1. The user (Ex: Clock) pattern would operate at the 
DQ rate for another option for system optimization on drift terms like Rx DQS2DQ. Each mode provides a per pin 
error count logging. Figure 1 shows the details about left shift Fibonacci LFSR circuit for (X != Y), (Tap[X] XOR 


=== PDF PAGE 153 ===
Page of 136
TRAINING
JEDEC PROPOSEDINITIALIZATION AND TRAINING
PROPOSED
Tap[Y]) produces Tap[Z] from same polynomial, whereas X and Y are usually < 24, Z is a large integer (1 to 224-1) 
and random looking across (X,Y) combos. Figure 2 shows the Fibonacci LFSR-24 pattern details.
Figure 71 — Fibonacci LFSR 24-bit circuit
Figure 72 — Fibonacci LFSR 24-bit pattern details
4.2.17.3. PRBS Pattern Generator for WTM
PRBS generator will start with the seed (Example 24b LFSR Seed: F7410B) and the below LFSR pattern table shows 
the example of shift mode where each DQ lanes gets different pattern on the lane. The upper 5 extra staging bits getting 
reset to 1, hence, users shall only independently program the LFSR seed [23:5] and need to calculate LFSR seed [4:0]

to correctly initialize the LFSR. Table X illustrates the mapping of the pattern to DQ [5:0]. Refer to LFSR section x.x.x

4.2.17.4. Entry and Exit for Write Training Mode (WTM)
To enter Write Training Mode (WTM), the host memory controller shall issue a Mode Register Write (MRW) 
command targeting MR05 OP[4:0] = 10001B, with the Write Training Mode entry opcode. When the DRAM is in 
Write Training mode, commands are still actively processed.
To exit Write Training Mode, the host memory controller shall issue an MRW command to MR5 OP[7:0] = 
00000000B, with the Write Training Mode Exit opcode.
4.2.17.5. Write Training Mode (WTM) Operation
The host enters WTM using MRW command and follows the sequence below.
1)
Perform MRW to enter WTM
2)
Host to perform MRW to pick LFSR (24-bit) or User pattern
3)
Host to program LFSR seed settings
4)
For each unique value of the host parameters,
5)
Issue a series of Write bursts (BL 24), with either LFSR or User pattern as shown in Figure 3.
6)
DRAM to internally check LFSR pattern per DQ lane
7)
DRAM to log error status/error counts on a per lane basis
8)
Host to read out the error logs through MRRs
9)
MRR to MRR command timing during Write DQ Training Mode shall use tMRR_WTM timing parameter as 
described in Mode Register Read section x.x.y.z.
10)
Clear the error status
11)
Repeat the above steps for the next value/parameter.
12)
Perform MRW to exit WTM
In this mode the only valid commands are WRITE, MRR and MRW.
Figure 73 — Write DQ Training Mode Timing

An independent 24-bit Fibonacci LFSR generator provides patterns for up to 6 DQ lanes. The source of the DQ output 
pattern shall be selected via MRDE:OP[0], where this field selects between LFSR output and the user-defined pattern 
resource. The MRDE[3] field shall be used to reset the LFSR seed value and associated staging flip-flops. Upon reset, 
the 5-bit staging flip-flops shall be initialized to their default value of 11111.
4.2.17.6. Error Logging
Each DQ lane shall implement phase error status registers to report WTM phase error information. DRAM shall allo-
cate four Mode Registers (MRDF, MRE0, MRE1, and MRE2) for each DQ lane to report either consolidated even/odd 
phase errors or individual 4-phase errors, as illustrated in Figure 4.
The WTM error reporting mode shall be selected through a Mode Register Write (MRW) to MRDE:OP[1], which 
determines whether the error status is reported as even/odd phase errors or as individual 4-phase errors.
Even/Odd Error Reporting: MRDE: OP[1] = 0b
The DRAM shall consolidate phase error information as follows:
•
Even phase errors: MRDF and MRE0 are combined and reported through MRDF.
•
Odd phase errors: MRE1 and MRE2 are combined and reported through MRE1.
Error status shall indicate the presence of any error in the following UIs:

4.2.18. Write Duty Cycle Adjuster (DCA)
DDR6 SDRAM supports a mode-register-adjustable Write(WR) DCA feature that enables the memory controller to 
adjust the DRAM's internal WR DQS tree duty cycle, enhancing DRAM WR margin. The DQS is shared across 
different PSDs(Pseudo Split Die). Therefore, the DCA code is determined based on the optimal value for all DQs 
within the DRAM die.
The memory controller can adjust the DQS duty cycle through the WR DCA mode registers and can determine the 
optimal Mode Register setting for WR DCA. This function adjusts the SDRAM internal DQS duty cycle (Static).
4.2.18.1. Duty Cycle Adjuster Range
The step range between the step 0 to 7(-7) is as follows. The difference of actual value between step N and step N+1 
is defined to be within a spec range, since the variation of duty cycle by changing DCA code is not linear.
Figure 74 — Write Duty Cycle Adjuster Range
4.2.18.2. The Relationship between DCA Code Change and Single/Two-phase Internal DQS 
Timing
In case of the DQS tree used single/two-phase DQS scheme, the duty-cycle ratio of all DQS per device can be adjusted 
directly according to the internal DQS controlled by the DCA code.
Using a 2-phase DQS scheme, the rising edge of the 0° internal DQS is the reference edge, while the 180° internal 
DQS is adjusted based on 0° internal DQS.
The WR DCA adjustment uses the “DCA for single/two-phase DQS” mode register bits, MR3E:OP[7:4]. A positive 
DCA adjustment results in a larger duty cycle ratio, while a negative DCA adjustment results in a smaller duty cycle 
ratio.

MODE REGISTER (MR) NAMING CONVENTION
JEDEC PROPOSEDMODE REGISTERS (MRS)
PROPOSED
6. Mode Registers (MRs)
6.1. Mode Register (MR) Naming Convention
Mode Registers (MRs) are arranged as Direct-Mapped Shared Mode Registers and Paged PSD Mode Registers. 
Direct-Mapped Shared MRs can be accessed (written to and read from) both PSD interfaces. Paged PSD MRs can only 
be accessed from the respective PSD interface in 2pX configurations. PSD MRs can be accessed using control bits in 
1pX configurations, as described in this section, the MRUPDATE section, and the Command Truth Table.
The Direct-Mapped Shared and Paged PSD Mode Register spaces contain up to 128 Mode Registers each, selectable 
by seven Mode Register Addresses, MRA[6:0]. MRA[7] selects between the Direct-Mapped Shared and Paged PSD 
spaces, with MRA[7]=0 for Direct-Mapped Shared MRs and MRA[7]=1 for Page PSD MRs. The Paged Mode 
Register space may contain more than one page, which are selectable by MR00[1:0] if defined. The default page is 
zero (0) for both PSDs.
Each Mode Register contains a byte-wide payload. All unused (RV) bits in a defined Mode Register and all undefined 
(RV) Mode Registers will be read as a zero (0).
6.2. Mode Register Attributes
Mode registers can have the following attributes, which are shown in the mode register definition tables.
Table 89 — Mode Register Naming Convention
 Direct-Mapped Shared and PSD Mode Registers
 Paged Mode Registers

Where: 
•
xx = MR address (using leading zero where 
applicable)
•

•
ww,xx = Selected MR addresses
•

•

•
y,z = Selected bits within the MR address
 Where: 
•

•
b:a = Page range
•
b,a = Selected pages
•

addresses within the page
•
y = Single bit, bit range, or selected bits within 
the paged MR address

Mode Register definition tables must follow these rules to ensure they are machine-readable: 
•
In the Attribute column, commas must be used to separate attributes.
•
In the Default column, “0” and “1” may be used for single-bit values. For all other widths, hex values must be 
used with an “h” suffix, as in “3h” or “1Ah”.
•
In the Description column, names of individual Mode Registers must be in all caps, using underscores in place 
of spaces, following the address, bit range, and a colon.
•
The address in the Description column must not use brackets around the page number, and the page number 
uses two digits with leading zero. This is the only place where these brackets are not used for the page number.
6.3. Mode Register Access
Shared and PSD Mode Registers access differs based on DRAM configuration.
Read Only
RO
This Mode Register can be read by software. Writes (MRWs) have no 
effect.
Reserved
RV
These bits are reserved for future expansion. They may not be physically 
implemented and shall read zero to ensure forward and backward 
compatibility.
 Sticky
 S
 This Mode Register is reset to the default only by power cycle, not Reset. 
This attribute can be combined with RW.

MODE REGISTER FUNCTIONAL MODES
JEDEC PROPOSEDMODE REGISTERS (MRS)

Figure 81 — 1pX PSD Right Connected Mode Register Access
6.4. Mode Register Functional Modes
Functions or modes (MR Functional Modes) to be executed are defined by a decoded combination of MR05 [7:0] and 
MR06, as shown in the MR05 and MR06 description s. The target Functional Mode is entered or executed within 
tMRD after an MRW or the last cycle of an MRUPDATE sequence. The MR05 description will also indicate if a Func-
tional Mode can only be enabled while in MRUPDATE Mode.
Functional Modes may be self-clearing or may require to be explicitly disabled, as indicated in the MR05 description. 
MR05 and MR06 is a are RW register s, allowing the state of the function to be read by an MRR command, if the MRR 
command is permitted while the Functional Mode is executing. If a Functional Mode is self-clearing, the MRR will 
return to 00h upon completion. Setting MR05[7:0]=00h or MR06[7:0]=00h will disable a the respective Functional 
Mode, unless it is self-clearing.
Unless explicitly stated that overlap of a self-clearing function by another function is permitted, only one Functional 
Mode may be entered/executed at a time.

6.6.1.6. MR05 - Functional Modes
2:0
R
MR04[2:0]: REFRESH_RATE
Average interval between refresh commands sent 
by the Host to the DRAM
000: Reserved
001: tREFI (1x Refresh Rate), Temperature<75°C 
nominal
010: tREFI (1x Refresh Rate), 75<Tempera-
ture≤80°C nominal
011: tREFI (1x Refresh Rate), 80<Tempera-
ture≤85°C nominal
100: tREFI/2 (2x Refresh Rate), 85<Tempera-
ture≤90°C nominal
101: tREFI/2 (2x Refresh Rate), 90<Tempera-
ture≤95°C nominal
110: tREFI/2 (2x Refresh Rate), Temperature>95°C 
nominal
111: Reserved
NOTE: The two PSDs (PSD 0 and PSD 1) on a single die share the same thermal sensor, therefore the thermal offset value is 
applied to the common thermal sensor.

6.6.1.13. MR0C - Read DQS Preamble
6.6.1.14. MR0D - Read DQS Preamble
3:0
RW
0h
MR0B[3:0]: WR_PRE_LENGTH
Write DQS Preamble (PRE) length
0000: 4*tCK static, 2*tCK toggle
0001: 4*tCK static, 4*tCK toggle
0010-1111: Reserved
1,2,3,4
NOTE 1: There are two physical registers assigned to each bit of this MR parameter, designated FSP0 and FSP1. Only the 
registers for the set point determined by the state of the FSP-WR bit (MR00[2]) will be written with an MRW command to this 
MR address.
NOTE 2: There are two physical registers assigned to each bit of this MR parameter, designated FSP0 and FSP1. The device 
will operate only according to the values stored in the registers for the active set point, i.e., the set point determined by the state 
of the FSP-OP bit (MR00[3]). The values in the registers for the inactive set point will be ignored by the device, and may be 
changed without affecting device operation.
NOTE 3: MR0B[3:0]=0001b setting is allowed for lower than TBD Gbps operation.
NOTE 4: MR0B[3:0]=0000b setting is allowed for lower than TBD Gbps operation.
NOTE 5: MR0B[7:4]=0001b setting is allowed for lower than TBD Gbps operation.
NOTE 6: MR0B[7:4]=0000b setting is allowed for lower than TBD Gbps operation.

is given. It should be noted that the settings are not specifically linear in relationship to the vendor optimized setting, so the host 
can walk through all the provided options and use the setting that works best in their environment.

MR46[7:0]: RV
MR47
Bits
Attribute
Default
Description
Note
7:0
RW
0h
MR47[7:0]: DRAM_SCRATCH_PAD
This DRAM Scratch Pad Mode register is used by the host 
controller to read back Control Words from the RCD. 
Control Words are the RCD equivalent of the DRAM Mode 
registers. The DRAM implements MR47 as a simple 
read/write register, writable via an MRW to address MR47 
and readable via an MRR to address MR47.
Any value is valid.
1
NOTE 1: The contents of this register have no function in the DRAM. Details for this function can be found in the DDR5 
RCD01 specification.

MR82[3:0]: PDA_SELECT_ID
The Select ID is set by MRW or MRUPDATE sequence. 
Default setting is all DRAMs execute MRW/MRUPD. For 
all other encodings, the DRAM will execute MRW/MRUP-
DATE when Select ID = Enumeration ID, except to this 
Mode Register which ignores the PDA match and will 
always be executed.

NOTE: 3: Since CTLE circuits cannot be typically by-passed, a disable option is not provided. Instead, a vendor optimized setting 
is given. It should be noted that the settings are not specifically linear in relationship to the vendor optimized setting, so the host 
can walk through all the provided options and use the setting that works best in their environment.

DDR6 SCRATCH PAD MODE REGISTER INFORMATION
JEDEC PROPOSEDMODE REGISTERS (MRS)
PROPOSED
6.8. DDR6 Scratch Pad Mode Register Information
This DRAM Scratch Pad Mode register is used by the host controller to read back Control Words from the RCD. 
Control Words are the RCD equivalent of the DRAM Mode registers. The DRAM implements MR47 as a simple 
read/write register, writable via an MRW to address MR47 and readable via an MRR to address MR47.
MR47: DRAM Scratch Pad Mode register information

RCD01 specification.

COMMAND AND OPERATION
JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION
PROPOSED
NOTE 1: Commands are two (2), four (4) or six (6) clock cycles long in CA 1N mode. They are defined by the 
states of CS of clocks pairs C0/C1, C2/C3, C4/C5, and by the states of CA[54:0] of clocks C0, C1, C2, C3, C4, C5. 
CS is 2N pulse width and center aligned to clocks C0, C2 and C4. CS is “Don’t Care” at clocks C1, C3, and C5. In 
CA 2N mode, the CS shall operate identically to CA 1N mode and the command lengths shall be doubled relative 
to 1N mode, corresponding to four (4), eight (8) or twelve (12) clock cycles long.
NOTE 2: "V" means a defined high "H" or low "L" logic level. "X" means "Don't Care", which includes "H", "L" 
or  floated
NOTE 3: Bank Group addresses BG[3:0] and Bank address BA determine which bank is to be operated  upon. 
Pseudo Split Die "PSD" is for 1pX6 die configs for PSD die split designation. PSD=L indicates the given command 
shall be executed at the PSD whose CAs are connected to the host. PSD=H indicates the given command shall be 
executed at the other PSD whose CAs are not connected to the host. "Ignore" is supported on 2p3 die configuration 
only for Read-9 and Partial Write operation and used when Ignore feature is enabled 
(MR0E[0]=1MRxx:OP[y]=1B). Refer to section 7.9XX for more details about the Command Ignore Read-9 and 
Partial Write operation. When not used, the PSD/Ignore bit is "V"
NOTE 4: If C1:CA3 = "H", the command is All Bank PRECHARGE (PREab) and must be applied to all banks 
with Individual BGs and BA are "V". If CA3:C1 = "L", the command is Dual Bank and Quad Bank PRECHARGE 
(PREdb/qb) and it is applied to the bank group pair determined by BG[2:0] and BA
NOTE 5: Auto Precharge "AP" = "H" during WRITE or READ commands indicate that the DRAM will precharge 
the row in the bank associated with the WRITE or READ command automatically at the completion of the respec-
tive command
NOTE 6: CS shall be compliant to power down entry/exit timing requirements. Refer to Power Down Function  
and Timing Description
NOTE 7: All Bank REFRESH (REFab) command requires C3:CA[0] = "H" and Dual Bank and Quad Bank 
REFRESH (REFdb/qb) command requires C3:CA[0] = "L". REFab is applied to all banks with individual BGs and 
BA are "V". BG[2:0] and BA determine the bank group pair for Dual Bank and Quad Bank REFRESH (REFdb/qb) 
operation
NOTE 8: When the WRITE command (WR) is issued, CS for the target rank is asserted "H" at C0/C1 and 
de-asserted "L" at C2/C3. The non-target rank CS is asserted "H" during C0/C1 and C2/C3 to control the NT-ODT 
setting and distinguish target and non-target ranks
NOTE 9: When CA Parity Check Mode is enabled (MR01xx:OP[4y]=1B, the host generates a parity operand 
PAR1 based on CA[54:0] of C0 and C1 of the command, excluding the PAR1 bit. For commands longer than two 
cycles, the host generates a additional parity operand PAR2 based on CA[54:0] of C2 and C3 of a four cycle 
command or C2, C3, C4 and C5 of a six cycle command, excluding the PAR2 bit. When CA Parity Check Mode is 
enabled, "X" defined in the command truth table shall be replaced by "V", except for DES command, since the 
defined logic level (H or L) is required in the command input to compute the parity state.
NOTE 10: Setting ODT=H allows On Die Termination (ODT) to persist when the device is in Power Down Mode
NOTE 11: All Bank REFRESH MANAGEMENT (RFMab) command requires C3:CA[1:0] = "VH". Dual Bank 
and Quad Bank  REFRESH MANAGEMENT (RFMdb/qb) command requires C3:CA[1:0] = "HL". Per Bank 
REFRESH MANAGEMENT (RFMpb) command requires C3:CA[1:0] = "LL". RFMab is applied to all banks with 
individual BGs and BA are "V". RFMdb is applied to the bank group pair determined by BG[2:0] and BA with BG3 
= "V". RFMpb is applied to a single bank as specified by PSD (applicable only for 1pX die configs), BG[3:0] and 
BA
NOTE 12: CA5 is optionally supported by discretion of DRAM supplier to enable extra CA pin. CA5 transfers CID 
address inputs to the DRAM for the purpose of capacity expansion. MRxx[y]=1 indicates that CA5 is enabled by 
the DRAM and shall be properly driven by the host for CID address inputs.  When CA Parity Check Mode is 
enabled (MRxx:OP[y]=1B, the host generates a parity operand PAR1  based on CA[5:0] of C0 and C1 of the 
command, excluding the PAR1 bit. For commands longer than two cycles, the host generates a additional parity 
operand PAR2 based on CA[5:0] of C2 and C3 of a four cycle command or C2, C3, C4 and C5 of a six cycle 
command, excluding the PAR2 bit. When CA Parity Check Mode is enabled, "X" defined in the command truth 
table shall be replaced by "V", except for DES command, since the defined logic level (H or L) is required in the 
command input to compute the parity state. 
NOTE 13: The MCME command works with additional setup and hold time requirements beyond multi-cycle CS 
assertion for any time the status of CS, CA and CK alignment may be unknown. It is shown as a 4-cycle command 
in command truth table but the CA inputs must remain in the proper command state for tMC_Setup + tMC_CSPW 
+ tMC H ld F

7.1.2. DDR6 CA 2N Mode
2N Mode shall allow the system to provide increased setup and hold margin on the Command/Address (CA) bus. 1N 
Mode shall be the default operating mode for DDR6 SDRAM devices. MCME command is used to enter into 
multi-cycle CS MRUPDATE sequence. MRUPDATE sequence shall be used to change mode register MRxx:OP[x] 
to set 1N vs 2N mode of operation.
In 1N Mode, the DDR6 CA interface shall support command lengths of 6 cycles, 4 cycles, and 2 cycles. In 2N Mode, 
the duration of each supported command shall be doubled relative to 1N Mode, corresponding to 12 cycles, 8 cycles, 
and 4 cycles, respectively.
The Chip Select (CS) function shall operate identically in both 1N and 2N modes. The CS# pulse width shall be 2nCK 
in both modes.
7.1.2.1. Mode Registers
Function

Operand

0: 1N Mode 
(Default)
1: 2N Mode

COMMAND INPUT AND CLOCK SYNC

7.2. Command Input and Clock Sync
7.2.1. Command Input
Implementation of an internal 4-phase clock requires synchronization (sync) to enable the Host to start commands 
aligned to an even rising edge of the differential CK_t/CK_c external clock (CK). Internally, the four clock phases, 0°, 
90°, 180° and 270°, are represented by Q0, Q1, Q2 and Q3, respectively, where each internal phase period equals four 
external CK cycles. Commands are only registered on Q0 or Q2, with CS being 2N centered on Q0 or Q2, and the 
corresponding command/address (CA) bits registered per CK as needed based on the command duration.
The Host is responsible for labeling these quadruples during the CK toggle initiation period of CSTM or CK Sync 
Operation, prior to issuing commands (exceptions noted in the following section).
4-cycle commands associated with data input or output (i.e., WRITE, READ, and MRR) can only start on Q0.
The MCME command can be started on any internal clock phase.
Figure 82 — Command Input
7.2.2. Clock (CK) Operation
During operation, the internal clock phase must be synchronized to the Host clock phase to properly decode commands 
(this is the "normal" or "CK Sync" state). When the CK Sync state is off, DES and the MCME command are allowed, 
the MRUPDATE Mode sequence may be executed, and the CK Sync Operation may be performed.
7.2.3. CK Sync Off Operation
The CK Sync state is set to "off' by following conditions:
•
Full Power-Down entry, with or without Frequency Change
•
CK Sync Off Functional Mode
•
Parity Safe-State
Following an MCME command, the CK Sync Off Functional Mode is executed while in MRUPDATE Mode with 
MR05[7:0]=04h. After the tCKSync_Off delay is met, the CK Sync State will be “Sync Off”. The off state does not

impact MRUPDATE Mode. CK Sync Off function is a self-clearing function once the CK Sync Off operation has been 
performed, allowing other MR functional modes to be executed.
Figure 83 — CK Sync Off Function
The CK Sync Off Functional Mode is executed from one PSD, but the Sync Off affects both PSDs. Once in Sync Off, 
either PSD can be used to perform the CK Sync Operation, however the "other" PSD must remain unused until 
tCKSync is satisfied.
7.2.4. CK Sync Operation
Any time the internal clock phase alignment with the Host clock phase is unknown, a clock synchronization operation 
is required before issuing commands. CSTM can be used to perform this alignment, however a shorter CK Sync Oper-
ation is also provided when only the phase alignment is needed, including following MRUPDATE Mode exit when a 
CK Sync Off was issued.
The CK Sync Operation procedure is as follows:
•
If needed, enter MCME on either PSD. Issue CK Sync Off Functional Mode, MR05[7:0]=04h, and wait 
tCKSync_Off. Exit MUPDATE Mode.
•
Issue CA[5:0]=00h with CS low for tCKSync_CAPre to meet the CA bus stable at low time.
•
Issue CA[5:0]=00h with CS high for tCKSync_CSPre to ensure CS is latched to release clock gating for the CA 
buffers.
•
Keep CS high for tCKSync_CSPost while issuing a single (1) cycle with CA[5:0]=18h, followed by 
CA[5:0]=00h. The cycle of CA[5:0]=18h must align with desired Host 0° CK phase (Q0). When CA4 or CA3 
is captured as ‘H’, along with CA5 and CA[2:0] as "L", the internal clock is set to 0° to align with Host clock 
phase.

Since CA Parity is not used during the CK Sync Operation, and if there are any concerns about Host phase tracking, 
an example method to detect internal clocking phase misalignment utilizes the CA parity check function is provided. 
In this example, the Host can issue 3 consecutive NOP commands to detect if there is any phase misalignment when 
CA parity is enabled. An example is shown in the figure below, where the first (1st) NOP has CA3 “L” and CA4 “H” 
for C0 and C1, the second (2nd) NOP has both CA3 and CA4 “L” for C0 and C1, and the third (3rd) NOP has CA3 
“H” and CA4 “L” for C0 and C1. If the internal phase is 90° different from the Host, it will cause a CA parity fail (CA 
parity check must be enabled).

7.3. Row Operation
7.3.1. ACTIVATE Command
The ACTIVATE command is used to open (or activate) a row in a particular bank for a subsequent access. The value 
on the BG[3:0] selects the bankgroup; BA inputs selects the bank within the bankgroup, and the address provided on 
the appropriate CA pins for R[18:0] to select the row (see Command Truth Table). This row remains active (or open) 
until a precharge command is issued to that bank or a precharge all command is issued. A bank must be precharged 
before opening a different row in the same bank.
The ACTIVATE command is required to be applied before any READ or WRITE operation can be executed. The 
SDRAM can accept a READ command at tRCD_R after the ACTIVATE command is issued, and a WRITE command 
at tRCD_W (or tRCD_W2 for non read-modify-write [1p6, 2p6, 1p12]) after the ACTIVATE command is issued.
The bank active and precharge times are defined as tRAS and tRP respectively. The minimum time interval between 
ACTIVATE commands to the same bank is determined by the RAS cycle time of the SDRAM (tRC).
Bank-to-bank command timing for ACTIVATE commands uses two different timing parameters, depending on 
whether the banks are in the same or different bank group. tRRD_S (short) is used for timing between banks located 
in different bank groups. tRRD_L (long) is used for timing between banks located in the same bank group. Consecutive 
ACTIVATE commands, allowed to be issued at tRRD_S, are restricted to a maximum of four within the time period 
tFAW (four activate window).
NOTE: – The following diagrams are just examples and the intention is to show the CK clock to CA is center-aligned. Expectation 
is the DRAM will have a delay from CK input to CA sampling that will need to be aligned to the center of the CA bit.

Sequential ACTIVATE commands are restricted to a maximum of four (4) within a rolling window determined by the 
tFAW timing parameter.

ACT-ACT-ACT-REFdb is allowed within tFAW, but ACT-ACT-ACT-ACT-REFdb is not allowed.

7.3.2. Precharge Operation
7.3.2.1. PRECHARGE Command
The PRECHARGE command is used to deactivate the open row in a particular bank or the open row in all banks. The 
bank(s) shall be available for subsequent row activation a specified time (tRP) after the PRECHARGE command is 
issued. Once a bank has been precharged, it is in the idle state and must be activated prior to any READ or WRITE 
commands being issued to that bank. Issuing a PRECHARGE command to a bank in idle state is allowed—the classic 
case is some bank being in idle state when an All-Bank PRECHARGE command is issued. tRP period needs to be 
satisfied before issuing next commands such as Activate, Self-refresh Entry, and Refresh, even if the target bank of 
the PRECHARGE command is already in the idle state. The tRP period shall be determined by the last PRECHARGE 
command issued to the bank.
The precharge-to-precharge delay is defined by tPPD in the core timing tables. tPPD applies to any combination of 
PRECHARGE commands (PREpb, PREdb, PREqb, PREab). To ensure that DDR6 devices can meet the instantaneous 
current demands, the row-Precharge time for an all-bank Precharge (tRPab) is longer than the per-bank Precharge time 
(tRPpb).
7.3.2.2. PRECHARGE Command Modes
DDR6 will support three different PRECHARGE commands: Per-Bank PRECHARGE (PREpb), 
Dual-Bank(/Quad-Bank) PRECHARGE (PREdb/PREqb) and All-Bank PRECHARGE (PREab). See the Command 
Truth Table for Encodings.
The Per-Bank PRECHARGE command (PREpb) applies precharge to a specific bank defined by BA in a specific bank 
group defined by BG[3:0], while an All-Bank PRECHARGE applies precharge to all banks in all bank groups.
The Dual-Bank PRECHARGE command (PREdb) applies precharge to specific bank pairs, as defined in Table 1. The 
Quad-Bank PRECHARGE command (PREqb) is an extension of PREdb, applying precharge to two sets of bank pairs 
simultaneously. PREqb uses the same bank pair mappings as the REFdb command in Table 1, but it operates across 
both PSDs at once. This command is unique to 1p6 devices and replaces PREdb. Note: PREdb and PREqb follow the 
same bank pair mappings as the REFRESH commands.

7.3.2.3. Auto-Precharge Operation
When a READ or WRITE command is issued to the DDR6 SDRAM, the AP bit (CA3/C3) can be set to enable the 
activated bank to automatically begin the precharge operation at the earliest possible moment after the read or write 
operation.
If AP is LOW when the READ or WRITE command is issued, then the normal read or write operation is executed and 
the bank remains activated at the completion of the operation.
If AP is HIGH when the READ or WRITE command is issued, the Auto-Precharge function is engaged. This feature 
enables the precharge operation to be partially or completely hidden when performing a read or write operation (depen-
dent upon read or write latency), thus improving system performance for random data access. READ with 
Auto-Precharge or WRITE with Auto-Precharge commands may be issued after tRCD_R or tRCD_W, respectively, 
has been satisfied. The DRAM bases the internal tRAS on tWTP or tRTP in MRxx to schedule the internal Precharge. 
tRC needs to be satisfied prior to issuing subsequent ACTIVATE commands to the same bank. The following figure 
shows an example of Meeting internal tRAS timings.
Figure 93 — Example of Meeting Internal tRAS Timing Constraints
NOTE: DES commands are shown for ease of illustration; other commands may be valid at these times.

command to ACTIVATE command to the same bank is required to satisfy timings shown in following two figures. 
Note that the diagrams in the Precharge Operation sections are just examples and the intention is to show CK aligned

After a READ with Auto-Precharge command, an ACTIVATE command can be issued to the same bank if the 
following two conditions are both satisfied: 
1)
The RAS Precharge time (tRP) has been satisfied from the clock at which the internal precharge operation 
began, which is estimated as nRTP after the READ with Auto-Precharge command.
2)
The RAS cycle time (tRC) from the previous bank activation has been satisfied.
Figure 96 — READ with Auto-Precharge
 NOTE: DES commands are shown for ease of illustration; other commands may be valid at these times.
WRITE with Auto-Precharge
If AP is HIGH when a WRITE command is issued, the WRITE with Auto-Precharge function is engaged. The device 
starts an Auto-Precharge on the rising edge nWTP cycles after the completion of the write operation.

7.4. Read/Write Operations
7.4.1. READ Command
After a bank has been activated, the Read Operation causes DDR6 SDRAM to retrieve and output data stored in its 
array. The Read Operation is initiated by the Read Command during which the beginning column address and 
Bank/Bank group address for the data to be retrieved from the array is provided.
The data is driven by the SDRAM on its DQ pins CAS Read Latency (RL) cycles after the Read Command along with 
the proper waveform on the Read DQS (RDQS) outputs. Read Latency is defined and measured from final cycle of 
the Read command to the first effective rising DQS (excluding read preamble). DDR6 SDRAM requires a DQS 
post-amble after completing all read DQ bursts. The length of DQS post-amble can be set by mode register.
7.4.1.1. READ Timing
A read command is initiated with CS, and CA[4:0] asserted to the proper state at the rising and falling edges of CK, 
as defined by the Command Truth Table, Table XXX.
The read latency (RL) is defined from the 4th rising edge of the CK_t of the command that starts a read command to 
the rising edge of the CK_t from which (tDQS2offset + tDQS2DQo) is measured.
The first valid data is available of RL * tCK + (tDQss (Max) + tDQS2DQo) or RL * tCK + (tDQS2ss (Min) + 
tDQS2DQO) after the CK_t rising edge of a read command.
The DQ-data is valid for tQW (DQ output window) and the controller is required to periodically train its internal 
capture clock to stay centered in the tQW window to compensate for timing changes due to temperature and voltage 
variation. Burst data is captured by the controller on successive edges of DQS until the 24 bit data burst is complete. 
The DQS is required to remain active (toggling) for tRPST (DQS postamble) after the completion of the burst read.
Figure 98 — Burst Read Operation
NOTE: 1: DES commands are shown for ease of illustration; other commands may be valid at these times.

7.4.1.3. Example Read to Read Operation with the 2nd Read is 2tCK or later
When the delay between two Read commands is slightly larger than tCCD_S, the second Read command must be 
delayed to the Modulus 2tCK command slot. In the case of only a 2tCK delay, the SoC sends 2tCK worth of DQS 
toggles regardless of the PRE and POST options. When the delay between the two Reads is 4tCK or larger, the 
behavior depends on the selected PRE and POST options.
In Figure 4, tRPRE = 4tCK and tRPST = 0.5tCK fit within the 4tCK gap on the Read channel. If tRPRE = 6tCK and 
tRPST = 0.5tCK (not shown), the DRAM would toggle for the full 4tCK gap. Figure 4 illustrates a within-PSD 
example, but the same concept can be extended to PSD-to-PSD scenarios as well.
Figure 101 — Back to Back Read Operation with 2nd Read 4tCK later (Within 
PSD)
NOTE: 1: DES commands are shown for ease of illustration; other commands may be valid at these times.
NOTE: 2: tDQSoffset = 0ps for illustration
NOTE: 3: BL=24, tRPRE=4nCK, tRPST=2.5nCK(Toggle)
NOTE: 4: The DQ signal is shown as “Don’t Care” before the first Read data bit indicating DFE is disabled. When DFE is enabled, 
the DQ signal shall be low for a minimum of 8UI prior to the first Read data bit for proper DFE synchronization. If inter-amble is 
only 2tCK, the DRAM will drive the DQ signal low for 4UI prior to the first Read data bit for proper DFE.

7.4.1.5. Read DQS Preamble Postamble Pre-Shift
Read Preamble and Postamble
The Read DQS strobe for the SDRAM requires a preamble prior to the first latching edge (the rising edge of Read 
DQS_t with data "valid"), and it requires a postamble after the last latching edge. The preamble and postamble lengths 
are set via mode register writes (MRW).
Table 96 — MR0C Definition
Bits

Default
Description

toggle (default)
001: 2*tCK static, 4*tCK 
toggle

0h
MR0C[2:0]:RD_DQS_PS
Read DQS Pre-Shift (PS)
00: 0*tCK (default)
01: 2*tCK
10: 4*tCK
11: Reserved
1,4,5
NOTE 1: There are two physical registers assigned to each bit of this MR parameter, designated set point 0 and set 
point 1. Please refer to TBD  section for more information on FSP operation.
NOTE 2: MR0C[1:0] = 00 supported over all frequency ranges.
NOTE 3: MR0C[1:0] = 01/10 setting is allowed for greater than TBD Mbps operation. 
NOTE 4: MR0C[4:2] = 0000 supported over all frequency ranges.
NOTE 5: MR0C[4:2] = 0001/0010/0011 setting is allowed for greater than TBD Mbps operation.

7.4.1.6. DQ Preamble
The DQ Preamble is the defined state of the DQ bus right before the normal data packets are transfered in order to keep 
the bus stable for the DFE. In DDR6, this is defined as the amount time in clocks that the DQ bus is kept low. As like 
regular preambles, the DQ Preamble will not force Read or Write gaps when doing back to back transactions. DQ 
bursts will always take priority over DQ Preambles.
Read DQ Preamble
The number of cycles for Read DQ Preamble is configured via MRxx.
Figure 105 — Example: Consecutive READ Operation: tCCD=MIN+8 (8 
Clock Gap and Postambles=Toggle w/8UI Read DQ Preamble)

7.4.2. WRITE Command
After a bank is activated, the Write operation stores input data into the DDR6 SDRAM array. It begins with a Write 
command, which specifies the starting column address and the Bank/Bank Group address.
Data is driven onto the DQ inputs after CAS Write Latency (CWL) cycles from the Write command, accompanied by 
the appropriate DQS waveform. WL is measured from the last cycle of the Write command to the first valid rising edge 
of DQS (excluding the write preamble).
After all write bursts, DDR6 SDRAM requires a DQS postamble, whose length is programmable via a mode register.
7.4.2.1. WRITE Timing
A WRITE command is initiated with CS, and CA[4:0] asserted to the proper state at the rising and falling edges of CK, 
as defined by the Command Truth Table, Table XXXX. The write latency (WL) is defined from the 4th rising edge of 
the CK_t that starts a write command to the rising edge of the CK_t from which (tDQS2offset+ tDQS2D) is measured. 
The first valid “latching” edge of DQS is required to be driven WL * tCK + tDQss (Max) or WL * tCK -tDQss(Min) 
after the rising edge of CK_t that completes a write command.
The DDR6 SDRAM uses an un-matched DQS-DQ path for lower power, so the DQS is required to arrive at the 
SDRAM ball prior to the DQ signal by the amount of tDQS2DQi. The DQS is required to be trained to arrive at the 
DQ pad center-aligned with the DQ-data. The DQ-data is required to be held for tDIVW (data input valid window) 
and the DQS is required to be periodically trained to stay centered in the tDIVW window to compensate for timing 
changes due to temperature and voltage variation. Burst data is captured by the SDRAM on successive edges of DQS 
until the 24 data burst is complete. DQS is required to remain active for tWPST (DQS postamble) after the completion 
of the burst WRITE. Pin input timings are measured relative to the crosspoint of CK_t and CK_c.
Figure 107 — Burst Write Operation
NOTE: 1: DES commands are shown for ease of illustration; other commands may be valid at these times.

7.4.2.5. Write Operation followed by Read Operation
When a read command follows a write command, the minimum gap between two commands is defined as 
min(tWTR_S/L). tWTR_L (Same BG) / tWTR_S (Different BG) is measured from the 4th rising edge of the CK_t 
command that satisfies tWTR_L (Same BG) / tWTR_S (Different BG) respectively after a write command to the 
following read command.

7.4.2.6. DDR6 Write Preambles, Postambles
Write Preamble and Postamble
DDR6 SDRAM supports a programmable write pre-amble and post-amble. The Write DQS strobe for the SDRAM 
requires a pre-amble prior to the first latching edge (the rising edge of Write DQS_t with data "valid"), and it requires 
a post-amble after the last latching edge. The pre-amble and post-amble lengths are set via mode register writes 
(MRW).
Preamble/postamble behavior such as static (no-toggle), toggling or combination of static and toggle, and its length 
can be set by mode register.

NOTE 1: There are two physical registers assigned to each bit of this MR parameter, designated set point 0 and set 
point 1. Only the registers for the set point determined by the state of the FSP-WR bit (MRyy OP[a]) will be written 
with an MRW command to this MR address.
NOTE 2: There are two physical registers assigned to each bit of this MR parameter, designated set point 0 and set 
point 1. The device will operate only according to the values stored in the registers for the active set point, i.e., the 
set point determined by the state of the FSP-OP bit (MRyy OP[b]). The values in the registers for the inactive set 
point will be ignored by the device, and may be changed without affecting device operation.
NOTE 3: MRxx OP[2:0] = 001B setting is allowed for lower than TBD Mbps operation.
NOTE 4: MRxx OP[2:0] = 000B setting is allowed for lower than TBD Mbps operation.

NOTE 6: MRxx OP[4:3] = 00B setting is allowed for lower than TBD Mbps operation.

7.4.3. Interamble
The DQS strobe requires a preamble prior to the first latching edge (the rising edge of DQS_t with data valid) and a 
postamble after the last latching edge. The preamble and postamble options are set via MRW commands. Additionally, 
the postamble- and preamble-configured size does not force the host to add command gaps in the command interval 
just to satisfy postamble or preamble settings (that is, preamble = 4 tCK + postamble = 2.5tCK does not force tCCD 
+6).
Regardless of READ or WRITE interambles, any time the Postambles of one transaction would overlap the beginning 
of the next transactions preamble, the interamble will be composed of only full toggles.
7.4.3.1. Read Interamble Timing Diagrams
In READ-to-READ operations with tCCD = BL/2, postamble for the first command and preamble for the second 
command disappear to create consecutive DQS latching edges for seamless burst operations. In the case of 
READ-to-READ operations with a command interval of tCCD +1, tCCD +2, etc., the toggles will always continue if 
the postambles and preambles overlap.


=== PDF PAGE 294 ===
 Page 270 of 417
READ/WRITE OPERATIONS
JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION
PROPOSED
Static preambles will only be used if there is a gap that exceeds the sum of both selected preamble & postamble.

7.4.5. Metadata
7.4.5.1. Introduction
DDR6 SDRAM supports a Metadata feature for Write and Read operations. In this mode, the data and Metadata are 
written/read in parallel to/from the array. This feature supports the MD_OFF, MD_8b and MD_16b modes. The modes 
can be chosen by MRxx OP[y] at initialization. MD_16b refers to the provision of 16 bits of metadata for every 128b 
of data. In a 2p3 device, MD_8b refers to the provision of 4 bits of metadata for every 64b of data and MD_16b refers 
to the provision of 8 bits of metadata for every 64b of data. The metadata bits will be placed in the existing location of 
metadata as defined in the data packet for both the Read and Write operations
7.4.5.2. Description
The Metadata feature is implemented through carveout, where columns in the array are reserved for storing the meta-
data bits. The number of columns reserved will depend on the metadata mode being selected. The DDR6 SDRAM shall 
powerup in the Default Mode (MRxx OP[y:z] =00) mode. In the modes where metadata is enabled, the metadata bits 
are part of the codeword of the on-die ECC engine.
MD_OFF
In this mode, the entire addressable column space is available for storing data. This mode is selected by setting MRxx 
OP[y:z]=11.
MD_8b
In the MD_8b mode, the DDR6 SDRAM provides 8 bits of metadata for every 128 bits of data. This mode is selected 
by setting MRxx OP[y:z]=01.In this mode Column addresses C [5:0] =0x00-0x3B is available for data and access to 
the remaining column space is prohibited.
Table 104 — MRxx Decode of Metadata Modes
Mode
MRxx:OP[y]
MRxx:OP[z]
Notes

0
0
1
MD_8b
0
1

=== PDF PAGE 303 ===

JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION

MD_16b
In the MD_16b mode, the DDR6 SDRAM provides 16 bits of metadata for every 128 bits of data. This mode is selected 
by setting MRxx OP[y:z]=10.In this mode Column addresses C [5:0] =0x00-0x37 is available for data and access to 
the remaining column space is prohibited.
7.4.5.3. Metadata Mode Selection
The Metadata mode can be selected by setting the MRxx OP[y:z] to the required state at initialization.
Timing
When the metadata mode is toggled, a delay of tMDMode_Select_Delay is required, before any command is issued to 
the SDRAM.
Miscellaneous
Any background training shall be restarted. DDR6 SDRAM shall be in idle state when the Metadata mode is selected. 
The CA bus shall maintain a DESELECT state during tMDMode_Select_Delay
Upon the selection of the Metadata mode, the DDR6 SDRAM shall reset ACI Complete Flag (MRxx OP[y]=0) until 
a full array Activation Counter Initialization (ACI) is completed by the host to set the Activation counter bits to a 
known state.
7.4.5.4. Metadata Mode Enable Sequence
The DDR6 SDRAM shall powerup in a Default Mode as shown in Table 1.In the Default mode Activate commands 
shall not be issued to the DRAM and any issued Activates are illegal.Illegal Activates shall be blocked by the DRAM. 
Once a Metadata mode is selected by the user the Mode Register bits used in selecting the Metadata mode are locked 
and cannot be rewritten unless a RESET is issued. Activate Commands are enabled once tMDMode_Select Delay has 
elapsed.
Initialization Sequence Pertaining to Metadata
The following sequence pertaining to Metadata shall be used.
1)
DDR6 SDRAM powers up into the Default Mode.Activate Commands shall not be issued. Any issued Activates 
shall be blocked by the DDR6 SDRAM
2)
The Metadata Mode is selected and MRxx:OP[y:z] set to something other than Default Mode.This action locks 
the MRxx:OP[y:z] and prevents the MRxxOP[y:z] bits from being rewritten. The CA bus shall maintain a 
DESELECT state during the tMDMode_Select_Delay time
3)
Activate commands can be issued to the DRAM once tMDMode_Select_Delay has elapsed.
4)
ACI operation needs to be performed after the Metadata Mode selection
Table 106 — Metadata Mode Timing Parameters
Parameter
Symbol
Value
Unit

Mode change and next 
command

tINIT3

REFRESH OPERATION
JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION

7.5. Refresh Operation
7.5.1. Refresh Command
Two refresh commands are supported: All-bank REFRESH (REFab) and Dual-bank REFRESH (REFdb). These 
commands are available for 2p3 and 2p6 configuration devices. For 1p6 configuration devices, the Dual-bank 
REFRESH command becomes a Quad-bank REFRESH (REFqb) command.
An All-bank REFRESH command (REFab) refreshes one or more rows in all banks as indicated by the internal row 
refresh counter. A "refresh" to a row means the row is activated, all bits on that row are sensed and restored (refreshed) 
to the sensed state, and the row is precharged.
A Dual-bank REFRESH command (REFdb) refreshes one or more rows in two banks as indicated by bank group 
addresses BG[2:0] and the bank address BA.
A Dual-bank REFRESH command (REFdb) can be issued in any BG[2:0] and BA order, with the internal bank counter 
being incremented with each REFdb command. The order of REFdb commands may be sequential round robin: 
(0,16)-(1,17)-(2,18)-(3,19)-(4,20)-(5,21)-(6,22)-(7,23)-(8,24)-(9,25)-(10,26)-(11,27)-(12,28)-(13,29)-(14,30)-(15,31)
. The order of REFdb commands may be random, for example, the REFdb commands may be issued in the following 
order: 
(0,16)-(9,25)-(8,24)-(2,18)-(1,17)-(10,26)-(3,19)-(12,28)-(4,20)-(11,27)-(13,29)-(6,22)-(5,21)-(15,31)-(7,23)-(14,30)
. After all banks have been refreshed using REFdb commands, the internal bank counter is reset or rolls over to 0, and 
the internal row refresh counter will be incremented. The controller can send another set of REFdb commands in the 
same order or a different order. It is illegal to send a REFdb command to the same bank unless all other banks have 
been refreshed using the REFdb command. The bank counter starts with the first REFdb command after a synchroni-
zation event.
A Quad-bank REFRESH command (REFqb) has the same behavior as the REFdb command, but is performed on the 
same two banks in both PSDs for a single REFqb command.
The bank counter is synchronized between the controller and DRAM by resetting it to 0. Resetting occurs upon issuing 
a RESET_n or at every exit from Self-Refresh. The REFab command also is a counter synchronization event and resets 
the bank counter to 0. Entering Self-Refresh or issuing a REFab command can be done at any valid time without 
cycling through all banks using the REFdb (or REFqb) command. After the bank counter is synchronized, the 
controller can issue REFdb (or REFqb) commands in any order as described in the previous paragraphs.
A REFab command issued when the bank counter is not zero will reset the bank counter to 0 and refreshes are 
performed to all banks as needed and as indicated by the internal row refresh counter. If another refresh command 
(REFab or REFdb or REFqb) is issued after the REFab command, then it uses an incremented value of the internal row 
refresh counter. The table below shows examples of both bank and refresh counter increment behavior.

•

the bank counter to 0. The REFab command must not be issued to the device until the following conditions have been 
met:
•
tRFCab has been satisfied following the prior REFab command.
•
tRFCdb (or tRFCqb) has been satisfied following the prior REFdb (or REFqb) command.
•
tRP has been satisfied following the prior PRECHARGE command.
When an all-bank refresh cycle has completed, all banks will be idle. After issuing REFab:
•
tRFCab must be satisfied before issuing an ACTIVATE command.
•
tRFCab must be satisfied before issuing a REFab or REFdb or REFqb command.

The maximum average refresh interval (tREFI) requirement depends on the device’s junction temperature (Tj), as indi-
cated by bits MR4:OP[4:0]. When REFab commands are issued and the temperature is Tj ≤ 85°C, the maximum 
average refresh interval is tREFI. When REFab commands are issued and the temperature is 85°C < Tj ≤ 95°C, the 
maximum average refresh interval is tREFI/2.
The device includes a method for the host to indicate when REFRESH commands are being issued at the tREFI/2 
refresh interval rate. The Refresh Interval Rate (RIR) indicator bit, MR4:OP[5], can be set if the host supports the 
refresh interval rate indication as part of the REFRESH command using C2:CA4. When enabled (MR4:OP[5]=1), the 
host issues REFab commands at tREFI with C2:CA4=L when Tj ≤ 85°C, and the host issues REFab commands at 
tREFI/2 with C2:CA4=H (Tj any allowable temperature).
When issuing Dual-bank REFRESH (REFdb) or Quad-bank REFRESH (REFqb) commands, the maximum average 
refresh interval is divided by 16 (tREFI or tREFI/2), depending on the MR4 temperature requirement.

tREFI is based on the 8192 REFRESH commands per bank that need to be issued within the baseline tREFW=32ms 
refresh period on the device.
In general, REFRESH commands need to be issued every tREFI interval. To allow for improved efficiency in sched-
uling and switching between tasks, some flexibility in the absolute refresh interval is provided. A maximum of two (2) 
REFRESH commands can be postponed during operation.
In the case that 2 REFRESH commands are postponed in a row, the resulting maximum interval between the 
surrounding REFRESH commands is limited to 3 x tREFI. At any given time, a maximum of 3 REFab commands can 
be issued within 1x tREFI. Self-Refresh may be entered with a maximum of 2 REFRESH commands being postponed. 
After exiting Self-Refresh with one or more REFRESH commands postponed, additional REFRESH commands may 
be postponed to the extent that the total number of postponed REFRESH commands (before and after the Self-Refresh) 
will never exceed 2. During Self-Refresh, the number of postponed REFRESH commands does not change.

7.5.2. Self-Refresh Operation
The Self-Refresh (SREF) command operation retains data in the device without external Refresh commands. While in 
SREF with Full Power-Down, the device retains data without external clockingDDR6 SDRAM even when the rest of 
the system is powered down and external clocking is disabled. The device has a built-in timer to accommodate the 
SREF operation. While in SREF, the device adjusts its internal average periodic refresh interval as needed, based on 
the on-die temperature sensor. The internal periodic refresh interval adjustment (increasing, decreasing or staying 
constant) does not require any external control.
The device enters Self-Refresh through the Self-Refresh Entry (SRE) command. SRE is issued by asserting CS for two 
consecutive clock cycles is a 2-cycle command with CA[2:0] = 000b during the first cycle and CA[2:0] = 100b during 
the second cycle. If the SRE command is issued with CA[3] HIGH during the second cycle, Self-Refresh is initiated 
with Full Power-Down. For 2p3 and 2p6 devices, a SRE command without Full Power-Down may be issued to each 
PSD independently. Before issuing the SRE command, the target PSD shall be idle with all banks precharged.
DDR6 SDRAM can operate in Self-Refresh across standard and elevated temperature ranges, managing its own power 
consumption as the operating temperature changes. For proper Self-Refresh operation, power supply pins (VDD, 
VDDQ, and VPP) shall remain at valid operating levels.
During Self-Refresh without Full Power-Down, all input pins of the PSD are activated and ODT states are maintained 
according to the state of the mode registers, as well as NT ODT. If not in Full Power-Down, the device may accept the 
following commands during Self-Refresh after tSRED is met: PDE, PDX, SRX, MRR, MRW, DES, NOP, and MCME 
(including MRUPDATE mode). All defined training modes in Chapter 4 are supported during Self-Refresh. Training 
modes must be properly exited and all required exit timing delays must be satisfied before issuing SRX. The minimum 
time that the device is required to remain in Self-Refresh is tSR.
The device exits Self-Refresh through the Self-Refresh Exit (SRX) command. SRX is issued by asserting CS for two 
consecutive clock cycles is a 2-cycle command with CA[2:0] = 000b during the first cycle and CA[2:0] = 110b during 
the second cycle. If SRX is issued while the device is not in Self-Refresh, the command will not execute. For 2p3 and 
2p6 devices, SRX shall be issued to each PSD independently. Once SRX is registered, only MRR, MRW, and MCME 
(including MRUPDATE mode) commands are allowed until tXSR is satisfied.
The use of Self-Refresh introduces the possibility that an internally timed Refresh event can be missed when 
Self-Refresh Exit is registered because entering and exiting Self-Refresh will reset the internal bank counter. Upon exit 
from Self-Refresh, at least one REFab or sixteen REFdb/REFqb commands shall be issued before entry into a subse-
quent Self-Refresh. Refer to the Refresh section when postponing or pulling in Refresh commands. This Refresh 
command does not count toward the tREFI-based periodic refresh requirement and does not modify the postponed or 


=== PDF PAGE 313 ===
Page of 417

PROPOSED
pulled-in refresh counts; the Refresh command does count toward the number of maximum refreshes permitted within 
2 × tREFI.
