7.5.2.1. Power-Down Entry and Exit during Self-Refresh
Entry to and exit from Full Power-Down mode is permitted while the device is in Self-Refresh. There are two ways to 
enter Full Power-Down during Self-Refresh: issue the SRE command with CA[3] HIGH during the second clock cycle 
or issue the Full Power-Down Entry (PDE) command after waiting tSRED following the SRE command. DuringFor 
Full Power-Down in Self-Refresh on 2p3 and 2p6 devices, the internal clock is stopped and Full Power-Down is 
entered after both PSDs have received the Full PDE command. Before Self-Refresh can be exited on the target PSD, 
the Power-Down Exit (PDX) command shall be issued and CK Sync shall be completed. The timing parameters and 
operation of Full Power-Down are defined in the Power-Down section.

7.5.3. Per Row Activation Counting (PRAC)
7.5.3.1. Introduction
Per Row Activation Counting (PRAC) monitors for row activity that may adversely affect the data stored in cells on 
neighboring rows within the DRAM array.
The intended implementation for PRAC is to add Activation Counter bits corresponding to every row in the DRAM. 
These bits store a count associated with the number of activations and the duration of the activations received by a row 
since the last time it was refreshed. Activations include any commands that result in an activation and precharge of a 
row.
7.5.3.2. Activation Counter Initialization (ACI)
Upon power-up or any time that device refresh requirements are violated, the Activation Counter bits may be in an 
unknown state, requiring initialization to put the bits into a known state.
Prior to use of the Activation Counter bits, a full array Activation Counter Initialization (ACI) must be performed. The 
DRAM will not track activation counts, nor issue an Alert Back-Off (ABO), until the Activation Counter bits are 
initialized.
To initialize the activation counter bits, the host sets MR4E35[0] = 1b to indicate to the device that a full array refresh 
will take place. Only REF, MRR, NOP and NT_ODT commands are permitted during initialization. Upon completion 
of a full refresh ACI cycle, the DRAM indicates completion by setting MR4E35[1] = 1b, which the host then follows 
by setting MR4E35[0] = 0b. The DRAM starts counting activations from that point forward and issues the ABO as 
necessary.
During ACI, the DRAM maintains refresh in the main array. If the host re-enters ACI by setting MR4E35[0] = 1b, the 
DRAM will reset MR4E35[1] = 0b until the initialization has been completed.
During ACI, the DRAM will perform refresh commands in the main array based on the ACI Main Array Refresh 
Control bit, MR34[4]. Performing an ACI with MR34[4]=0 (default) does not require the DRAM to refresh the main 
array, and any data previously written may be corrupted. Performing an ACI with MR34[4]=1 requires the DRAM to 
refresh the main array.
Like normal DRAM cells, the activation counter bits require refresh to maintain the stored values. Anytime refresh is 
violated during ACI or after ACI, the counter bits need set to a known state by repeating the ACI. A full array refresh 
will set counter bits to a known state, but ABOs may be generated during the refresh.
During hPPR and MBIST/mPPR, the system does not maintain refresh to the DRAM. Therefore, the host must perform 
a full array ACI before rewriting data following the hPPR and MBIST/mPPR operations. During sPPR, the system is 
required to maintain refresh to DRAM. However, before the sPPR operation, the DRAM is not required to maintain 
data integrity (refresh) for the row element designated to be used for the repair. tPGM_Exit timing provides sufficient 
time to the DRAM to update the activation count of the new row as well as proactively perform mitigation for neigh-
boring rows of the new row before completion of sPPR operation. Likewise for sPPR_Undo, the DRAM may update 
the activation count of the old row as well as proactively perform mitigation for neighboring rows of the old row before 
completion of sPPR operation.
7.5.3.3. Per Row Activation Counting Core Timing Parameters
PRAC requires additional counter cells per row in the DRAM to store Activation (ACT) command counts. Updating 
the Activation Counter cells requires a read-modify-write to the Activation Counter cells.


=== PDF PAGE 316 ===
 Page 292 of 417

An example of the timings affected by the updating of the activation counter bits is shown below. The “i” references 
DRAM internal commands, not external commands issued by the Host. In this example, the PRE command initiates 
an internal read (iRD) followed by an internal write (iWR) to increment the Activation Counting bits for all open rows 
affected by the PRE command. This is the Activation Counter Update (ACU), with the associated tACU timing.
Figure 133 — Example ACT-PRE with ACU
7.5.3.4. Per Row Activation Counting Response
Since the DRAM is updating row activation counts based on row activity, back-off action is required upon one or more 
rows reaching a counter threshold level or levels, or when registers holding row addresses for targeted refreshing 
reaches or passes a critical level. This back-off action is a request by the DRAM for the host to pause normal activity 
for a recovery period so that refresh management (RFM) commands or other functions can be performed. The back-off 
protocol is described in the next section.
The counter and/or register threshold level(s) may be changed (reduced) by setting the adaptive activation counting 
levels in MR4D34[3:2] to something other than the default MR4D34[3:2] = 00b. These adaptive levels are based on 
the default starting value. The threshold level(s) reductions are approximately level A = default - 10%, level B = default 
- 20%, and level C = default - 30%. Changing levels requires an ACI orwith full array refresh to set/reset all counters 
to a known state below critical threshold levels and to empty the registers.
In addition to the back-off which is required to prevent activation counts from exceeding the threshold level, the device 
should proactively perform targeted refreshes during Refresh Management (RFM) and/or Refresh (REF) commands 
to reduce and/or prevent the occurrence of the alert back-off being triggered. RFM commands must be issued either 
periodically or based on a bank activation count, as described below.
The host may implement either Periodic RFM (PRFM) or Bank Activation Threshold (BAT), but is not required to 
support both modes simultaneously. When BAT is used to ensure proper refresh management, Periodic RFM 
commands are not required. Likewise when BAT is not active, PRFM shall be used to ensure proper refresh manage-
ment.
7.5.3.5. Periodic Refresh Management (PRFM)
Periodic Refresh Management (PRFM) requires the host to send an RFM command to each bank within every tPRFMi 
window, with the maximum gap of 1.5*tPRFMi between each RFM command. RFMab, RFMdb and RFMpb 
commands are applicable, with RFMdb and RFMpb requiring the same time gap stated above on a per equivalent bank 
basis.
When using PRFM, time is partitioned into consecutive tPRFMi windows. The host shall issue at least one RFM 
command to each bank during each window. To allow scheduling flexibility across window boundaries, the maximum

allowable gap between RFM commands to the same bank is 1.5 × tPRFMi. To avoid a strictly deterministic refresh 
pattern, the host is encouraged to vary the timing of RFM commands within and across tPRFMi windows, such that 
RFM activity remains periodic, not fixed to a rigid schedule.
Since tPRFMi is a window, no more than the equivalent of 1 RFM command may be postponed.
All references to dual bank, REFdb or RFMdb apply to 2p3 and 2p6 configuration devices. For 1p6 configuration 
devices, all references to “dual bank” become “quad bank”, references to REFdb become REFqb, and references to 
“RFMdb” become “RFMqb”, with equivalent command separation and timings.
7.5.3.6. Bank Activation Threshold (BAT)
Bank Activation Threshold (BAT) requires that the host track the level of activity that occurs on a per-bank basis. 
When the bank activation threshold (BAT) is reached (shown below), a RFMab, RFMdb or RFMpb command is issued 
to the bank or banks at the BAT. Upon issuing an RFM command, any bank or banks receiving the command can have 
its BAT count decremented by the BAT threshold, with the result not less than zero (0).
Bank activation counting is done for every ACT/PRE combination, and additional counts are added by dividing the 
activation time by tRC or longer to account for long tRAS.
The duration of the RFM command (tRFM) is sufficient for the row refresh span of ±1 and ±2 physically adjacent rows 
and the target row to be refreshed. Each row refresh span is determined as tRRFab, tRRFdb and tRRFpb.

Similar to Refresh commands, a bank shall be idle before it can receive the RFM command.

an active state, or be accessed by a READ or a WRITE command. When the RFM command has completed, the 
affected bank(s) will be in the idle state.

7.5.3.7. Back-Off Protocol
To maintain data integrity, the DRAM may request the host to pause normal activity for a maintenance period where 
refresh management (RFM) commands or other functions may be performed. This is called a “back-off.”
7.5.3.8. Alert Back-Off Protocol
The back-off protocol uses the ALERT signal and mode register settings to provide a handshake between the DRAM 
and the host. This is the alert back-off (ABO).
7.5.3.9. PRAC-Triggered Alert Back-Off
Upon reaching critical threshold levels by one or more rows, or when registers holding addresses for targeted 
refreshing reaches or passes a critical level, the DRAM asserts ALERT and sets the ABO flag at MR4EC8[0] = 1b, 
indicating that ALERT was asserted due to additional refresh management (RFM) being required. All commands 
received by the device are executed while ALERT is asserted to maintain synchronization.

Commands that may trigger the ABO include PREab/PREdb/PREpb, WRA, RDA, REFab/REFdb, and 
RFMab/RFMdb/RFMpb. ALERT will be asserted after issuing the trigger command but before the max(5ns,10nCK) 
a maximum of 8ns following the completion of the respective command duration (e.g. PREab/PREdb/PREpb = tRP, 
REFab/REFdb = tRFC, RFMab/RFMdb/RFMpb = tRFM).
Since the PREpb required during hPPR/sPPR and the PREab during mPPR have different functions than closing a row, 
no counter bits will be incremented associated with the repaired row, therefore no ABO will be triggered in this 
instance.
7.5.3.10. Alert Back-Off
To instantiate the ABO, ALERT is asserted with a fixed duration of clock cycles as defined by tALERT_PW.
Figure 135 — ABO: MRFMaACT = 2 Case (MR4D34[1:0] = 01b)
After the ALERT is asserted, a bounded number of ACTIVATE (ACT) commands are permitted before the host starts 
the RFM-based recovery period. Issuing ACT commands along with normal traffic is allowed within the tABO_ACT 
window, however no additional ACT commands are allowed at the end of the tABO_ACT window until ABO_Re-
covery is complete. The host shall complete idling all banks within tREFI after the ALERT is asserted, which is 
followed by the recovery period (ABO_Recovery).
The recovery period begins when all banks have been idled in preparation for RFMab commands. The DRAM 
self-clears the ABO flag by setting MR4FC8[0]=0b by the end of the ABO_Recovery period. ABO_Recovery ends 
tRFMab after the last RFMab command or after tALERT_PW, whichever takes longer.
The only RFM command allowed during the ABO_Recovery period is RFMab. The duration of the RFMab command 
is described in the previous section (Per Row Activation Counting Response). The number of RFMab commands 
required to be issued per ABO maintenance period is defined by mode register ABO_RFM bits, MR4D34[1:0]. 
Systems that have stricter requirements for isochronous bandwidth may limit the number of RFMab commands by 
setting MR4D34[1:0] to a lower value.

If additional RFM commands are needed, the ABO protocol is restarted following the ABO delay, as described below.
During the ABO_Recovery period, a host can issue only RFMab, REFab/REFdb, PDE, PDX, SRE, 
PREab/PREdb/PREpb, NOP, NT_ODT, MRW (PRAC-related MR changes not allowed during ABO_Recovery) and 
MRR commands.
Refresh commands are issued to meet device refresh requirements, but they do not perform any of the required refresh 
management.
Power Down Entry and Exit (PDE, PDX) and Self Refresh Entry and Exit (SRE, SRX) are allowed during recovery 
period.
In Self Refresh, the device de-asserts ALERT if triggered by an ABO. Upon exiting Self Refresh, the device re-eval-
uates and issues a new ALERT if ABO is still needed. In power-down, the device keeps the current ALERT state.
7.5.3.11. Alert Back-Off Delays
ABO_Delay defines an interval in which the device services a minimum number of commands that may trigger an 
ABO before it can reassert ALERT. The ABO_Delay count is specified by MR34[1:0]. Adherence to the ABO_Delay 
count is set by the ABO_Delay Control bit, MR34[5], where the default MR34[5]=0 requires that ALERT will not be 
reasserted until the ABO_Delay count is satisfied, and MR34[5]=1 allows the ALERT to be reasserted as needed to 
maintain data integrity.
ABO_Delay is specified by the minimum number of commands in MR4D[1:0].
Commands that may trigger ALERT include PREab/PREdb/PREpb, WRA, RDA, REFab/REFdb, and 
RFMab/RFMdb/RFMpb and Manual ECS. Any of these commands that occur are included in the total ABO_Delay 
count allowed.
Following ABO_Delay, any of the commands included in the PRAC-Triggered Alert Back-Off section above may 
trigger the ABO, as well as an ACT command.
ALERT may be shared between sub-channels in a channel, but parameters associated with ABO (tABO_ACT, 
tALERT_PW, ABO_Recovery, ABO_Delay) are independent per sub-channel.

 Page 298 of 417

Figure 136 — Bank-to-Bank Alert Back-Off Delay
7.5.3.12. ALERT Priorities
At times, Write CRC, Alert Back-Off, and CA parity errors may occur simultaneously Alert Back-Off may occur 
simultaneously with other functions that also use ALERT, resulting in an overlap of ALERT assertions or the occur-
rence of the two events back-to-back (non-overlapped) may result in a short “glitch” on ALERT. For all events, the 
DRAM shall treat the ALERT as an “OR” function where ALERT is issued whenever an event occurs, and the DRAM 
sets the corresponding Write CRC or, Alert Back-Off (MR4FC8[0]), or CA parity error flag indicating which type of 
event, or possibly both multiple, occurred.
7.5.3.13. Activation Counter Errors
The DRAM has the capability to detect errors that occur within the counter bits, and the following protocol defines 
how the device reports the associated failing row address to the host.
If a counter bit error an Activation Counter Error (ACE) is detected, the device sets the Counter Error Flag, 
MR4FC8[1] = 1b, if not previously set, and. While the Counter Error Flag is set, the counter function for the specific 
row with the error is disabled. The DRAM does not assert the ABO to request RFM commands for errors associated 
with that row. All other rows without counter errors continue to count activity and assert ALERT, if necessary.
During ECS, if the Counter Error Flag is set and the DRAM logs a failing row address, the DRAM will update 
MR50-MR53C9-MRCB with the failing row address and set the Counter Address Report Flag, MR4FC8[2]=1b, when 
the ECS reaches the failing row. Only the first failing row will be reported. Additional rows will be reported in a subse-
quent scrub if they continue to fail after the Counter Address Report Flag is cleared by the host.
A counter RAS error that occurs during the ECS may still trigger the counter error flag, but may not set the counter 
address report flag and report the failing row address.
The host clears the counter error flag and counter address report flag, once the necessary information about the failing 
row has been collected and mitigative actions have been taken.

7.5.3.14. Per Row Activation Counting Testing
Per Row Activation Counting (PRAC) testing is activated by setting MR4E35[2] = 1b. Once enabled, the counter for 
a target row to be tested is initialized by the host setting MR4E35[3] = 1b, followed by an ACT/PRE to the target row. 
The host then sets MR4E35[3] = 0b and issues 32 activations or fewer to the target row or rows to trigger the ABO 
(multiple increments to the counter bits may occur depending on tRAS duration, mimicking normal operations). ABO 
is required as described previously before testing subsequent rows.
Depending on counter bit starting values, all DRAMs in the package or on a DIMM devices may not trigger the ABO 
at the same time (detected by MR4FC8[0]); however, all devices will trigger within 32 activations.
After PRAC testing is complete, the test mode is cleared by the Host setting MR4E35[2] = 0b. Since the counter cells 
will be in an unknown state following testing, a full ACI is required before resuming normal operations.
Refresh commands are not allowed while PRAC Testing is enabled. Data in normal cells are not maintained during in 
PRAC testing.
7.5.3.15. Alert Verification
ALERT verification is triggered by setting MR4E35[4] = 1b. The DRAM asserts ALERT and sets the ABO flag at 
MR4FC8[0] = 1b within tMRD after the MRW and will keep ALERT asserted for tALERT_PW. The host should 
follow all normal ABO procedures for clearing the ALERT. MR4E35[4] and MR4FC8[0] will be self-cleared by the 
device prior to the end of the ABO_Recovery period.
Data in normal cells are maintained during ALERT verification, as long as refresh is maintained.
7.5.3.16. Mode Registers
NOTE: Self-clearing mode register bits (MR35[4], MRC8[0]) are automatically reset by the DRAM and do not require 
host intervention to clear.

POWER-DOWN MODE

7.6. Power-down mode
7.6.1. Types of Power-Down modes
DDR6 SDRAM supports three power-down modes: Full Power-Down (Full-PD), Full Power-Down with Frequency 
Change (Full-PD FC), and ODT Power-Down (ODT-PD).
•
Full-PD : In this mode, power consumption is minimized by disabling both the CK and CA input buffers.
•
ODT-PD : In this mode, only the CA input buffers are disabled, while the CK buffer remains active. This config-
uration allows the DDR6 to support the Write NT-ODT command.
7.6.2. Power-Down Entry and Exit
Full-PDis entered when the PDE command is registered with C0:CA4=L. Full-PD with Frequency change is entered 
when the PDE command is registered with C0:CA4=Land C1:CA3=H. ODT-PDis entered when the PDE command 
is registered with C0:CA4=H.
Full-PDalso can be entered with Self-Refresh Entry (SRE) command with C0:CA3=H. Likewise, Full-PD with 
Frequency change also can be entered with C1:CA3=H.
The PDE command must not be issued while the following operations are in progress on the same PSD:
•

7.6.3. Full-PD Entry and Exit
Entering Full-PDis permitted when the PSD is in IDLE or Self-Refresh. In IDLE PD, all banks are closed and all other 
commands to the PSD needs to be completed. The PSD will be in an Idle state and ready to enter a Power-Down mode.
Entering Full-PDdeactivates the input and output buffers, excluding CS and RESET_n which remain enabled 
throughout the duration of this mode. CS assertion is not permitted until the Power-Down Exit (PDX).
To exit Full-PD, the host asserts CS high with the tCSH period to wake up the deactivated input buffers. After tXP_-
FULL, CK Sync must be initiated.

JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION

In the Full-PD with Frequency change, the DRAM flips the FSP-OP value at PDX sequence and begins referencing 
the FSP-related Mode Registers (MRs). Frequency change sequence is finally determined by the CS pulse-width of 
PDX sequence. The host shall assert CS for duration tCSH during a frequency change, and for duration tCSH_FC_-
Cancel when canceling the change.
Since the frequency change operation may involve VREF and ODT values, the changes will be performed after the CS 
is de-asserted. After the FSP MR transition, deactivated input buffers are enabled. After tXP_FC, CK sync must be 
initiated.

needs to be changed concurrently. The host initiates the FSP update sequence concurrently on all ranks by asserting 
CS on same clock edge.

7.6.4. ODT-PD Entry and Exit
Entering ODT-PD is permitted when the PSD is in IDLE. In ODT-PD, all banks are closed and all other commands to 
the PSD needs to be completed. The PSD will be in an Idle state and ready to enter a Power-Down mode.
Entering ODT PD deactivates the input and output buffers, excluding CS, CK_t/c, and RESET_n. While in ODT PD 
Mode, only Write NT ODT commands and PDX ODT commands are allowed tCPDED after ODT-PDE command. 
Clock input buffers CK_t and CK_c remain enabled, allowing the appropriate Write NT ODT command to be passed 
through and decoded while ODT PD Mode (i.e., the DRAM monitors CS and will not exit ODT PD as a result of a 
valid Write NT ODT command being registered). Table 1 shows the command truth table for ODT PD Mode.
If CA-parity check is enabled upon entering the ODT-PD, the DRAM disables CA parity checking and operates based 
on CS only. However, the WR NT-ODT or PDX_ODT operation remains delayed by tCA_PAR_Delay in the same

WAKE-UP CS (WUCS)
JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION
PROPOSED
7.7. Wake-up CS (WUCS)
7.7.1. Introduction
DDR6 supports Wake-up CS (WUCS) functionality. The feature allows the DRAM to enter a clock-gating state on the 
CMD/ADDR interface during periods of inactivity and to exit that state in a controlled, deterministic manner ahead of 
the first command of a new access sequence. When Wake-up CS is enabled, all four internal clock phases (CK/4_0°, 
CK/4_90°, CK/4_180°, and CK/4_270°) may be gated during idle periods, and every CS assertion acts as a wake-up 
event.
Figure 147 — Wake-up CS Timing Diagram Example
7.7.2. tCS_PRE - Pre-command Wake-up Period
tCS_PRE is the minimum time between the assertion of the Wake-up CS and the first valid command. tCS_PRE is an 
analog timing parameter. During tCS_PRE, the CMD/ADDR receivers are enabled and the device prepares to sample 
incoming commands. The minimum value of tCS_PRE(min) is 1.5ns. Once tCS_PRE has elapsed from the Wake-up 
CS assertion, the CMD/ADDR interface remains ready to sample commands indefinitely, with no clock gating applied 
while in the ready state.
7.7.3. tCS_POST - Post-command Inactivity Timeout
tCS_POST is a digital, programmable parameter that defines the number of clock cycles after the last command (or 
last CS pulse assertion) before the CMD/ADDR interface automatically returns to the power-save (clock-gating) state, 
provided no new command has been issued in the interim. tCS_POST is set via MRW as shown in Table 1. The host 
may extend the active window deterministically by issuing periodic NOP commands before tCS_POST expires.


=== PDF PAGE 338 ===
 Page 314 of 417
WAKE-UP CS (WUCS)
JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION
PROPOSED
7.7.4. Wake-up CS in MRUPDATE and CSTM Modes
Wake-up CS settings shall only be modified while the device is in MRUPDATE mode. Upon issuing the MCME 
command, Wake-up CS functionality is automatically disabled after tMCME_Delay. Wake-up CS is re-enabled 
following MRUPDATE mode exit, using either the previous settings (if unchanged) or the newly programmed settings 
(if reprogrammed during MRUPDATE).
7.7.5. Wake-up CS Sequence
The Wake-up CS sequence operates as follows:
1)
After power-up or reset, Wake-up CS functionality is disabled.
2)
The host shall enable Wake-up CS operation by programming the associated mode register field to a non-default 
value. The default MR setting 00b disables the feature.
3)
When Wake-up CS is enabled, the DRAM may place the CMD/ADDR receive path into a power-saving state 
after tCS_POST of command inactivity.
4)
Prior to issuing a command while the CMD/ADDR receive path is in the power-saving state, the host shall issue 
a Wake-up CS pulse.
5)
The first valid command shall not be issued until at least tCS_PRE(min) after the rising edge of the Wake-up 
CS pulse.
6)
Following a Wake-up CS pulse, the CMD/ADDR receive path shall remain in the active-ready state and capable 
of accepting commands.
7)
Any command CS assertion occurring while the receive path is already active will also reset the tCS_POST 
activity timer.
8)
If no command activity occurs for tCS_POST after the previous command, the CMD/ADDR receive path may 
automatically return to the power-saving state.

MODE REGISTER OPERATIONS
JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION

7.8. Mode Register Operations
7.8.1. Mode Register Read
The Mode Register Read (MRR) command is used to read configuration and status data from the DDR6 SDRAM 
registers. The MRR command is initiated with CS and CA [5:0] in the proper state as defined by the Command Truth 
Table. The mode register address operands (MA [7:0]) allow the user to select one of the available Mode Registers 
(MRs). MRR has a command burst length (BL) 24. The mode register contents are available at the start of 8th and ends 
at 23rd UI data bits of DQ [2:0] after RL + tCK2DQO following the MRR command. Every Opcode, OP [7:0], is avail-
able for 2 UI lengths as shown in Table 1-6. The minimum MRR to MRR command period is defined by tMRR. The 
minimum MRR command to Any command period is defined by tMRD as shown in Table 7. The truth table for MRR 
is defined in Table 8.
To avoid a potentially worst-case pattern, DQ1 bits shall have their contents inverted relative to DQ0 and DQ2 (repre-
sented with “!”). The data in the burst (BL0-7) shall be either “0” or “1”, with “1” indicating that the content of the 
later UIs (BL8-23) is inverted. Subsequent data bits DQ [5:3] for 2p6, 1p6 and DQ [11:6] for 1p12 configuration 
contain valid but undefined content. DQS is toggled for the duration of the Mode Register Read burst.
7.8.1.1. MRR Timing Diagram
The timing diagram below depicts the tMRR and tMRD timing for MRR command to MRR command and MRR to 
6-cycle MRW command to PSD specific MR or any other valid.
Figure 148 — Mode Register Read Timing Diagram
NOTE: 1: Only DES command is allowed during tMRR and tMRD periods.
7.8.1.2. MRR behavior for Shared MR and PSD MR
For MRR, 2pX and 1pX operate differently for Shared MR and PSD MR accesses.
2pX Mode
•
Each PSD reads its own PSD MR.
•
The corresponding OP values are output through the DQ pins associated with that PSD.
1pX Mode
•
Shared MR Read: The OP values are output through all DQs, with the data replicated in 3-DQ groups.
•
PSD MR Read: Each PSD's OP values are output through the DQ pins associated with that PSD.
Note: For detailed information regarding Shared and PSD MR access mechanisms, please refer to Mode Register 
Access in Section x.x.y

7.8.2. Mode Register Write
The Mode Register Write (MRW) command is used to write configuration data to the mode registers. The MRW 
command is initiated by setting CS and CA [5:0] to valid levels as defined in Command Truth Table. The mode register 
address operands (MA [7:0]) allow the user to select one of available Mode Registers (MRs). The Mode Register Write 
command is an MRW 6-cycle long command. The minimum MRW command period is defined by tMRW. The 
minimum MRW command to any command period is defined by tMRD as shown in Table 1. The truth table for mode 
register write (MRW) is defined in Table 2. The Mode Register Write command to read-only registers has no impact 
on the functionality of the device.
The timing diagram below depicts the timing for MRW command to MRW command and MRW to 4-cycle or any 
other valid command.
Figure 149 — Mode Register Write Timing
NOTE: Only DES command is allowed during tMRW and tMRD periods.

7.8.3. Mode Register Update (MRUPDATE) Mode
The Mode Register Update (MRUPDATE) Mode performs the equivalent function as the Mode Register Write 
(MRW) command, but without the command bits, saving two Command/Address (CA) cycles for every Mode Register 
write operation. MRUPDATE is multi-cycle CS per C/A cycle as shown in the first figure below, as compared to the 
single-cycle CS MRW command.
7.8.3.1. MRUPDATE Mode Entry
MRUPDATE Mode is entered by issuing an MCME command, which also enables multi-cycle CS.
Entering MRUPDATE Mode resets the starting reference point for the DRAM to know that all subsequent commands 
received will be a function of four (4) CA cycles. The 4 CA cycles are shown in the table below.
7.8.3.2. MRUPDATE Mode Execution
For the device to latch the MRUPATE in cases where the alignment between CS, CA and CK may be unknown, each 
CA cycle must meet the required timings. The CA inputs must reach the proper command state and provide tMC_Setup 
of setup prior to CS transitioning from LOW to HIGH, CS must remain HIGH for tMC_CSPW, and the CA inputs 
must remain in the proper command state and provide tMC_Hold of hold after CS transitions from HIGH to LOW.

7.8.3.3. MRUPDATE Mode Exit
MRUPDATE Mode is disabled by executing four cycles of MRUPDATE with CA4=LOW. The MRA and OP bits 
states may be any combination of HIGH and LOW. Disabling MRUPDATE will also disable multi-cycle CS.

7.9. Other Operations
7.9.1. On-Die Termination for DQ, DQS
On-Die Termination (ODT) is a feature of the DDR6 SDRAM that allows the DRAM to change termination resistance 
for each DQ and DQS. The ODT feature is designed to improve signal integrity of the memory channel by allowing 
the DRAM controller to independently change termination resistance for any or all DRAM devices. In addition to the 
control capability of the DQ ODT, the DQS ODT can be independently programmed via MR63:OP[5:3]. All ODT 
control is command & mode register based. ODT is enabled via Write and non-target Write commands, disabled via 
Read and Mode Register Read commands, or set to the default “PARK” value through settings in MR63-MR66.
A simple functional representation of the DRAM ODT feature is shown in Figure 1.
DQ, DQS
Figure 151 — Functional Representation of ODT
The switch is enabled by the internal ODT control logic, which uses command decode, Mode Register Settings and 
other control information, see below. The value of RTT is determined by the settings of Mode Register bits.
7.9.1.1. ODT Modes and State Table
The ODT Mode of DDR6 SDRAM has four states: Data/Strobe Termination Disable, RTT_WR, RTT_NT_WR and 
RTT_PARK/DQS_RTT_PARK. The ODT Mode is enabled based on Mode Registers for each RTT listed below. In 
this case, the value of RTT is determined by the settings of these mode register bits.
Application: Controller can control each RTT condition with Write (WR), Read (RD) or Mode Register Read (MRR) 
commands and use of ODT Offset Control Mode Registers.
•
Data Termination Disable: DRAM driving data upon receiving Read command disables the termination after 
RL-2-8UI, and re-enables the termination at RL + BL/2 (RL and BL/2 defined in clock cycles).
•
Strobe Termination Disable: DRAM driving strobe upon receiving Read command disables the termination 
after RL-2-tRPRE-(Read_DQS_Pre-shift) and re-enables the termination at RL+BL/2- 0.5+tRPST-(Read_D-
QS_Pre-shift) (RL and BL/2 defined in clock cycles).
•
RTT_WR: For the rank that is being written to provide termination, it adjusts timing based on ODT Control 
Mode Register settings.
•
RTT_NT_WR: DRAM applies RTT_NT_WR if it sees a CS asserted during the third and fourth pulse of the 
Write command, and it adjusts timing based on ODT Control Mode Register settings.
To other

OTHER OPERATIONS
JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION

•
RTT_PARK: Default parked value set via MR63:OP[2:0]; enabled when a Read or Write is not active.
•
DQS_RTT_PARK: Default parked value for DQS set via MR63:OP[5:3]; enabled when a Read is not active.
Those RTT values have priority as follows:
1)
Data Termination Disable and Strobe Termination Disable
2)
RTT_WR
3)
RTT_NT_WR
4)
RTT_PARK and DQS_RTT_PARK
This means if there is a Write command, then the DRAM turns on RTT_WR, not RTT_NT_WR, and also if there is a 
Read command, then the DRAM disables data termination and goes into driving mode.
If CS is driven high during the third and fourth cycle of a Write command, then RTT_NT_WR is enabled for the 
non-target rank. This provides additional and potentially different termination options for the other ranks on the 
channel.

7.9.1.2. Dynamic ODT
Dynamic ODT is the feature that allows the DDR6 SDRAM to switch between pre-programmed ODT termination 
states in response to command activity without requiring MRW during normal traffic.
ODT Functional Description
The function is described as follows:
•
Four RTT values are available: RTT_NT_WR, RTT_WR, RTT_PARK and DQS_RTT_PARK. 
–
The value for RTT_NT_WR is preselected via MR64:OP[5:3].
–
The value for RTT_WR is preselected via MR64:OP[2:0].
–
The value for RTT_PARK is preselected via MR63:OP[2:0].
–
The value for DQS_RTT_PARK is preselected via MR63:OP[5:3].
•
During operation without RD, MRR, WR, Non-target WR commands, the termination is controlled as follows: 
–
RTT_PARK is selected for DQ
–
DQS_RTT_PARK is selected for DQS.
•
When a Write command (WR) is registered, the termination is controlled as follows: 
–
A latency ODTLon_WR after the Write command, termination strength RTT_WR is selected.
–
A latency ODTLoff_WR after the Write command, termination strength RTT_WR is de-selected.
•
The termination, RTT_NT_WR, for the non-target Write command is selected and de-selected by latencies 
ODTLon_WR_NT and ODTLoff_WR_NT, respectively.
•
When a Read command (RD) is registered, the termination is controlled as follows: 
–
A latency ODTLoff_RD after the Read command, data termination is disabled. Then, ODTLon_RD after 
the Read command, data termination is enabled.
–
A latency ODTLoff_RD_DQS after the Read command, strobe termination is disabled. 
ODTLon_RD_DQS after the Read command, strobe termination is enabled.
To achieve the minimum write burst duration, ODTLoff_X and ODTLon_X latencies contain independent program-
mable mode register offsets:
•
The values for the Write command ODT control offsets are preselected via MR65.

The duration of a Write or Read command is a full burst cycle, BL/2. The termination select ("ODTLon_...") and 
de-select ("ODTLoff_...") latency settings shall not result in an ODT pulse width which violates a burst cycle (BL/2) 
minimum duration. The equation "ODTLoff_X - ODTLon_X >= BL/2” must be met, where X is the termination 
latency setting associated with a particular command type (WR, WR_NT).
See Table 3 for ODT latency and timing parameter details.

tODTQoff, tODTQon During Read Operations
At the start of a read burst, the ODT is disabled and transitions to the DRAM drive state tODTQoff before the start of 
the DQ, DQS burst. tODTQoff(min) and tODTQoff(max) are minimum and maximum RTT change timing skews 
between different RTT states with respect to the DQ, DQS toggle start point. At the end of a read burst, the ODT is 
enabled tODTQon after the end of the DQ, DQS burst. tODTQon(min) and tODTQon(max) are minimum and 
maximum RTT change timing skews between different RTT states with respect to the DQ, DQS toggle end point. 
Unless the RTT is specifically disabled, no High-Z state shall be allowed during RTT transition. During DRAM Drive 
state, the DRAM RON shall keep the DQ signal low prior to the first DQ transition.
Figure 2 is an example showing the tODTQoff/on(min), tODTQoff/on(avg) and tODTQoff/on(max) with respect to 
the RTT status and effects on the DQ lines prior to the burst.
Figure 152 — tODTQoff,on Clarification – DQ, DQS RTT Park to Read
NOTE 1: ODTLon_WR_NT = WL + ODTLon_WR_NT_Offset - EVEN(RU((tODTon,max - 
tDQS2DQi,min)/tCK) + 8.5UI - tDQSoffset,min - tDQSS_min)
NOTE 2: ODTLoff_WR_NT = WL + ODTLoff_WR_NT_Offset + EVEN(RU((tDQS2DQI,max – 
tODTon,min)/tCK) + 23.5UI + tDQSoffset,max + tDQSS,max)
NOTE 3: ODTLon_WR_NT_offset, ODTLoff_WR_NT_offset = 0 (default value) in this Table.

tODTon, tODToff During Write and Non-target Write Operations
tODTon is defined as the time required for the DRAM to transition from the RTT_PARK state to the write- termination 
state (RTT_WR for Write, RTT_NT_WR for Non-Target Write). tODTon is measured after the corresponding ODT 
turn-on latency (ODTLon_WR or ODTLon_NT_WR) from the Write command has been satisfied. tODToff is defined 
as the time required for the DRAM to transition from the write-termination state back to the RTT_PARK state, and 
tODToff is measured after the corresponding ODT turn-off latency (ODTLoff_WR or ODTLoff_NT_WR) from the 
Write command has been satisfied. Unless the RTT is specifically disabled, no High-Z state shall be allowed during 
RTT transition.
Figure 153 — tODTon,off Clarification – DQ RTT Park to Write
NOTE: 1: The host will keep the DQ signal LOW 8UI prior to the data driven in D0.
NOTE: 2: The DFE should assume that 8UI prior to D0 the signal is LOW.

7.9.2. Temperature Sensor and Temperature Gradient
DDR6 devices feature a temperature sensor whose status (MR04:OP[2:0]) can be read to determine the minimum 
required refresh rate. Either the temperature sensor readout or the device TOPER may be used by the system to deter-
mine whether the refresh rate and operating temperature requirements are being met. Additionally, the device’s 
temperature status (MR04:OP[2:0]) can be read by the system to determine the current approximate temperature range 
sensed by the device.
DDR6 devices shall monitor device temperature and update MR4 according to tTSI. Upon completion of device initial-
ization, the device temperature status bits shall be no older than tTSI. MR4 will be updated even when device is in Self 
Refresh state.
When using the temperature sensor, the actual device case temperature may be higher than the TOPER specification 
that applies for the standard or elevated temperature ranges. For example, TCASE may be above 85°C when 
MR04:OP[2:0]=100B. DDR6 devices shall allow for 2°C temperature margin between the point at which the device 
updates the MR04 value and the point at which the controller reconfigures the system accordingly.
Each MR04[2:0] setting specifies a nominal temperature range, and the six ranges defined by MR04[2:0] are deter-
mined by five temperature thresholds which will be nominally 75°C, 80°C, 85°C, 90°C, and 95°C. The 3rd threshold 
(nominally 85°C) is used by the system to switch to 2x refresh. The 5th threshold (nominally 95°C) is used by the 
system to throttle activity to keep the DRAM at a safe operating temperature. The 2nd threshold (nominally 80°C) 
allows the system to take actions which delay reaching the 3rd threshold. The 4rd threshold (nominally 90 °C) allows 
the system to take actions which delay reaching the 5th threshold. The 1st threshold (nominally 75°C) has no minimum 
value specified and the 5th threshold (nominally 95°C) has no maximum temperature value specified.
To ensure proper operation using the temperature sensor, applications should consider the following factors:
•
TempGradient is the maximum temperature gradient experienced by the memory device at the temperature of 
interest over a range of 2°C, measured in the range of interest 75-95°C.
•
ReadInterval is the maximum time period between Temperature Sensor mode register bits being read by the 
system.
•
TempSensorInterval (tTSI) is the maximum delay between internal updates of the Temperature Sensor mode 
register bits.
•
SysRespDelay is the maximum time between a read of the Temperature Sensor mode register bits and the 
response by the system.
The required frequency of polling the Temperature Sensor mode register bits by the system uses the maximum Temp-
Gradient and the maximum response time of the system in the following equation: 
TempGradient x (ReadInterval + tTSI + SysRespDelay) ≤ TempMargin

Figure 154 — Temp Sensor Timing
7.9.2.1. Temperature Sensor Read Out
DDR6 provides temperature sensor readout information as reference. Temperature is not located in the same area as 
memory array. Only limited position temperature is measured.
DDR temperature sensor cannot provide accurate enough temperature due to circuits and placement. Host can use this 
information as temperature gradient information. Temperature accuracy is not guaranteed by design values.
MR56 is assigned for temperature sensor report. Please refer MR56 definition for more detailed information.
Mode register read out shows on-die temperature sensor value.
7.9.2.2. Thermal Offset
The host may provide a temperature-offset to the DDR6 SDRAM, allowing the memory’s self-refresh timing or the 
2x-refresh decision point to be adjusted for reliable operation. This offset is programmed via the appropriate MR (e.g., 
MR04:OP[7:5]). Once applied, the DRAM’s Temperature-Compensated Self-Refresh (TCSR) circuit uses the 
adjusted temperature reference to modify the refresh interval as required, and the update is reflected in the 
refresh-control field (e.g. MR04:OP[2:0]) within approximately 200 µs.

NOTE 1: DRAM vendors must report all of the possible settings over the operating temperature range of the device.

 NOTE 2: The 2x Refresh Rate must be provided by the system before the DRAM Tj has gone up by more than

equal to 011.
 NOTE 3: The device may not operate properly when MR04[2:0]=110 and the DRAM Tj has gone up by more than 
2 °C (Temperature Margin) since the first report out of MR04[2:0]=110. This condition is reset when MR04[2:0] 
is equal to 101. MR04[2:0]=110 must be a temporary condition of the DRAM, to be addressed by immediately 
reducing the Tj of the DRAM by throttling its power, and/or the power of nearby devices.
 NOTE 4: MR04[4] = 0 at power-up. MR04[2:0] bits are valid after initialization sequence (Te).
 NOTE 5: When MR04[4]=1, the refresh rate reported in MR04[2:0] has changed since the last MR04 read. A mode 
register read from MR04 will reset MR04[4] to '0'.
 NOTE 6: MR04[2:0] bits indicate the latest Refresh Rate whenever MR04[4] = 1
 NOTE 7: See the section on “Temperature Sensor” for information on the recommended frequency of reading 
MR04
 NOTE 8: The two PSDs (PSD0 and PSD1) on a single die share the same thermal sensor, therefore the thermal 
offset value in MR04[7:5] is applied to the common thermal sensor

7.9.3. DQS and CLK Interval Oscillator
As voltage and temperature change on the SDRAM die, the DQS and CLK clock tree delay will shift and may require 
re-training. The DDR6-SDRAM includes an internal DQS and CLK clock tree oscillator to measure the amount of 
delay over a given time interval (determined by the controller), allowing the controller to compare the trained delay 
value to the delay value seen at a later time. The DQS and CLK interval oscillator will provide the controller with 
important information regarding the need to re-train, and the magnitude of potential error.
The DQS and CLK interval oscillator is started by issuing an MRW MR05 [Start DQS/CLK Interval Osc] command 
as described in the Mode Register Functional Modes section, which will start an internal ring oscillator that counts the 
number of times a signal propagates through a replica of the DQS or CLK clock tree.
The DQS and CLK interval oscillator may be stopped by issuing an MRW MR05 [Stop DQS/CLK Interval Osc] 
command as described in the MRW Functional Mode section, or the controller may instruct the SDRAM to count for 
a specific number of clocks and then stop automatically (See MR45xx,46xx, and 47xx for more information).If 
MR20xx is set to automatically stop the DQS and CLK interval oscillator, then the MRW MR05 [Stop DQS/CLK 
Interval Osc] command shall not be used. Similarly, if MR23 is set to automatically stop the CLK interval oscillator, 
then the MRW MR05 [Stop CLK Interval Osc] command shall not be used.
When the DQS and CLK interval oscillator is stopped by either method, the result of the oscillator counter is automat-
ically stored in MR1Exx/MR1Fxx and MR21xx/MR22xx
MRW commands to MR20xx during an ongoing DQS/CLK interval oscillator operation are not permitted. MRW 
commands to MR23 during an ongoing CLK interval oscillator operation are not permitted.MR20xxand MR23may be 
reprogrammed after the tMRD delay following the automatic oscillator stop.
DQS interval oscillator and CLK interval oscillator cannot be operated simultaneously. After completing DQS (or 
CLK) interval oscillator operation, MRW MR05 [Start CLK (or DQS) Interval Osc] command can be issued.
The DRAM shall respond in one of two ways if an MRW MR05 [Start DQS/CLK Interval Osc] is issued during an 
ongoing DQS/CLK interval oscillator operation (automatic or manual). One option is the DRAM will ignore the 
concurrent start command, in which case the DQS/CLK interval oscillator operation will proceed as normal with all 
timing constraints referencing the original start command. The other option is for the DRAM to restart the DQS 
interval oscillator operation, in which case all timing constraints will reference the most recent (subsequent) start 
command. The host must account for the worst-case option in the event that a concurrent start command is issued. If 
issuing a concurrent MRW MR05 [Start DQS/CLK Interval Osc] results in a loss of run time tracking, the results stored 
in MR1Exx/MR1F and MR21xx/MR22 should be ignored and the full operation restarted.
Entering Full Power-down during an ongoing DQS/CLK interval oscillator operation is permitted. Upon exiting Full 
Power-down, an operation started in automatic mode shall be allowed to complete naturally based upon the specified 
number of clocks (cumulative before and after Full Power-down). If a manual operation started prior to entering Full 
Power-down is not stopped prior as well, the operation shall be manually stopped upon Full Power-down exit. The 
results stored in MR1Exx/MR1Fxx and MR21xx/MR22xx for a DQS and CLK interval oscillator operation that spans 
Full Power-down entry/exit should be ignored and the full operation restarted.
The controller may adjust the accuracy of the result by running the DQS/CLK interval oscillator for shorter (less accu-
rate) or longer (more accurate) duration. The accuracy of the result for a given temperature and voltage is determined 
by the following equation:
DQS Oscillator Granularity Error =
2 * ( DQS delay )
Run time
CLK Oscillator Granularity Error =
2 * (CLK delay)
Run time


=== PDF PAGE 366 ===
 Page 342 of 417
OTHER OPERATIONS
JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION
PROPOSED
Where:
Run time
total time between start and stop commands
DQS delay
the value of the DQS clock tree delay (tRX_DQS2DQtDQS2DQi min/max)
CLK delay
the value of the CLK clock tree delay (tTX_CLK2DQtCK2DQo min/max)
Additional matching error must be included, which is the difference between DQS/CLK replica circuit and the actual 
DQS/CLK clock tree across voltage and temperature. The matching error is vendor specific.
Therefore, the total accuracy of the DQS or CLK interval oscillator counter is given by:
•
DQS(or CLK) Interval Oscillator Accuracy = 1 - Granularity Error - Matching Error
Example: If the total time between start and stop commands is 100 ns, and the maximum DQS clock tree delay is TBD 
ps (tRX_DQS2DQtDQS2DQi max), then the DQS interval oscillator granularity error is:
This equates to a granularity timing error of TBD ps.
Assuming a circuit Matching Error of 5 ps across voltage and temperature, then the accuracy is:
The result of the DQS/CLK interval oscillator is defined as the number of DQS/CLK clock tree delays that can be 
counted within the "run time," determined by the controller. The result for DQSCLK is stored in MR1Exx and 
MR1Fxx, and that for CLK is also stored in MR21xx and MR22xx. MR1Exx and MR21xx contain the least significant 
bits (LSB) of the result for DQS and CLK, respectively. MR1Fxx and MR22xx contain the most significant bits (MSB) 
of the result for DQS and CLK, respectively. MR1Exx and MR1Fxx are overwritten by the SDRAM when an MRW 
MR05 [Stop DQS Interval Osc] command is received. Similarly, for a CLK interval oscillator stop command, MR21xx 
and MR22xx are also overwritten when SDRAM receives MRW MR05 [Stop CLK Interval Osc] command.
The SDRAM counter will count to its maximum value (=2^16) and stop. If the maximum value is read from the mode 
registers, then the memory controller must assume that the counter overflowed the register and discard the result. The 
longest "run time" for the oscillator that will not overflow the counter registers can be calculated as follows:
•
Longest tRX_DQS2DQtDQS2DQi Run Time Interval = 216* tRX_DQS2DQtDQS2DQi (min) =216 * TBD =

2 * ( TBD ns)

NOTE 1: The WOSCMatch is the matching error between the actual DQS and DQS interval oscillator over voltage 
and temp. The ROSCMatch is the matching error between the actual CLK and CLK interval oscillator over voltage 
and temp.
NOTE 2: This parameter will be characterized or guaranteed by design.
NOTE 3: The WOSCMatch and ROSCMatch are defined as the following: WOSCMatch = [tRX_DQS2DQtD-
QS2DQi(V, T) - tDQSOSC(V, T) - WOSCoffset] Where tRX_DQS2DQtDQS2DQi(V,T), tDQSOSC(V,T) and 
WOSCOffset(V,T) are determined over the same voltage and temp conditions. ROSCMatch = [tTX_-
CLK2DQtCK2DQo(V, T) - tCLKOSC(V, T) - ROSCOffset] Where tTX_CLK2DQtCK2DQo(V,T), tCLKOSC(V,T) 
and ROSCOffset(V,T) are determined over the same voltage and temp conditions.
NOTE 4: The runtime of the oscillator must be at least 200ns for determining tDQSOSC(V,T) and tCLKOSC(V,T).
NOTE 5: The input stimulus for tRX_DQS2DQtDQS2DQi and tTX_CLK2DQtCK2DQo will be consistent over 
voltage and temp conditions.
NOTE 6: The WOSCOffset is the average difference of the endpoints across voltage and temp.
NOTE 7: tRX_DQS2DQtDQS2DQi(V,T) delay will be the average of DQS to DQ delay over the runtime period. 
tTX_CLK2DQtCK2DQo(V,T) delay will be the average of CLK to DQ delay over the runtime period.
NOTE 8: The matching error and offset of WOSC came from DQS interval oscillator.
NOTE 9: The matching error and offset of ROSC came from CLK interval oscillator.

7.9.4. Post Package Repair (PPR)
DDR6 supports three functions of Post Package Repair (PPR) for failed row addresses: Hard Post Package Repair 
(hPPR) and MBIST PPR (mPPR) for permanent row repair, and Soft Post Package Repair (sPPR) for temporary row 
repair. mPPR is to be used in conjunction with the MBIST function to repair failing addresses identified during 
MBIST.
7.9.4.1. Guard Key Protection
Access to hPPR, sPPR, MBIST, and mPPR modes is protected through a sequential MRW guard key (‘PPR guard 
key’), which prevents unintended PPR programming. The sequential MRW guard key is the same for hPPR, sPPR, 
MBIST and mPPR.
The PPR guard key requires a sequence of four MRW commands to be issued immediately after issuing the MRW to 
enter PPR or MBIST. The guard key sequence is entered in the specified order as stated below and shown in the guard 
key encoding for the MR30 table. Interruptions to the guard key sequence by other MRW/MRR commands or non-MR 
commands (e.g., ACT, WR, RD, REFab, REFdb, REFqb) are prohibited. Although interruption of the guard key entry 
is not allowed, if the guard key is not entered in the required order or is interrupted by other commands, 
hPPR/sPPR/MBIST/mPPR entry will not execute and the offending command that interrupted the PPR guard key may 
or may not execute correctly. However, the offending command will not cause the device to lock up and subsequent 
ACT and WR commands will be conducted as normal DRAM commands. The DRAM does not provide an error indi-
cation if an incorrect PPR guard key sequence is entered.
Figure 164 — Guard Key Timing Diagram
NOTE: Only DES commands are allowed during tMRD.

7.9.4.2. Hard Post Package Repair (hPPR)
hPPR resources are allocated per bank in the PSD Mode Registers. The hPPR/sPPR resource designation 
(MRBF-MRC6) will indicate the hPPR resource availability and should be read/checked prior to attempting a repair. 
It is important to note that hPPR repairs are permanent. 
7.9.4.3. hPPR Fail Row Address Repair
Entry into hPPR is through a mode register enable and an ACT command is used to transmit the bank and row address 
of the row to be repaired. For DRAM configured as 2p3 or 2p6, an hPPR shall only be done on one PSD at a time and 
the other PSD shall remain idle. After tRCD time, a WRA command is used to select the individual DRAM through 
the DQ bits and to transfer the repair address to the DRAM. Following the program time and a PREpb command, exit 
hPPR through the mode register disable and resume normal operation after tPGMPST_hPPR.
hPPR Fail Row Address Repair Procedure:
1)
Verify hPPR resource availability by issuing an MRR to read the hPPR/sPPR resource designation registers in 
the PSDMode Registers (MRBF-MRC6). If no repair resource is available (11, hPPR and sPPR resource is not 
available), the host controller should not initiate hPPR mode.
2)
Before entering hPPR mode, precharge all banks and ensure they are idle; disable CRC mode.

3)
Enable hPPR by setting MR32[0] = 1 and wait tMRD.
4)
Issue the guard key as four consecutive MR30[7:0] MRW commands as described above.
5)
Issue an ACT command with the BG, Bank and Row fail address; wait tRCD.
6)
Issue a WRA command with a valid column address; wait WL. The DRAM will ignore the column address.
7)
After WL, drive DQ[2:0] HIGH for 12tCK on the target DRAM. For non-target DRAMs, drive DQ[2:0] LOW

8)
Wait tPGM_hPPRa or tPGM_hPPRb for internal repair, then issue PREpb.
9)
Wait tPGM_EXIT_hPPR after PREpb command to enable recognition of the repaired row address.
10)
Exit hPPR by setting MR32[0] = 0 and wait tPGMPST_hPPR.
11)
Resume normal operation.
12)
When repairing more than one failed address in a bank with more than one hPPR resource available, repeat steps 
3–10. Otherwise, repeat steps 1-10 for repairs in other banks.
During hPPR mode, REFab/REFdb commands are allowed, but array contents are not guaranteed. Upon receiving a 
REFab or REFdb command in hPPR mode, the DRAM may ignore the Refresh operation but will not disrupt the repair 
operation. Other commands except REFab/REFdb during tPGM_hPPR can cause incomplete repair so no other

7.9.5. Soft Post Package Repair (sPPR)
Soft Post Package Repair (sPPR) enables rapid, temporary repair of a single row address per bank on DDR6 DRAM 
devices. In contrast, Hard Post Package Repair (hPPR) provides permanent row repair with a longer execution time. 
sPPR and hPPR differ in their operational limitations and intended use cases.
The host controller must ensure that refresh requirements for all other rows are maintained during sPPR operations. 
sPPR information is retained as long as VDD remains within the operating range. If power is removed or the device is 
RESET, the sPPR will revert to the unrepaired state. If hPPR or mPPR modes are entered while sPPR is enabled, the 
DRAM will disable sPPR in the target bank and clear the current address prior to executing. MBIST and sPPR may 
not be enabled at the same time. sPPR must have been disabled, cleared and unlocked prior to entering MBIST.
Each bank has one sPPR resource shared with hPPR; when the hPPR resources for a bank are used up, the bank has 
no more available resources for sPPR. To preserve sPPR availability, this shared resource will be used for hPPR by 
the DRAM only after all other hPPR resources are exhausted. The host is responsible for reading the hPPR/sPPR 
resource registers prior to issuing sPPR to ensure the resource is available in the targeted bank. The sPPR Verification 
mode can be used to validate if the targeted row was repaired correctly. The use of the sPPR resource will be reflected 
in the hPPR/sPPR resource registers as 10B.
DDR6 shall support the sPPR Undo and sPPR Lock operations. sPPR Undo will undo a previous sPPR and cause the 
sPPR resource to be taken back to its unused condition. The original row will appear back in the memory map at its 
original location following an sPPR Undo and will be reflected in the hPPR/sPPR resource register state. sPPR Lock 
will lock the specific sPPR resource at its current location and not allow another sPPR or an sPPR Undo to be 
performed to that resource.
Rows that have been replaced by spare rows do not require refresh by the DRAM. When a spare row is moved in or 
out of the memory map, the host controller is responsible for issuing sufficient ACT commands to any unmapped row 
to maintain required data retention. The host controller is also responsible for performing all necessary data copy oper-
ations between the original row and the spare row.
7.9.5.1. sPPR Fail Row Address Repair
Entry into sPPR is through a mode register enable and an ACT command is used to transmit the bank and row address 
of the row to be repaired. For DRAM configured as 2p3 or 2p6, an sPPR shall only be done on one PSD at a time and 
the other PSD shall remain idle. After tRCD time, a WR command is used to select the individual DRAM through the 
DQ bits and to transfer the repair address to the DRAM. Following the program time and a PREpb command, exit sPPR 
through the mode register disable and resume normal operation after tPGMPST_sPPR.
sPPR Fail Row Address Repair Procedure: 
1)
Verify sPPR resource availability by issuing an MRR to read the hPPR/sPPR resource designation registers in 
the PSD Mode Registers (MR33-48 MRBF-MRC6). If no resources are available (10B, hPPR resource is avail-
able and sPPR resource is not available, 11B, hPPR and sPPR resource is not available), do not initiate sPPR 
mode.
2)
Back up the data of the target row address in the bank prior to executing sPPR. The backup data should be one 
row per bank.
3)
Precharge all banks and ensure they are idle; disable CRC mode.
4)
Enable sPPR by setting MR32:OP[2:1] = 01B and wait tMRD.
5)
Issue the guard key as four consecutive MR30:OP[7:0] MRW commands as described in PPR Guard Key 
Protection.
6)
Issue an ACT command with the BG, Bank, and Row fail address; wait tRCD.
7)
Issue a WR command with a valid column address; wait WL. The DRAM will ignore the column address.


=== PDF PAGE 384 ===
 Page 360 of 417
OTHER OPERATIONS
JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION
PROPOSED
8)
After WL, drive DQ[2:0] HIGH for 12tCK on the target DRAM. For non-target DRAMs, drive DQ[2:0] LOW 
for 12tCK. If all the DQ[2:0] data bits are neither all LOW nor all HIGH for 12tCK, then sPPR mode execution 
is unknown. For 2p6, 1p6 and 1p12 devices, data bits other than DQ[2:0] are don't cares.
9)
Wait tPGM_sPPR for the internal repair register to be written, then issue PREpb to the bank.
10)
Wait tPGM_EXIT_sPPR after PREpb to enable recognition of the repaired row address.
11)
Exit sPPR by setting MR32:OP[2:1] = 00B and wait tPGMPST_sPPR.
12)
Restore the backed-up data to the repaired array
13)
sPPR can be performed without affecting the hPPR previously performed, provided a row is available in that 
repair region. In the case of conducting a subsequent sPPR in the same bank, a sPPR Undo must be performed 
before initiating sPPR. In the case of conducting sPPR in a different bank, repeat steps 1–12. During sPPR, a 
REFRESH command is not allowed between sPPR MRW entry and exit.

7.9.5.2. sPPR Undo
sPPR Undo restores an sPPR resource to its unused state, equivalent to its condition following reset, and will be 
reflected in the hPPR/sPPR resource register state. This operation uses The sPPR Undo procedure follows the same 
protocol as the sPPR sequence below,except MR32:OP[2:1] is set to 10B. 
1)
Precharge all banks and ensure they are idle; disable CRC mode.
2)
Enable sPPR Undo by setting MR32:OP[2:1] = 10B and wait tMRD.
3)
Issue the guard key as four consecutive MR30:OP[7:0] MRW commands as described in PPR Guard Key 
Protection.
4)
Issue an ACT command with the BG, Bank, and Row fail address; wait tRCD.
5)
Issue a WR command with a valid column address; wait WL. The DRAM will ignore the column address.
6)
After WL, drive DQ[2:0] HIGH for 12tCK on the target DRAM. For non-target DRAMs, drive DQ[2:0] LOW 
for 12tCK. If all the DQ[2:0] data bits are neither all LOW nor all HIGH for 12tCK, then sPPR mode execution 
is unknown. For 2p6, 1p6 and 1p12 devices, data bits other than DQ[2:0] are don't cares.
7)
Wait tPGM_sPPR for the internal repair register to be un-written, then issue PREpb to the bank.
8)
Wait tPGM_EXIT_sPPR after PREpb to enable recognition of the repaired row address.
9)
Exit sPPR Undo by setting MR32:OP[2:1] = 00B and wait tPGMPST_sPPR.
 The host controller must issue an ACT command with the same BG bits, Bank bits, and row address used in the most 
recent sPPR for that resource. If the row address does not match the most recent sPPR, the DRAM may or may not 
perform the undo. All required data copy operations are the responsibility of the host controller.
After an sPPR Undo, the resource can be reassigned to a new or previous location. Data within the sPPR resource is 
retained but does not require refresh by the DRAM. If data validity must be maintained, the host controller shall issue 
sufficient ACT commands while the row is mapped in.
7.9.5.3. sPPR Lock
sPPR Lock secures an sPPR resource in its current location, preventing any subsequent sPPR or sPPR Undo operations 
on that resource. Locks are applied individually to each sPPR resource. To remove a lock, a hardware reset or power 
cycle must be performed. A hardware reset or power cycle is also required before executing any hPPR operation if any 
sPPR resources remain locked.
The sPPR Lock procedure follows the same protocol as the sPPR sequence below, except MR32:OP[2:1] is set to 11B. 
1)
Precharge all banks and ensure they are idle; disable CRC mode.
2)
Enable sPPR Lock by setting MR32:OP[2:1] = 11B and wait tMRD.

for 12tCK. If all the DQ[2:0] data bits are neither all LOW nor all HIGH for 12tCK, then sPPR mode execution

 The ACT command shall include BG bits, Bank bits, and the row address associated with the most recent sPPR for 
that resource. If the row address does not match the most recent sPPR, the device may or may not perform the lock.
7.9.5.4. sPPR Verification
sPPR Verification is a DDR6 feature that validates whether a specified row address matches the most recent sPPR 
assignment for a given bank. This operation follows the same protocol as the sPPR sequence, with MR32:OP[3] set to 
1. The verification result is stored in MR32:OP[4], where a value of 1 indicates success and 0 indicates failure. 
MR32:OP[4] is shared across all banks within each PSD and is reset to 0 after every MRR to the verification register. 
Multiple row addresses from different banks can be verified; however, the host controller must read MR32:OP[4] after 
each ACT command to prevent overwriting the previous verification status.
The following is the procedure for sPPR Verification: 
1)
Precharge all banks and ensure they are idle; disable CRC mode.
2)
Enable sPPR Verification by setting MR32:OP[3] = 1 and wait tMRD.
3)
Issue the guard key as four consecutive MR30:OP[7:0] MRW commands as described in PPR Guard Key 
Protection.
4)
Issue an ACT command with the BG, Bank and Row fail address and wait tRAS.
5)
Issue a PREpb command to the bank and wait tRP.
6)
Issue an MRR command to MR32:OP[4] to read the verification status and wait tMRR.
7)
Exit sPPR Verification by setting MR32:OP[3] = 0 and wait tMRD.
8)
In the case of verifying a subsequent Row address from another bank, repeat steps 4–6.

7.9.6. MBIST / mPPR
DDR6 devices support Memory Built-In Self-Test (MBIST) and Memory Built-In Self-Test Post Package Repair 
(mPPR) functions to address hard failures, such as single-bit or multi-bit errors within a device. These functions enable 
detection and repair of weak cells. 
•
MBIST: The DRAM executes vendor-specific test patterns to scan the memory array and identify hard failures.
•
mPPR: The DRAM repairs failing addresses identified during MBIST.
Figure 168 — MBIST Flow Diagram
7.9.6.1. MBIST
MBIST shall only be executed when all banks are in the idle state after device initialization but must be completed 
prior to mPPR. To enable MBIST, the host shall issue an MRW command with MR31[0] = 1. During MBIST, the 
DRAM applies vendor-specific test patterns to the memory array to detect hard failures. The DRAM asserts ALERT 
for the duration of MBIST and de-asserts ALERT upon completion. For 2p3 or 2p6 devices, initiating MBIST through 
either PSD causes the DRAM to execute MBIST on both PSDs.
After MBIST concludes and ALERT is LOW, the DRAM updates the MBIST transparency status in MRC7[1:0], 
which the host shall read. If the transparency status indicates remaining failures and the controller elects to perform 
mPPR, mPPR shall be executed immediately after reading the transparency status. Detailed transparency status defi-
nitions are provided in MRC7.

PROPOSED
 The following steps shall be performed to execute MBIST. For 2p3 or 2p6 devices, steps 2-4 performed through one 
PSD executes MBIST on both PSDs. 
1)
Precharge all banks and ensure they are idle; disable CRC mode.
2)
Enable MBIST by setting MR31[0] = 1 and wait tMRD.
3)
Issue the guard key as four consecutive MR30[7:0] MRW commands as described in PPR Guard Key Protec-
tion. Upon completion of the guard key sequence, the DRAM enters MBIST operation and asserts ALERT 
HIGH for tSELF_TEST. 
a)
While ALERT is HIGH, only DES commands are permitted.
b)
When MBIST concludes, the DRAM de-asserts ALERT LOW to indicate completion. MBIST transpar-
ency status is updated in MRC7.
4)
Exit MBIST by setting MR31[0] = 0 and wait tMRD.
5)
Issue an MRR to MRC7[1:0] and wait tMRR. If transparency indicates that failures remain and repair resources 
are available, proceed to the mPPR procedure.
The following are the MBIST transparency outcomes of MRC7[1:0]:
•
00: No error detected / MBIST has not run since INIT (default)
•
01: Error detected; repair resources are available
•
10: Error detected; no repair resources are available
•
11: Unresolved operation, abort or retry
Figure 169 — MBIST Entry
7.9.6.2. mPPR
If the host issues an MRR to MRC7[1:0] and obtains outcome 01 (error detected; repair resources are available) then 
mPPR shall be performed by issuing an MRW with MR31[1] = 1. The mPPR procedure will repair a single row iden-
tified during MBIST. Attempting mPPR without MBIST may result in an unexpected repair. mPPR resources are sepa-
rated from normal hPPR/sPPR resources.

The following steps shall be performed to execute mPPR. For 2p3 or 2p6 devices, all steps shall be performed through 
each PSD.
1)
Precharge all banks and ensure they are idle; disable CRC mode.
2)
Enable mPPR by setting MR31[1] = 1 and wait tMRD.
3)
Issue the guard key as four consecutive MR30[7:0] MRW commands as described in PPR Guard Key Protec-
tion.
4)
Issue an ACT command with a valid BG, Bank, and Row address; wait tRCD. The DRAM will ignore the 
address.
5)
Issue a WRA command with a valid column address; wait WL. The DRAM will ignore the column address.
6)
After WL, drive DQ[2:0] HIGH for 12tCK on the target DRAM. Only DRAMs which most recently have had 
an MBIST transparency result of 01 may be considered “target DRAMs”. For non-target DRAMs, drive 
DQ[2:0] LOW for 12tCK. If DQ[2:0] data bits are neither all LOW nor all HIGH for 12tCK, then mPPR mode 
execution is unknown. For 2p6, 1p6 and 1p12 devices, data bits other than DQ[2:0] are don't cares.
7)
Wait tPGM_mPPR for internal row repair, then issue a PREab command.
8)
Wait tPGM_EXIT_mPPR after PREab command to enable the DRAM to update MBIST transparency.
9)
Exit mPPR by setting MR31[1] = 0 and wait tPGMPST_mPPR.

10)
Issue an MRR to MRC7[1:0] and wait tMRR. If MBIST transparency result is 01 (error detected; repair resources 
are available), repeat steps 2-10 to perform additional mPPR. Otherwise, the DRAM may continue to the next 
planned operation.

7.9.7. DQ RX Decision Feedback Equalization (DFE)
The DDR6 supports high data rates. Operating at elevated frequencies within the memory channel can result in signif-
icant inter-symbol interference (ISI) due to insertion loss and reflections. To compensate channel characteristics and 
enhance signal integrity, equalization techniques are required on the DRAM receiver (RX) side.
DDR6 provides per-pin controlled Decision Feedback Equalization (DFE) for DQ RX. A 2-tap DFE is selected to 
equalize the received DQ signals, as it effectively reduces the impact of insertion loss and reflections without ampli-
fying the noise.
To eliminate ISI, the per-pin DFE Tap-1 and Tap-2 bits should be enabled and appropriately configured for the data 
rates. When the DFE is enabled, the DQs shall be driven low for a minimum of 8UI prior to the first Write data bit to 
ensure proper DFE synchronization. In case of back to back write, there is no need to add pre-drive.
Figure 171 — Per-pin DFE Pre-Drive Requirement
7.9.7.1. DFE Tap Coefficients
The DFE tap coefficients shown in Table 1 are used by the memory controller to adjust the strengths of Tap-1 and 
Tap-2, to adapt ISI cancellation based on channel performance. Tables 2 and 3 list the DFE Tap-1 and Tap-2 coeffi-
cient values, respectively, according to their step size, minimum, and maximum values. Table 4 is DFE Tap-2 Coeffi-
cient Step Table for polarity inversion.

7.9.8. CRC
DDR6 supports CRC for write and read operations. Write and read CRC can be enabled by separate Write CRC Enable 
and Read CRC Enable mode register bits in MR01OP[3] and MR01OP[2], respectively.
7.9.8.1. Write CRC
The controller generates the CRC checksum and forms the write data packet as shown in Section 1.1.4.3.
The DRAM checks for an error in a received write data packet by comparing the received checksum
against the computed checksum and reports errors using the ALERT signal if there is a mismatch.
DRAM can write data to the DRAM core without waiting for CRC check for full writes. If bad data is written to the 
DRAM core then controller will retry the transaction and overwrite the bad data. Controller is responsible for data 
coherency.
There is no write latency adder when Write CRC is enabled.
Write CRC Error Handling
When DRAM detects CRC error on a received write data packet, then it drives ALERT signal to ‘1’ for 
tCRC_ALERT_PW.
The latency to ALERT signal is defined as tCRC_ALERT in Figure 1.
DRAM willshall set the Write CRC Error Status bit in MRxxOP[0] to '1' upon detecting a CRC error within 
(tCRC_ALERT__ON + tMRD). The Write CRC Error Status bit remains '1' until the host clears it explicitly using an 
MRW command or MRUPDATE Mode.
The controller upon seeing an error as a pulse width willmay retry the write transactions. The controller understands 
the worst-case delay for ALERT (during initialization) and can back up the transactions accordingly or the controller 
can be made more intelligent and try to correlate the write CRC error to a specific rank or a transaction. The controller 
is also responsible for opening any pages and ensuring that retrying of writes is done in a coherent fashion.


=== PDF PAGE 409 ===
Page of 417
OTHER OPERATIONS
JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION
PROPOSED
The pulse width may be seen longer than CRC_ALERT_PW at the controller if there are multiple CRC errors as the 
ALERT is a daisy chain bus or a many-to-one bus.
Figure 173 — CRC Error Reporting Timing Diagram for CRC-8-D64 in 2p3 
with MD_OFF
NOTE: 1: CRC_ALERT_PW is specified from the point where the DRAM starts to drive the signal high to the point where the driver 
releases and the controller starts to pull the signal down.
NOTE: 2: Timing diagram applies to 2p3 devices in MD_OFF Metadata Mode with CRC-4-SC-8 CRC-8-D64.
7.9.8.2. Read CRC
The DDR6 SDRAM generates the CRC checksum and forms the read data packet as shown in Section 1.1.4.4. The 
controller can check for an error in a received data packet by comparing the received checksum against the computed 
checksum and if there is a mismatch then controller may retry the transaction.
Read latency adder exists when read CRC is enabled depends on data rate.
7.9.8.3. CRC Codes
The CRC-4 and CRC-8polynomials used by DDR6 are X4 + X3 + X2 + 1 (0x1d)and X8 + X4 + X3 + X2 + 1 (0x11d), 
respectively, which are different from DDR5. The CRC-4-SC-8 is a subcode derived from row-splitting the CRC-4 
code to get 8 checksum bits.The CRC-4 code includes 64 data bits, 4 metadata bits, and 4 CRC checksum bits. The 
CRC-8-D64 code includes 64 data bits and 8 CRC checksum bits. The CRC-8-D136 code includes 128 data bits, 8 
metadata or zero pad bits, and 8 CRC checksum bits. The CRC code for the 2p3 device configuration is configured as 
CRC-4 or CRC-8-D64 using the CRC Code for 2p3 mode register bit in MR01[1]. The CRC code for 1p6/2p6/1p12 
device configurations is CRC-8-D136. The selected CRC code will be instantiated once for the 2p3/1p6/2p6 device 
configurations or twice for the 1p12 device configuration.

7.9.8.4. CRC Configurations with Metadata Modes
CRC is supported for metadata modes MD_OFF, MD_8b_A, MD_8b_B, and MD_16b., and the CRC mode config-
ured separately as CRC-4 or CRC-4-SC-8 using the CRC Mode mode register bit in MRxx OP[y]. The device config-
uration as 2p3/1p6/2p6/1p12 will determine how many times the CRC code is instantiated in the data packet. Table 2 
shows which CRC codes are supported for various device configurations and metadata modes.

Location of CRC Checksum Bits in the Data Packet
CRC-4 checksum bits alwaysoccupy the last 4 bits of DQ2, and in addition, the last 4 bits of DQ5 for 1p6/2p6/1p12, 
and in addition, the last 4 bits of DQ8 and DQ11 for 1p12. CRC-4-SC-8CRC-8-D64checksum bits alwaysoccupy the 
last 8 bits of DQ2, and in addition, the last 8 bits of DQ5 for 1p6/2p6/1p12, and in addition, the last 8 bits of DQ8 and 
DQ11 for 1p12. CRC-8-D136 checksum bits occupy the last 8 bits of DQ5 for 1p6/2p6/1p12, and in addition, the last 
8 bits of DQ11 for 1p12.
Stored CRC
In certain combinations of metadata modes and CRC codes, the CRC parity bits are a subset of the metadata bits. 
During writes, the CRC parity bits will be checked and also stored as metadata bits.Stored CRC is configured by using 
MR01OP[1:0] to select a CRC code, whose checksum bits are located within a subset of the metadata bits associated 
with the metadata mode. The read CRC function may then be disabled by setting MR01OP[2] to 0, as reading the meta-
data bits will produce the CRC parity bits. This concept, known as stored CRC, avoids additional read latency normally 
associated with read CRC. If the CRC parity bits are a subset of the metadata bits but the read CRC function is enabled 
by setting MR01OP[2] to 1, then the CRC parity bits shall be regenerated during a read and output on the DQ bus.
Zero Pad
When non-data bit locations in the data packet are unused, for example, by metadata or CRC checksum bits, the unused 
bits in the data packet shall be set to 0s.
Mapping Data Packet Bits to CRC Codes

7.9.9. On-Die ECC
[DIRECTIONAL] The DDR6 SDRAM device shall have the On-Die ECC and timings aligned to correction capa-
bility.
[DIRECTIONAL] DDR6 SDRAM device shall also provide additional transparency (i.e., Uncorrectable Error infor-
mation) for the use of System RAS solution.
The device supports on-die ECC covering the array bits. The on-die ECC may be whatever type of ECC 
the device manufacturer determines is to best support its design objectives. However, the multi-bit error (MBE) diag-
nostic coverage (DC) shall be available to host.
7.9.9.1. Error Definitions
SBE = Single bit error: one single bit error per ECC code word; sometimes referred to as payload or mission data.
MBE = Multi-bit error: two or more single bit errors per ECC code word; sometimes referred to as payload or mission 
data.
7.9.9.2. ECC Diagnostic Coverage
The on-die ECC corrects one bit error (SBE) detected in the ECC code word. More than one bit error detected in the 
ECC code word is classifieds as an uncorrectable MBE.
The device’s on-die ECC has the ability to meet or exceed the following diagnostics:
100% SBE correction
•
On-die ECC corrects one bit error (SBE) detected in the ECC code word
≥
 to 95% MBE detection
•
On-die ECC classifies more than one bit error detected in the ECC code word as an uncorrectable MBE and the 
detected MBE should be registered
7.9.9.3. MBE Registration
The on-die ECC MBE detection should be registered in MR97 OP[3].
Table 191 — On-Die ECC Diagnostic Coverage
SBE Correction
MBE Detection
100%
≥99.5%

JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND AND OPERATION
PROPOSED
7.9.10. Bounded Fault
[DIRECTIONAL]
7.9.10.1. 2p6/2p4
A bounded fault shall not impact more than 32 bits in a 128-bit data fetch.
The data bits impacted by a bounded fault will be confined to at most 4 sub-packets of the data packet.
The impacted data sub-packets can be present anywhere in the data packet.
7.9.10.2. 2p3/2p2
A bounded fault shall not impact more than 24 bits in a 64-bit data fetch.
The data bits impacted by a bounded fault will be confined to at most 3 sub-packets of the data packet.
The impacted data sub-packets can be present anywhere in the data packet.

7.9.13. Fault Registers
To be balloted in Q4'26.
7.9.13.1. Fault Registers
Two fault registers, MR97 and MR98, register up to 15 functions when a fault(s) is detected by a covered function. 
Fault registers MR97 and MR98 are default LOW and remain LOW unless a covered function has detected a fault and 
sets the associated register bit HIGH. The affected fault registers’ bit(s) retain their fault status until either (1) the 
affected register is read from; (2) a RESET command is applied; or (3) power is removed.
The supported functions, when enabled via MR99 and MR100, control the alert output when a fault occurs. FMR3 and 
FMR4 may be written to when the device is in idle state and no data is driven. The alert output is driven active, asyn-
chronously, upon fault occurrence and remains active until either (1) the affected MR97 and/or MR98 is read from, 
with one exception: MR97 OP[0] clearingw is controlled by PRAC operation and is cleared as defined by PRAC alert 
back-off protocol (ABO); (2) a RESET command is applied; or (3) power is removed. The logical diagram below 
depicts the functionality.
Figure 193 — Fault Register Logical Diagram
Fault register MR97 OP[7] is derived by ORing MR98 OP[7:0], logical diagram below depicts this functionality. 
When the host interrogates MR97 and if MR97 OP[7] is LOW, then the host does not need to read MR98.

7.9.13.2. Fault Register Definitions
Fault registers MR97 and MR98 define which functions register fault occurrence. These functions are not required to 
be supported by the device; however, if a function is supported, it should use the defined register bit. MR97 and MR98 
are default LOW and remain LOW unless a covered function has a detected fault and sets the associated register bit 
HIGH. The affected fault registers' bit(s) retain their fault status until either (1) register is read from (Note: MR97 
OP[7] is not required to be reset by MRR command; MR97 OP[7] may be cleared by clearing MR98); (2) a RESET 
command applied, or (3) power is removed.

7.9.13.3. Fault Alert Control
The two registers, MR99 and MR100, control which functions with registered faults affect the alert output status. 
MR99 and MR100 are default LOW and remain LOW until written to; these two MR registers may be written to when 
the device is in idle state and no data is driven. These two registers retain set values until either (1) rewritten to different 
state (2) a RESET command is applied, or (3) power is removed.

7.9.13.5. Error Definitions

DBE: Double bit error: two single bit errors per ECC code word; sometimes referred to as payload or mission data.
MBE: Multi-bit error: two or more single bit errors per ECC code word; sometimes referred to as payload or mission
