7.9.14. Command Address Parity (CA Parity)
7.9.14.1. CA Parity Checking
CA parity checking detects bit errors in the command address bus. The CA Parity Checking mode register, 
MR01OP[4], enables or disables CA parity checking. The default is disenabled. The DRAM must be in idle state when 
CA Parity checking mode register is changed. When CA parity checking is disabled, the DRAM does not need to check 
CA parity bit(s) in each command. When CA parity checking is enabled, the CA parity shall be checked and ALERT 
shall be asserted if a CA parity error is detected.
The CA Parity Command Blocking mode register, MR01OP[5], selects blocking or nonblocking mode when a CA 
parity error is detected. The default is nonblocking.
When CA parity checking is enabled in nonblocking mode, an erroneous command will be executed, but the DRAM 
may execute an unknown command. There is no additional latency increase compared to when CA parity checking is 
disabled, but DRAM will be in unknown state when a CA parity error is detected, so it is highly recommended that the 
host reset the DRAM.
When CA parity checking is enabled in blocking mode, an erroneous command that fails the CA parity check shall not 
be executed. An additional delay (PL : Parity LatencytPAR_CA_Delay) is applied to all commands. The command is 
held for the time of the Parity Latency (PL) before it is executed inside the device. This means that issuing timing of 
the internal command is determined with PL. Since some commands on one or more DRAM devices will be blocked 
after the first CA parity error, while these commands may be executed on one or more other DRAM devices that did 
not have a CA parity error, the host should replay commands starting from the first command that might have had a 
CA parity error. The earliest command that could possibly have had a detected CA parity error can be determined by 
working backwards from the time the host detects the ALERT, the propagation delay of ALERT from the DRAM to 
the host, and tPAR_ALERT_ON. The exact command that had a CA parity error can be determined by reading the CA 
Parity Log C0 to C5 mode registers.
7.9.14.2. Parity Calculation
Most commands in the Command Truth Table have a PAR1 bit in the 2nd cycle in CA4. 4-cycle and 6-cycle commands 
in the Command Truth Table have a PAR2 bit in the 4th or 6th cycle, respectively, in CA4. The convention of parity is 
even parity, meaning that valid parity is defined as an even number of ones across the inputs used for parity computa-
tion combined with the parity signal. In other words, the parity bit is chosen so that the total number of 1’s in the trans-
mitted signal, including the parity bit is even. The parity check passes if the PAR1 is valid in 2-cycle commands and 
passes if both PAR1 and PAR2 are valid in 4- and 6-cycle commands.
To generate valid parity bits, the host shall calculate PAR1 and PAR2 as follows. PAR1 and PAR2 satisfy even parity. 
The host shall calculate PAR1 as the XOR of all of the bits except itself in CA0, CA1, CA2, CA3, CA4, and CA5 (if 
supported) in the 1st and 2nd cycles of the command, excluding itself. In 4-cycle commands, the host shall calculate 
PAR2 as the XOR of all of the bits except itself in CA0, CA1, CA2, CA3, CA4, and CA5 (if supported) in the 3rd and 
4th cycles of the command. In 6-cycle commands, the host shall calculate PAR2 as the XOR of all of the bits except 
itself in CA0, CA1, CA2, CA3, CA4, and CA5 (if supported) in the 3rd, 4th, 5th, and 6th cycles of the command. Only 
DES is allowed between valid commands to prevent DRAM from any malfunctioning.
7.9.14.3. CA Parity Error Detection and Actions
When CA parity checking is enabled, and the DRAM detects a CA parity error in any command as qualified by CS, 
then it must perform the following steps:

7.9.14.4. 4- and 6-Cycle Command Cancel
DDR6 DRAM commands ACT, RD, REFab, REFdb, REFqb, RFMab, RFMdb, RFMqb, RFMpb, MRR, MRW are 4- 
and 6-cycle commands without associated ODT control requirements. The DRAM will not execute these 4- and 
6-cycle commands if the CS is HIGH on the last two cycles of the command (command cancel).
For example, if the RCD or IMB detects a parity error on the 1st and/or 2nd cycles of a 2-, 4-, or 6-cycle command 
using the PAR1 bit, the CS will remain LOW for the duration of the 2, 4, or 6 cycles, respectively.
For example, if the RCD or IMB detects a parity error on the 3rd and/or 4th cycles of a 4-cycle command using the 
PAR2 bit, the CS will remain HIGH for the 1st, 2nd, 3rd, and 4th cycles of the command. If the command is a Write, 
then it will be converted to non-target termination command in the DRAM. If the command is either RD, REFab, 
REFdb, REFqb, RFMab, RFMdb, RFMqb, RFMpb, or MRR, then the erroneous command will be canceled in the 
DRAM.
For example, if the RCD or IMB detects a parity error on the 3rd, 4th, 5th, and/or 6th cycles of a 6-cycle command using 
the PAR2 bit, the CS will remain HIGH for the 1st and 2nd cycles, LOW for the 3rd, and 4th cycles, and HIGH for 5th, 
and 6th cycles of the command. If the command is either ACT or MRW, then the erroneous command will be canceled 
in the DRAM.
Command cancel is not intended by the host rather it is a result of a CA parity error detected by the RCD or IMB. So 
the relationship between the canceled command and the next valid command shall not be illegal.

If CA parity checking is enabled, the check also applies to cancelled commands. For example, if the RCD or IMB 
detects a parity error using the PAR2 bit, the RCD or IMB may recalculate the PAR2 bit, so the DRAM should still 
expect the PAR2 check to pass.

must be at least tCMD_cancel after the last cycle of the cancelled command.

7.10. Command Ignore
The host can dynamically choose which Activate, Read, Write, and/or Precharge commands to ignore or execute for 
selected DRAM die(s) by configuring mode register bits and by setting an Ignore bit in C0:CA4 in the command to 
low (active low) or high (active high). Ignored commands (including Write-NTODT) shall be treated like NOP 
commands. Write-NTODT commands will also be treated like NOP commands.CA parity checks, when enabled, will 
also apply to ignored commands.When CA parity checking is enabled, responses to CA parity errors will be the same 
whether a command is ignored or not. The Command Ignore function is only supported by 2p3 DRAM devices.
All command timing constraints need to be satisfied as though the command is being executed, even if the command 
is ignored. If the current or next command is ignored, assume different bank groups when applying command timing 
constraints.
The Command Ignore Enable/Disable bit, MR0EOP[0], may be set to 0b (active low) or 1b (active high) to dynami-
cally ignore Activate, Read, Write, or Precharge commands for a DRAM die when the Ignore bit in the Activate, Read, 
Write, or Precharge command is low (active low) or high (active high) as defined in the Command Ignore Polarity bit, 
MR0EOP[1].
When the Command Ignore function is disabled (i.e., MR0EOP[0] = 0b), then the Ignore bit in C0:CA4 becomes don’t 
care. When the Command Ignore function is enabled (i.e., MR0E OP[0] = 1b) and the Command Ignore Polarity is 
‘low’ (i.e., MR0EOP[1] = 0b) then the DRAM die shall dynamically ignore Activate, Read, Write, or Precharge 
commands when the Ignore bit in C0:CA4 is low. When the Command Ignore function is enabled (i.e., MR0EOP[0] 
= 1b) and the Command Ignore Polarity is ‘high’ (i.e., MR0EOP[1] = 1b) then the DRAM die shall dynamically ignore 
Activate, Read, Write, or Precharge commands when the Ignore bit in C0:CA4 is high.
A Mode Register Write command with Per-DRAM Addressability (PDA) may be used to set the Command Ignore 
Enable/Disable bit or the Command Ignore Polarity bit.

DQS2DQ AND CK2DQ AC PARAMETERS
JEDEC PROPOSEDCOMMAND CONSTRAINTS AND AC TIMING
PROPOSED
8.3. DQS2DQ and CK2DQ AC Parameters
tDQS2DQi defines the timing offset from DQS input to DQ input for write operation and it reflects an internal delay 
of DRAM from a DQS receiver to a DQ latch. tCK2DQo defines the timing offset from CK input to DQ output for 
read operation and it reflects an internal delay of DRAM from a CK receiver to an DQ output driver. These parameters 
apply to write/read/ODT command timing equations for command scheduling.

8.5. Training-Related Timing Constraints
Unballoted.
The device can enter a read or write training state by issuing commands such as READ DQ CALIBRATION. For 
training commands that read or write the device, READ latency (RL) and WRITE latency (WL) are counted from the 
rising CK edge of each training command with the same timing relationship as any typical READ or WRITE 
command.

11. AC and DC Input/Output Operating Conditions
11.1. Bit Error Rate
Unballoted.
This section provides an overview of the bit error rate (BER) and the desired statistical level of confidence.
Figure 199 — General Equation
NOTE: Where: 
n = number of bits in a trial 
SLC = statistical level of confidence 
- BER = bit error rate
- k = intermediate number of specific errors found in trial
- N = number of errors recorded during trial
If no errors are assumed in a given test period, the second term drops out and the equation becomes: 
JEDEC recommends testing to 99.5% confidence levels; however, one may choose a number that is viable for their 
own manufacturing levels. To determine how many bits of data should be sent (again, assuming zero errors, or N = 0), 
using BER = 1E-9and confidence level SLC = 99.5%, the result is n = (1/BER)(-ln(1 - 0.995) = 5.298 x 109.

UNIT INTERVAL AND JITTER DEFINITION
JEDEC PROPOSEDAC AND DC INPUT/OUTPUT OPERATING CONDITIONS
PROPOSED
11.2. Unit Interval and Jitter Definition
11.2.1. Introduction
This section describes the Unit Interval (UI) and UI Jitter definitions associated with the Jitter parameters specified in 
Tx DQS2DQ Stressed Edge Jitter and Input Clock Jitter specifications.
11.2.2. Unit Interval (UI)
The definition of Unit Interval (UI) is a minimum time interval between condition changes of a signal. DDR-based 
signals are referenced to the differential crossing point of CK_t and CK_c. 2UI=1tCK for DDR-based signals (for 
example, DQ, DQS).
The UI definitions shown in Figure 1and Figure 2 are for DDR-based signals. The times at which the differential 
crossing points of the clock occur are defined at t1, t2, …, tn-1, tn. The UI at index "n" is defined as an arbitrary time 
in steady state, where n=0 is chosen as the starting crossing point.
Figure 200 — UI Definition in Terms of Adjacent Edge Timings
Figure 201 — UI Definition Using Clock Waveforms
UIn = tn - tn-1
UI
T0
T1
T2
Tn-1
Tn
...
2UI


=== PDF PAGE 469 ===
Page of 449
UNIT INTERVAL AND JITTER DEFINITION
JEDEC PROPOSEDAC AND DC INPUT/OUTPUT OPERATING CONDITIONS
PROPOSED
11.2.3. UI Jitter Definition
If a number of UI edges are computed or measured at times t1, t2, …, tn-1, tn,..., tK, where K is the maximum number 
of samples, then the UI jitter at any instance "n" is defined in Figure3, where T = the ideal UI size.
Figure 202 — UI Jitter for "nth" UI Definition (in terms of ideal UI)
In a large sample with random Gaussian-like jitter (therefore very close to symmetric distribution), the average of all 
UI sizes usually turns out to be very close to the ideal UI size.
The equation described in Figure 3 assumes starting from an instant steady state, where n=0 is chosen as the starting 
point.1 UI = one bit, which means 2 UI = one full cycle or time period of the forwarded strobe. Example: For 6.4 GT/s 
signaling, the forwarded strobe frequency is 3.2 GHz, or 1 UI = 156.25 ps.
Deterministic jitter is analyzed in terms of the peak-to-peak value and in terms of specific frequency components 
present in the jitter, isolating the causes for each frequency. Random jitter is unbounded and analyzed in terms of statis-
tical distribution to convert to a bit error rate (BER) for the link.
11.2.4. UI-UI Jitter Definition
UI-UI (read as "UI to UI") jitter is defined to be the jitter between two consecutive UI as shown in Figure 4.
Figure 203 — UI-UI Jitter Definitions
UI(jit) n = (tn - tn - 1) - T, n = 1, 2, 3, ..., K
UIn = UIn - UIn-1, n = 2, 3, ..., K

JEDEC PROPOSEDAC AND DC INPUT MEASUREMENT LEVELS

12. AC and DC Input Measurement Levels
12.1. CA Rx Voltage and Timings
To be balloted in Q4'26.
The command, address and control (including chip select) input receiver compliance mask for voltage and timing is 
shown in the figure below. All CA signals apply the same compliance mask and the CS signal applies its own compli-
ance mask independently. All signals applied to the compliance mask operate in single data rate mode.
The CA input receiver mask (Rx Mask) for voltage and timing is applied across all CA[13:0] pins and the CS_n pin. 
For the remainder of this section, the reference to "CA" should be applied to the device CA[13:0] pins as well as the 
CS_n pin. The Rx Mask defines the area the input signal must not encroach for the device input receiver to successfully 
capture a valid input signal; it is not the valid data-eye.
Figure 208 — CA/CS_n Rx Mask
Figure 209 — Across-Pin VREFCA Voltage Variation
Vcent_CA(pin mid) is defined as the midpoint between the largest Vcent_CA voltage level and the smallest Vcent_CA 
voltage level across all CA and CS_n pins for a given device component.
Each CA Vcent level is defined by the center (widest opening) of the cumulative data input eye as depicted in the 
previous figure (Across-Pin VREFCA Voltage Variation). This clarifies any device component level variation must be

DIFFERENTIAL INPUT CLOCK VOLTAGE SENSITIVITY
JEDEC PROPOSEDAC AND DC INPUT MEASUREMENT LEVELS
PROPOSED
12.3. Differential Input Clock Voltage Sensitivity
12.3.1. Differential Input Clock Voltage Sensitivity
The differential input clock voltage sensitivity test provides the methodology for testing the receiver’s sensitivity to 
clock by varying input voltage in the absence of Inter-Symbol Interference (ISI), jitter (Rj, Dj, DCD) and crosstalk 
noise. This specifies the Rx voltage sensitivity requirement. The system input swing to the DRAM must be larger than 
the DRAM Rx at the specified BER
Figure 214 — Example of DDR6 Memory Interconnect

INPUT CLOCK JITTER SPECIFICATION
JEDEC PROPOSEDAC AND DC INPUT MEASUREMENT LEVELS
PROPOSED
12.4. Input Clock Jitter Specification
To be balloted in Q4'26.
The clock is being driven to the device either by the RCD for L/RDIMM modules, or by the host for U/SODIMM 
modules.
Figure 216 — HOST Driving Clock Signals to the device
12.4.1. Specifications for Input Clock Jitter
The Random Jitter (Rj) specified is a random jitter meeting a Gaussian distribution. The Deterministic Jitter (Dj) spec-
ified is bounded. Input clock violating the min/max jitter values may result in malfunction of the device.
NOTE: For the following tables: BUJ = Bounded Uncorrelated Jitter; DCD = Duty Cycle Distortion; Dj = Determin-
istic Jitter; Rj = Random Jitter; Tj = Total jitter; pp = Peak-to-Peak
Table 234 — Input Clock Jitter Specifications for 3200 to 4800
NOTE: f0 = Data Rate/2, example: if data rate is 3200MT/s, then f0 = 1600
NOTE: Rise and fall time slopes (V/ns) are measured between +100 mV and -100 mV of the differential output of reference clock
NOTE: On-die noise is similar to that occurring with all the transmitter and receiver lanes toggling need to be stimulated. When there 
is no socket in transmitter measurement setup, in many cases, the contribution of the cross-talk is not significant or can be estimated 
within tolerable error even with all the transmitter lanes sending patterns. When a socket is present, such as DUT being a device 
component, the contribution of the cross-talk could be significant. To minimize the impact of crosstalk on the measurement results, 
a small group of selected lanes in the vicinity of the lane under test may be turned off (sending DC), while the remaining Tx lanes 
send patterns to the corresponding Rx receivers so as to excite realistic on-die noise profile from device switching. Note that there 
may be cases when one of Dj and Rj specifications is met and another violated in which case the signaling analysis should be run 
to determine link feasibility.
NOTE: Duty Cycle Error defined as absolute difference between average value of all UI with that of average of odd UI, which in 
magnitude would equal absolute difference between average of all UI and average of all even UI.
NOTE: Rj RMS value of 1-UI jitter without BUJ, but on-die system-like noise present. This extraction is to be done after software 
correction of DCD
NOTE: Dj pp value of 1-UI jitter (after software assisted DCC). Without BUJ, but on-die system like noise present. Dj indicates 
Djdd of dual-Dirac fitting, after software correction of DCD
NOTE: Rj RMS value of N-UI jitter without BUJ, but on-die system like noise present. Evaluated for 1 < N < 4. This extraction is 
to be done after software correction of DCD
NOTE: Dj pp value of N-UI jitter without BUJ, but on-die system like noise present. Evaluated for 1 < N < 4. Dj indicates Djdd of 
dual-Dirac fitting, after software correction of DCD
NOTE: Rj RMS value of N-UI jitter without BUJ, but on-die system like noise present. Evaluated for 3 < N < 31. This extraction is 
to be done after software correction of DCD
NOTE: Dj pp value of N-UI jitter without BUJ, but on-die system like noise present. Evaluated for 3 < N < 31. Dj indicates Djdd of 
dual-Dirac fitting, after software correction of DCD
NOTE: The validation methodology for these parameters will be covered in future ballots

RX DQS JITTER SENSITIVITY
JEDEC PROPOSEDAC AND DC INPUT MEASUREMENT LEVELS
PROPOSED
12.7. Rx DQS Jitter Sensitivity
To be balloted in Q4'26.
The receiver strobe jitter sensitivity test provides the methodology for testing the receiver’s strobe sensitivity to an 
applied duty cycle distortion (DCD) and/or random jitter (Rj) at the forwarded strobe input without adding jitter, noise 
and ISI to the data. The receiver must pass the appropriate BER rate when no crosstalk or ISI is applied and it must 
pass through the combination of applied DCD and Rj.
Figure 220 — Rx Forwarded Strobes for Jitter Sensitivity Testing
12.7.1. Specifications
The following tables provide Rx DQS jitter sensitivity specifications for the receivers when operating at various 
transfer rates. These parameters are tested on the CTC2 card without Rx equalization set. Additive DFE Gain Bias can 
be set.
Also note:
•
BER = bit error rate; DCD = duty cycle distortion; Rj = random jitter
•
Validation methodology will be defined in future ballots. 2UI is defined ast 1tCK for the parameters.
•
All measurements at BER=1E-9.
•
The test should be done after the DQS and DQ voltage sensitivity tests are complete and passing.
•
Voltage swings and slew rates for strobe and DQ signals can be set as long as they meet specifications. The DQS 
and DQ input voltage swing and/or slew rate can be adjusted, without exceeding the specifications, for this test.
Table 243 — Rx DQS Jitter Sensitivity — 3200-4800
NOTE: Each of ΔtRx_DQ_tMargin_DQS_DCD, ΔtRx_DQ_tMargin_DQS_Rj, and ΔtRx_DQ_tMargin_DQS_DCD_Rj can be 
relaxed by up to 5% if tRx_DQ_tMargin exceeds the spec by 5% or more.
NOTE: DQ timing width: timing width for any data lane using repetitive patterns (check note 3 for the pattern) measured at 
BER=1E-9.
NOTE: Magnitude of degradation of timing width for any data lane using repetitive no ISI patterns with DCD injection in forwarded 
strobe DQS compared to tRx_DQ_tMargin, measured at BER=1E-9. The magnitude of DCD is specified in Test Conditions for Rx 
DQS Jitter Sensitivity Testing. Test using clock-like pattern of repeating three 1s and three 0s.
NOTE: Magnitude of degradation of timing width for any data lane using repetitive no ISI patterns with only Rj injection in 
forwarded strobe DQS measured at BER=1E-9, compared to tRx_DQ_tMargin. The magnitude of Rj is specified in Test Conditions 
for Rx DQS Jitter Sensitivity Testing.
NOTE: Magnitude of degradation of timing width for any data lane using repetitive no ISI patterns with DCD and Rj injection in 
forwarded strobe DQS measured at BER=1E-9, compared to tRx_DQ_tMargin. The magnitudes of DCD and Rj are specified in Test 
Conditions for Rx DQS Jitter Sensitivity Testing.

12.7.2. Test Conditions
The following table lists the amount of duty cycle distortion (DCD) and/or random jitter (Rj) that must be applied to 
the forwarded strobe when measuring the Rx strobe jitter sensitivity parameters specified in the previous tables.
Table 247 — Test Conditions for Rx DQS Jitter Sensitivity Testing — 3200-8800
NOTE: While imposing these specifications, the strobe lane is stressed, but the data input is kept large amplitude and no jitter or ISI 
injection. The specified voltages are at the Rx input pin. The DQS and DQ input voltage swing and/or slew rate can be adjusted, 
without exceeding the specifications, for this test.
NOTE: The jitter response of the forwarded strobe channel depends on the input voltage, primarily due to bandwidth limitations of 
the clock receiver. For this revision, no separate specification of jitter as a function of input amplitude is specified; instead, the 
response characterization is done at the specified clock amplitude only. The specified voltages are at the Rx input pin.
NOTE: Various DCD values should be tested, complying within the maximum limits.
NOTE: Various Rj values should be tested, complying within the maximum limits.
NOTE: Various combinations of DCD and Rj values should be tested, complying within the maximum limits. The maximum timing 
margin degradation as a result of the injected jitter is specified in a separate table.
NOTE: Although the device has bursty traffic, current available BERTs that can be used for this test do not support burst traffic 
patterns. A continuous strobe and continuous DQ are used for this parameter. The clock-like pattern repeating three 1s and three 0s 
is used for this test.
NOTE: Duty cycle distortion (in UI DCD) as applied to the input forwarded DQS from BERT (UI).
NOTE: RMS value of Rj (specified as edge jitter) applied to the input forwarded DQS from BERT (values of the edge jitter RMS 
values specified as % of UI).
NOTE: Duty cycle distortion (specified as UI DCD) and rms values of Rj (specified as edge jitter) applied to the input forwarded 
DQS from BERT (values of the edge jitter RMS values specified as % of UI).
NOTE: Voltage swing and slew rates can be set for strobe and DQ signals as long as they meet the specification. The DQS and DQ 
input voltage swing and/or slew rate can be adjusted, without exceeding the specifications, for this test.

RX STRESSED EYE
JEDEC PROPOSEDAC AND DC INPUT MEASUREMENT LEVELS
PROPOSED
12.9. Rx Stressed Eye
To be balloted in Q4'26.
The stressed eye tests provide a methodology for creating the appropriate stress for the device’s receiver with the 
combination of ISI (both loss and reflective), jitter (Rj, Dj, DCD), and crosstalk noise. The receiver must pass the 
appropriate BER rate when the equivalent stressed eye is applied through the combination of ISI, jitter and crosstalk.
Figure 223 — Example: RX Stressed Test Setup in the Presence of ISI, Jitter and 
Crosstalk
Figure 224 — Example of Rx Stressed Eye Height and Eye Width
12.9.1. Parameters
In the following tables, BER = bit error rate, DCD = duty cycle distortion, Rj = random jitter, Sj = sinusoidal jitter, p-p 
= peak to peak.
Table 252 — Test Conditions for Rx Stressed Eye Tests for DDR5 3200-4800
NOTE: Must meet minimum BER of IE-16 or better requirements with the stressed eye at the slicer of the receiver (after equalization 
is applied in the summer). The eye shape is verified by measuring to BER 1E-9 and extrapolating to BER 1E-16.
NOTE: These parameters are applied on the defined golden reference channel with parameters TBD.
NOTE: DFE tap 1-4 bias settings that give the best eye margin are used and referr to the values in the DFE Tap Coefficients 
MIN/MAX Ranges table. DFE tap range limits apply: sum of absolute values of Tap-2, Tap-3, and Tap-4 should be less than 60mV 
(|Tap-2| + |Tap-3| + |Tap-4| < 60mV) after the tap multiplier is applied.
NOTE: Evaluated with no DC supply voltage drift.
NOTE: Evaluated with no temperature drift.
NOTE: Supply voltage noise limited according to DC bandwidth specifications (see DC Operating Conditions).
NOTE: The stressed eye is assumed to have a diamond shape.

ALERT OUTPUT
JEDEC PROPOSEDAC AND DC OUTPUT LEVELS AND TIMING
PROPOSED
13.2. Alert Output
13.2.1. Alert Signal
Alert is an output pin which signals fault status defined.
13.2.2. Alert Output
The Alert output shall be inactive when the DRAM has not detected a fault condition. The Alert output will be Hi-Z 
when inactive and the Host shall provide termination to VSS. The Alert output shall be active when the DRAM has 
detected a covered fault condition. The Alert output will drive high, to VDDQ, when active and the Host shall provide 
termination to VSS.
The DRAM pull-up driver strength is fixed at RZQ/3.5. The Host shall set the pull-down impedance(s), i.e., External 
Pulldown, to derive the desired VOH Level.
13.2.3. Alert Pull-Up Driver Characteristics
Alert output pin driver characteristics are defined by the following table.
Table 257 — Alert Output Driver Pull-up Impedance – RonPU_Alert
RonPU_Alert

NOTE 1: All values are without ZQ calibration. {Fixed ZQcode applied to avoid hazard on Alert pin.} NOTE 2: 
Vout assumes typical VDDQ and VDD; external pulldown with matching impedance.
NOTE 3: VOH level is dependent on Host on pull-down resistance, i.e., External Pulldown. The Signal integrity 
of the channel between the DRAM and the Host should be confirmed during simulation and design
effort.

13.2.5. VOH calculations
Alert VOH at Host can be determined by:
•
VOH = (VDDQ * External Pulldown) / (RonPU_Alert + External Pulldown)

TX DQS_TO_DQ STRESSED EDGE JITTER
JEDEC PROPOSEDAC AND DC OUTPUT LEVELS AND TIMING
PROPOSED
13.4. Tx DQS_to_DQ Stressed Edge Jitter
13.4.1. Introduction
Tx DQS-to-DQ Stress Edge provides the methodology for testing the DRAM transmitter’s timing characteristics under 
various DQS to DQ skew conditions. This test measures how each DQ edge aligns to its associated strobe when 
stressed and reports Random Jitter (Rj), Deterministic Jitter (Dj), and Total Jitter (Tj). The applied DQS-to-DQ skew 
conditions are determined by the Host’s responsibility and capability to adjust advanced DQS edges through the Read 
DQS Offset Timing (mode TBD).
The total Tx DQS_to_DQ Stressed Edge jitter, Tj, is defined as Dj+Q*Rj, where Q isTBD 14.07 calculated at BER of 
1E-12. The Deterministic Jitter (Dj) as specified is bounded. The Random Jitter (Rj) as specified meets a Gaussian 
distribution.
13.4.2. TX DQS_to_DQ Stressed Edge Jitter Parameters

JEDEC PROPOSEDAC AND DC OUTPUT LEVELS AND TIMING

13.5. Tx DQ Stressed Eye
To be balloted in Q4'26.
Tx DQ stressed eye height and eye width must meet minimum specification values at BER=E-9 and confidence level 
99.5%. Tx DQ Stressed Eye shows the DQS to DQ skew for both eye width and height. To support different host 
receiver (Rx) designs, it is the responsibility of the host to ensure the advanced DQS edges are adjusted accordingly 
via the Read DQS Offset Timing mode register settings (MR40:OP[2:0]).

TX DQ STRESSED EYE
JEDEC PROPOSEDAC AND DC OUTPUT LEVELS AND TIMING

13.5.1. Tx DQ Stressed Eye Parameters
The following notes apply to all tables:
•
EH = Eye Height; EW = Eye Width; BER = Bit Error Rate, SES = Stressed Eye Skew
•
Minimum BER E-9 and Confidence Level of 99.5% per pin.
•
Refer to the minimum Bit Error Rate (BER) requirements for DDR5.
•
The validation methodology for these parameters will be covered in the future.
•
Mismatch is defined as DQS to DQ mismatch in UI increments.
•
The DQ pins' duty cycle must be adjusted as close to 50% as possible using the Duty Cycle Adjuster feature 
prior to running the Tx DQS jtter test.
•
The mode registers for the Duty Cycle Adjuster are MR43 and MR44. The mode registers for the per-pin DCA 
of DQS are MR103-MR110. The mode registers for the per-pin DCA of DQLx are MR(133+8x) and 
MR(134+8x), where 0 ≤x ≤7. The mode registers for the per-pin DCA of DQUy are MR(197+8y) and 
MR(198+8y), where 0 ≤y ≤7.
•
Spread spectrum clocking (SSC) must be disabled while running these tests.
•
These parameters are tested using the continuous PRBS8 LFSR training pattern, which is sent out on all DQ 
lanes off the device without the need for sending out continuous MRR commands. The MR25 OP[3] is set to 1 
to enable this feature.
•
These parameters are tested on the CTC2 card only.
•
Matched DQS to DQ would require the DQs to be adjusted by 0.5UI to place it in the center of the DQ eye. 1UI 
mismatch would require the DQS to be adjusted 1.5UI. Generally, for XUI mismatch the DQ must be adjusted 
XUI + 0.5UI to be placed in the center of the eye.
Table 269 — Tx DQ Stressed Eye Parameters for DDR5 5200–6400
NOTE: The number of UI’s accumulated will depend on the speed of the link. For higher speeds, higher UI accumulation may be 
specified. For lower speeds, N=4,5 UI may not be applicable.

REFERENCE LOAD FOR AC TIMING AND OUTPUT SLEW RATE
JEDEC PROPOSEDELECTRICAL CHARACTERISTICS AND AC TIMING
PROPOSED
16. Electrical Characteristics and AC Timing
Unballoted.
16.1. Reference Load for AC Timing and Output Slew Rate
The figure below represents the effective reference load of 50 ohms used in defining the relevant AC timing parameters 
of the device as well as output slew rate measurements.
RON nominal of DQ, DQS_t, and DQS_c drivers uses 34 ohms to specify the relevant AC timing parameter values of 
the device. 
•
The maximum DC high level of output signal = 1.0 × VDDQ.
•
The minimum DC low level of output signal = {34/(34 + 50)} × VDDQ = 0.4 × VDDQ
•
The nominal reference level of an output signal can be approximated by the following: 
–
The center of maximum DC high and minimum DC low 
•
•
= {(1 + 0.4)/2} × VDDQ = 0.7 × VDDQ
The actual reference level of output signal might vary with driver RON and reference load tolerances. Thus, the actual 
reference level or midpoint of an output signal is at the widest part of the output signal’s eye. Prior to measuring AC 
parameters, the reference level of the verification tool should be set to an appropriate level.
It is not intended as a precise representation of any particular system environment or a depiction of the actual load 
presented by a production tester. System designers should use IBIS or other simulation tools to correlate the timing 
reference load to a system environment. Manufacturers correlate to their production test conditions, generally one or 
more coaxial transmission lines terminated at the tester electronics.
Figure 232 — Reference Load for AC Timing and Output Slew Rate
